--- Page 19 ---
# PREFACE.

A singular misfortune has governed the fate of nearly all of Leonardo da Vinci's most famous works. Two of the three most important were never completed, as obstacles arose during his lifetime that forced him to leave them unfinished: namely, the Sforza Monument and the wall painting of the Battle of Anghiari. Meanwhile, the third—the painting of the Last Supper in Milan—has suffered permanent damage from decay and the repeated restorations to which it was recklessly subjected during the 17th and 18th centuries. Nevertheless, no other painting of the Renaissance has become so well known and popular through copies of every kind.

Vasari says, and rightly so, in his Life of Leonardo, "that he labored much more by his word than in fact or by deed," and the biographer evidently had in mind the numerous works in Manuscript which have been preserved to this day. To us, now, it seems almost inexplicable that these valuable and interesting original texts should have remained unpublished for so long, and indeed forgotten. It is certain that during the 16th and 17th centuries their exceptional value was highly appreciated. This is proved not merely by the prices they commanded, but also by the exceptional interest that has been attached to the change of ownership of even a few pages of Manuscript.

That their contents remained a mystery despite this eagerness to possess the Manuscripts can only be explained by the many great difficulties involved in the task of deciphering them. The handwriting is

--- Page 20 ---
so peculiar that it requires considerable practice to read even a few detached phrases, much more to solve with any certainty the numerous difficulties of alternative readings, and to master the sense as a connected whole. Vasari observes with reference to Leonardo’s writing: “he wrote backwards, in crude characters, and with the left hand, so that anyone who is not practiced in reading them cannot understand them.” The aid of a mirror in reading reversed handwriting appears to me useful only for a first experimental reading. Speaking from my own experience, the persistent use of it is too fatiguing and inconvenient to be practically advisable, considering the enormous mass of Manuscripts to be deciphered. And as, after all, Leonardo’s handwriting runs backwards just as all Oriental characters run backwards—that is to say, from right to left—the difficulty of reading directly from the writing is not insuperable. This obvious peculiarity in the writing is not, however, by any means the only obstacle in the way of mastering the text. Leonardo made use of a spelling system peculiar to himself; he had a fashion of merging several short words into one long one, or, again, he would quite arbitrarily divide a long word into two separate halves; added to this, there is no punctuation whatever to regulate the division and structure of the sentences, nor are there any accents—and the reader may imagine that such difficulties were almost sufficient to make the task seem a desperate one to a beginner. It is therefore not surprising that the good intentions of some of Leonardo’s most reverent admirers should have failed.

Leonardo’s literary labors in various departments of both Art and Science were essentially those of an inquirer; hence, the analytical method is what he employs in arguing out his investigations and dissertations. The vast structure of his scientific theories is consequently built up of numerous separate researches, and it is much to be lamented that he should never have compiled and arranged them. His love for detailed research—as it seems to me—was the reason that in almost all the Manuscripts, the different paragraphs appear to us to be in utter confusion; on one and the same page, observations on the most dissimilar subjects follow each other without any connection. A page, for instance, will begin with some principles of astronomy, or the motion of the earth; then come the laws of sound, and finally some precepts regarding color. Another page will begin with his investigations on the structure of the intestines and end with philosophical remarks regarding the relationship of poetry to painting; and so forth.

--- Page 21 ---
Leonardo himself lamented this confusion, and for that reason I do not think that the publication of the texts in the order in which they occur in the originals would at all fulfill his intentions. No reader could find his way through such a labyrinth; Leonardo himself could not have done it.

Added to this, more than half of the five thousand manuscript pages which now remain to us are written on loose leaves, and are at present arranged in a manner which has no justification beyond the whim of the collector who first brought them together to make volumes of varying length. Indeed, even in the volumes whose pages were numbered by Leonardo himself, their order—so far as the connection of the texts was concerned—was obviously a matter of indifference to him. The only point he seems to have kept in view when first writing down his notes was that each observation should be complete by the end of the page on which it was begun. The exceptions to this rule are extremely few, and it is certainly noteworthy that we find in such cases, in bound volumes with his numbered pages, the written notes: "turn over," "this is the continuation of the previous page," and the like. Is this not sufficient to prove that it was only in quite exceptional cases that the writer intended the consecutive pages to remain connected when he should, at last, carry out the often-planned arrangement of his writings?

What this final arrangement was to be, Leonardo has in most cases indicated with considerable completeness. In other cases this authoritative clue is missing, but the difficulties arising from this are not insuperable; for, as the subject of the separate paragraphs is always distinct and well-defined in itself, it is quite possible to construct a well-planned whole out of the scattered materials of his scientific system, and I may venture to state that I have devoted especial care and thought to the proper execution of this responsible task.

The beginning of Leonardo's literary labors dates from about his thirty-seventh year, and he seems to have carried them on without any serious interruption until his death. Thus the manuscripts that remain represent a period of about thirty years. Within this span of time his handwriting altered so little that it is impossible to judge from it the date of any particular text. The exact dates, indeed, can only be assigned to certain notebooks in which the year is incidentally indicated, and in which the order

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor's explanation of how he organized Leonardo da Vinci's scattered notes into a logical, chronological system for this edition, contrasting his method with earlier, incomplete versions of Leonardo's "Treatise on Painting."</meta>

<page-num>XVI</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

*The sequence of the leaves has not been changed since Leonardo used them. The help these provide for a chronological arrangement of the manuscripts is generally self-evident. Using this evidence, I have assigned to the original manuscripts—now scattered throughout England, Italy, and France—the order in which they were produced. In many matters of detail, it is highly important to be able to verify the time and place at which certain observations were made and recorded. For this purpose, the Bibliography of the Manuscripts provided at the end of Volume II may be regarded as a nearly complete index of all of Leonardo's surviving literary works. The consecutive numbers (from 1 to 1566) at the top of each passage in this work indicate their logical order based on subject matter. Meanwhile, the letters and numbers to the left of each paragraph refer to the original manuscript and page number where that specific passage can be found. Thus, by referring to the List of Manuscripts at the beginning of Volume I and to the Bibliography at the end of Volume II, the reader can easily determine in every instance not only the period to which the passage belongs but also exactly where it was located in the original document. In this way, by following the sequence of numbers in the bibliographical index, the reader may reconstruct the original order of the manuscripts and piece together the various texts found on the original sheets—at least as much of them as fell within the scope of this work. It should be noted here, however, that Leonardo's manuscripts contain, besides the passages printed here, a great number of notes and essays on mechanics, physics, and other subjects, many of which could only be properly handled by specialists. I have provided as complete a review of these writings as seemed necessary in the bibliographical notes.*

*In 1651, Raphael Trichet Dufresne of Paris published a selection from Leonardo's writings on painting. This treatise became so popular that it has since been reprinted about twenty-two times <note>original: "two-and-twenty"</note> and in six different languages. However, none of these editions were derived from the original texts, which were thought to have been lost. Instead, they were based on early copies in which Leonardo's text had been more or less altered <note>original: "mutilated"</note> and which were all fragmentary. The oldest and, on the whole, the best copy of Leonardo's essays and principles <note>original: "precepts"</note> on painting is in the Vatican Library. This has been printed twice: first by Manzi in 1817, and secondly by Ludwig in 1882. Still, this ancient copy and its published editions contain much for which it would be unwise <note>original: "rash"</note> to hold Leonardo responsible, and some portions—such as the very—*

<summary>The editor explains his system for chronologically and logically indexing Leonardo’s scattered notes and contrasts his comprehensive work with earlier, flawed editions of Leonardo's writings on painting.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, manuscript organization, bibliography, Raphael Trichet Dufresne, Treatise on Painting, Vatican Library, Manzi, Ludwig, history of science</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
Important rules for the proportions of the human figure are entirely missing; on the other hand, they contain passages which, if they are genuine, cannot now be verified from any original manuscript still in existence. These copies, at any rate, neither give us the original order of the texts as written by Leonardo, nor do they provide any substitute by connecting them according to a logical scheme; indeed, in their chaotic confusion, they are anything but satisfactory reading. The fault, no doubt, lies with the compiler of the Vatican copy, which appears to be the source from which all the published and widely known texts were derived; for, instead of arranging the passages himself, he was satisfied with recording a suggestion for a final arrangement of them into eight distinct parts, without attempting to carry out his plan. Under the mistaken idea that this distribution plan might belong not to the compiler, but to Leonardo himself, various editors up to the present day have very unwisely continued to adopt this order—or rather, disorder.

I, like other researchers, had given up the original manuscript of the *Trattato della Pittura* as lost, until, in the beginning of 1880, I was enabled by the generosity of Lord Ashburnham to inspect his manuscripts, and was fortunate enough to discover among them the original text of the best-known portion of the *Trattato* in his magnificent library at Ashburnham Place. Although this discovery was only of a fragment—though a considerable fragment—it prompted me to search further and provided the key to the mystery that had so long surrounded the first origin of all the known copies of the *Trattato*. The extensive research I was subsequently able to conduct, the results of which are combined in this work, was only made possible by the unrestricted permission granted to me to investigate all the manuscripts by Leonardo dispersed throughout Europe, and to reproduce the highly important original sketches they contain using the "photogravure" process. Her Majesty the Queen graciously granted me special permission to copy the manuscripts at the Royal Library at Windsor for publication. The Commission Centrale Administrative de l’Institut de France, Paris, in response to an application from Sir Frederic Leighton, P. R. A., a corresponding member of the Institut, most liberally gave me free permission to work for several months in their private collection to decipher the manuscripts preserved there. The same favor that Lord Ashburnham had already granted me was extended to me by the Earl of Leicester, the Marchese Trivulzi, and the curators of the Ambrosian Library at Milan, by the Conte Manzoni at Rome, and by

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface, specifically acknowledging the various institutions and individuals who assisted in the research and production of this collection of Leonardo da Vinci's writings.</meta>

<page-num>XVIII</page-num>

-># PREFACE.<-

*...other private owners of Leonardo's manuscripts; as well as the directors of the Louvre in Paris, the Academy in Venice, the Uffizi in Florence, the Royal Library in Turin, the British Museum, and the South Kensington Museum <note>Now the Victoria and Albert Museum (V&A).</note>. I am also greatly indebted to the librarians of these various collections for much assistance in my labors, and more particularly to Monsieur Louis Lalanne of the Institute of France; Abbot Ceriani of the Ambrosian Library; Mr. Maude Thompson, Keeper of Manuscripts at the British Museum; Mr. Holmes, the Queen’s Librarian at Windsor; the Reverend Vere Bayne, Librarian of Christ Church College at Oxford; and the Reverend A. Napier, Librarian to the Earl of Leicester at Holkham Hall.*

*In correcting the Italian text for the press, I have had the advantage of valuable advice from Knight Commander <note>original: "Commendatore"</note> Giovanni Morelli, a Senator of the Kingdom <note>original: "Senatore del Regno"</note>, and from Signor Gustavo Frizzoni of Milan. The translation of the Italian text into English, despite many difficulties, is mainly due to Mrs. R. C. Bell. Meanwhile, the rendering of several of the most puzzling and important passages—particularly in the second half of Volume I—I owe to the tireless interest taken in this work by Mr. E. J. Poynter, R.A. <note>Edward John Poynter was a prominent painter and later President of the Royal Academy (R.A.).</note>. Finally, I must express my thanks to Mr. Alfred Marks of Long Ditton, who has most kindly assisted me throughout in the revision of the proof sheets.*

*I owe the notes and dissertations on the architectural texts in Volume II to my friend Baron Henri de Geymüller of Paris.*

*I may further mention, regarding the illustrations, that the negatives for the production of the "photogravures" <note>An early chemical-photographic process used to produce high-quality prints of drawings and paintings.</note> by Monsieur Dujardin of Paris were all taken directly from the originals.*

*It is scarcely necessary to add that most of the drawings reproduced here in facsimile have never been published before. As I am now, at the end of a project lasting several years, in a position to review the general character of Leonardo's writings, I may perhaps be permitted to add a word as to my own estimate of their value. I have already shown that it is only due to a random series of unfortunate circumstances that we have not, long ago, known Leonardo not merely as a painter, but as an author, a philosopher, and a naturalist. There can be no doubt that in more than one department, his principles and discoveries were infinitely more in accord with the teachings of modern science than with the views of his contemporaries. For this reason, his extraordinary gifts and merits are far more likely to be appreciated in our own time...*

<summary>The author acknowledges the many librarians, scholars, and translators who assisted in compiling Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts and argues that Leonardo's scientific and philosophical insights were centuries ahead of his time.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, archives, librarians, Renaissance scholarship, photogravure, scientific history, art history, E. J. Poynter, Henri de Geymüller</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
than they could have been during the preceding centuries. He has been unjustly accused of wasting his talents by starting a variety of studies and then, having barely begun, casting them aside. The truth is that the labors of three centuries have hardly been enough to clarify some of the problems that occupied his mighty mind.

Alexander von Humboldt has testified that “he was the first to start on the road toward the point where all the impressions of our senses converge in the idea of the Unity of Nature.” Indeed, even more may be said. The very words inscribed on the monument of Alexander von Humboldt himself, in Berlin, are perhaps the most appropriate for summarizing our assessment of Leonardo’s genius:

“Majestati naturae par ingenium.”

LONDON, April 1883.

J. P. R.

<meta>This page serves as the opening of Volume I, establishing the editorial methods used to transcribe and translate Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts.</meta>

<lang>English</lang>
<sig>A</sig>

### I.

# Preliminary Discourse and General Introduction to the Book on Painting.
<note>original: "Prolegomena"</note>

## Key to the signs and abbreviations.
<note>original: "Clavis sigillorum"</note>

1.  In the few instances in which Leonardo wrote from left to right in the ordinary way, this is stated in a note. In all other cases, the writing is backwards <note>Leonardo da Vinci famously used "mirror writing," which is read by holding the page up to a mirror.</note>.
2.  The numbers printed above the line in the revised text—$^2, ^3, ^4$, etc.—indicate the start of each line in the original manuscript. In many instances, the breaking off of the lines in the original manuscript accounts for peculiarities in the structure of Leonardo's sentences. In the translation, these numbers refer only to the footnotes and have been introduced in passages that require an explanation.
3.  Clerical errors and obvious mistakes in spelling have been corrected in the text but are provided in the notes, so that all the peculiarities of the original text omitted in the revised version may be seen at a glance.
4.  Leonardo frequently employs the following abbreviations:
    <term>ꝑ</term> for *per* or *pr*; for example, <term>ꝑ</term> che = *perche* (because); or <term>ꝑ</term> a = *sopra* (above).
    <term>ꝺ</term> for *di* (of).
    <term>ꝃ</term> for *br*; for example, <term>ꝃ</term> eve = *breve* (brief).
    <term>ꝩ</term> for *ver*; for example, in <term>ꝩ</term> so = *inverso* (towards).
    <term>ꝼ</term> for *ser*; for example, <term>ꝼ</term> vo = *servo* (servant).

These occur so constantly and are so minor that it has not been thought necessary to point them out. He also uses:

i for *uno* (one, masculine).
î for *una* (one, feminine).

<summary>This page introduces the editorial conventions for the volume, explaining Leonardo da Vinci's use of mirror writing, line-numbering systems, and common shorthand abbreviations found in his manuscripts.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, mirror writing, manuscript transcription, paleography, Italian abbreviations, editorial notes</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
## PROLEGOMENA.

5. *Such abbreviations as are common in familiar speech are retained in the text; for example, un sol punto.*

6. *Leonardo's usual way of spelling, such as ochio spechio for occhio specchio, has also been left unaltered.*

7. *The combinations of two or three words into one, which Leonardo so frequently used, and which are so puzzling to the eye that they make reading difficult—though they are plain to the ear—have been separated in the revised text; for example, leforme ditutti has been changed to le forme di tutti. These combinations were, however, undoubtedly intentional; in almost every case they indicate the author's desire to substitute a sort of phonetic writing for the rules in general use. This doubling of letters—as, for instance, in chessia for che sia and essella for e se la—is, I believe, clear evidence of what may be called the orthography of Leonardo da Vinci. The separation of the words has resulted in the loss of these doubled letters, but the original spelling has been provided for reference in the footnotes.*

8. *Leonardo commonly wrote ā ē ī ō ū or v̄ for an, en, in, on, un. This sign occasionally, but not often, represents m. It has been retained, as it was common in printed type in the 15th and 16th centuries.*

9. *Leonardo sometimes writes j for i, particularly where it is joined to m, n, or u; for example, linje, tienj, mjnor. As he never places a dot over the ordinary i (at least when he writes from right to left), it is clear that he uses j for i (he does not dot the j either) simply to avoid confusing ni or ui with m, or mi with nu. As this difficulty cannot occur in print, I have restored the usual spelling i for j without mentioning it in the notes.*

10. *Accents and apostrophes are entirely lacking in the original manuscript, but it seemed necessary to introduce them into the printed text. The accent has also been added in those parts of the verb avere (to have) in which Leonardo had dropped the h: such as ò, ài, à, ànno.*

11. | || ||| ( ) *In the manuscripts, there are no marks of punctuation except these, and they have been retained wherever they occur. The dot (·) is always placed by Leonardo just above the line of writing and is never used as a period, but only to divide words according to the sense; it very often occurs between every word, particularly in manuscripts from about 1490. When a letter or number is placed between two dots, such as . a . or . 3 ., it usually refers to a corresponding sign on a diagram or sketch.*

| || ||| *These commonly serve to separate sentences which are entirely distinct.*

| . *This mark commonly indicates that words written above or below the line are to be inserted. In the revised text, they have simply been inserted.*

*In the notes, these passages are distinguished by the following signs:*

« » *indicates that the words were written above the line.*

“ ” *indicates that the words were written below the line.*

( ) *This mark is used by Leonardo to set off a digression, a parenthesis, or a quotation from another of his own works; however, it often takes the place of the colon.*

( *A simple bracket placed at the beginning of one or more lines serves to emphasize particular sentences; it is also used to mark distinct sentences that have no connection with the rest of the text on the same page. In the printed text, such sentences have been denoted by the mark ¶.*

--- Page 41 ---
PROLEGOMENA.

---

— *The last line of a section commonly ends with a horizontal line of varying length, making it equal in length to the preceding lines of writing.*

12. 3, 4, 5. *These figures, if written large, or some similar mark, are occasionally placed at the end of a page or at the beginning of a passage that has been crossed out; this indicates that the continuation is to be found elsewhere, where the same sign is repeated.*

*The signs ⊖ omicron, which occur in the passages on painting, were added by an early copyist and have therefore not been reproduced in the notes.*

13. , . : ; ! ? *These punctuation marks are never used in the original manuscripts. It seemed necessary, however, to insert such marks to make the text intelligible. A full stop is only used at the end of a section to avoid confusion with Leonardo's own use of points (see No. 11), for he never places one at the end of a section or paragraph. Wherever a full stop seemed missing in the middle of the text, I have used a semicolon (;). The colon (:) is used instead of a full stop where a point (.) occurs in the original.*

14. [ ] *Passages between brackets are crossed out in the original.*

15. *When a word or passage of the revised text is printed in small type, it indicates that the reading is doubtful due to partial obliteration.*

16. |||||| *indicates passages in which the original writing is entirely destroyed.*

17. R *indicates that the passage is written in red chalk.*

18. (R) *indicates that the original writing in red chalk has been written over in pen and ink.*

19. P *indicates that the original writing is in silverpoint.*

20. 1ª 2ª 3ª etc. *the front page—recto—of sheet 1, 2, 3, etc.*
1ᵇ 2ᵇ 3ᵇ etc. *the back page—verso—of sheet 1, 2, 3, etc.*

*The manuscripts Tr. and S. K. M. I² are the only ones in which the pages are numbered. In all other manuscripts, only the leaf is numbered. In referring to the Codex Atlanticus, a double series of numbers has been used. The first applies only to the larger leaves of the Codex, on which two or more of the original leaves of the manuscript have been mounted; the second series does not exist in the Codex itself; it refers to the original pages in the order in which they were placed in it. By this second series of numbers, the correspondence of the front and back pages has been verified. Wherever a different number occurs in Leonardo’s writing in addition to the consecutive numbering, it is quoted in parentheses, thus:—C 27ᵇ (3ª); this indicates that the back page of leaf 27 in manuscript C was originally numbered 3.*

21. *A Roman II, such as 26 IIª 26 IIᵇ, indicates that the same number (26) occurs twice. In the Codex Atlanticus, I/I is used for II.*

22. O', O'' *indicates that the passages so marked are originally notes written on the inside of the manuscript cover; O' on the front or upper cover, O'' on the back cover.*

23. *The woodcuts introduced into the text are facsimile reproductions of Leonardo's own sketches and drawings that accompany the manuscripts. However, the letters and numbers attached to them have been inserted in ordinary writing.*

--- Page 46 ---
8
GENERAL INTRODUCTION TO THE BOOK ON PAINTING.

Contrary to the universal custom of Western nations, Leonardo committed almost all his notes to paper in a handwriting that goes from right to left. This singular habit has sometimes been explained by assuming that Leonardo felt it necessary to place every possible obstacle in the way of the publication of his works. This assumption, however, seems to me to rest on no solid grounds and is merely a hypothesis at best. Perfectly explicit statements prove, on the contrary, that Leonardo wished to publish his writings and that he cared greatly that they should be known and read; and anyone who has taken the trouble to become familiar with the Master’s writing will, I think, hardly resist the conviction that even the style of the writing was expressly adapted to that purpose.

We know from the evidence of his friend Luca Paciolo that Leonardo drew with his left hand and used it with perfect ease.<sup>1</sup> In fact, in almost every drawing authentically known to be genuine—as those included in the texts of the manuscripts must be—wherever shading is introduced, the strokes run from left to right (downwards) as they would be drawn with the left hand.<sup>2</sup>

The question of why Leonardo drew and wrote with his left hand is now probably a useless one. There is nothing to justify us in deciding whether accidental circumstance or mere whim was the cause. It is worth noting that the earliest notes, written in his twenty-first year, when he could hardly have had such reasons for caution as are attributed to him,<sup>3</sup> are written backwards.

The contents of Leonardo’s manuscripts sufficiently prove that he certainly intended them for publication, though the form is probably not always what he finally intended it to be.

The appeal or address "tu" (you), which frequently occurs and more particularly in theoretical passages, is often no doubt meant for the reader; but in other cases, it indicates instead the specifically meditative character of the passage. Abstract speculations acquire a particular charm from this soliloquizing form—it is as if we overheard the mental process of the author.

In the passages indicated below, Leonardo expresses himself clearly regarding the end and purpose of his literary labors.

In one passage in the manuscript at Holkham (No. 1), he speaks of keeping a certain invention to himself and not making it public. As he uses this reserve in no other instance, this exception sufficiently proves the rule.

---

1 "It is also written backwards and left-handed so that it cannot be read except with a mirror, or by holding the paper from its reverse side against the light, as I know you understand without me saying more, and as our Leonardo da Vinci does, the light of painting, who is left-handed, as has been said many times." (L. PACIOLO, *Divina Proportione*, Venice, 1509.)

2 This was first pointed out in the ‘*Critical review of the drawings by the old Masters in the Dresden Gallery*’ by Senator GIOV. MORELLI.

3 "To express it much as he did: on one hand, narrow and conventional minds, and on the other, adventurers tilting against everything that had previously allowed for the establishment of those rules which, by determining the limit of the possible and the impossible, prevent the researcher from falling into despair and melancholy, exaggerated at will what he had said (?) to react against abuses and paradoxes, and used his own expressions (?) to represent him as a charlatan or a madman. When he spoke, at least, his persuasive eloquence gave his ideas their full value; but to allow thoughts incompletely expressed, unfinished drafts, and projects for inventions of all kinds to be seen in his papers, *would have been to expose himself to calumny and theft*." (CH. RAVAISSON-MOLLIEN, *Les Manuscrits de L. de Vinci*, Paris 1881, p. 2). But we might suppose that Leonardo would have considered his papers and his instruments quite safe by keeping them locked away in his own room.

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Leonardo da Vinci’s manuscripts, specifically focusing on his intended organization for his "Book on Painting" and his scientific observations on optics.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>GENERAL INTRODUCTION TO THE BOOK ON PAINTING.</header>

*In the passage from Manuscript F (No. 2), the expression “to put together” <note>original: “mettere insieme”</note> is equally characteristic of his working method and of the condition of the manuscripts. By this, he means the classification of the individual details of his research so as to make a connected whole. This could be done more easily because it was his practice to write separate chapters on separate sheets of paper.*

*The manuscript in the British Museum begins with an apology (No. 4) for the obvious disorder of the text, which is very interesting. This apology applies equally well to the notes on mathematics—where it is placed—and to all the branches of science about which Leonardo wrote.*

*The passages (Nos. 5—7) are soliloquies and refer to the arrangement of different manuscripts as preparation for publication in the form Leonardo himself intended. From all this, it was clearly not his intention that the notes should be printed exactly as they lay, in confusion, under his hand.*

*The plans that Leonardo himself proposed for the arrangement of the Book on Painting, as well as his other writings, give us a clue—as we shall soon see—which enables us to perfectly reconstruct the entire work based on his own rules and with a logical sequence.*

*We may conclude that sections 9, 10, and 11, headed “Preface” <note>original: “Proemio”</note>, refer to the Book on Painting, and more particularly to the lessons on Perspective, because section 21, with its special title “Preface to perspective” <note>original: “Proemio di prospettiva”</note>, is written on the same sheet in the original (Codex Atlanticus 117<sup>b</sup>; 561<sup>b</sup>).*

*Sections 12 to 20 give us the guiding idea of the general plan and of the object and purpose of the Book on Painting <note>original: “Libro di Pittura”</note>.*

*No. 21, “Preface to perspective, that is, on the function of the eye” <note>original: “Proemio di prospettiva, cioè dell’ ufitio dell’ ochio”</note>, follows naturally after the other general introductions. Our acceptance of this introduction, it is true, completely invalidates the arrangement of materials that has been adopted by every editor of the old copies of the Treatise <note>original: “Trattato”</note> since <term>DUFRESNE</term> <note>Charles Alphonse du Fresne (1611–1688), a French scholar who edited the first printed edition of Leonardo's Treatise on Painting in 1651.</note>; but it must be remembered that those editions contain only disconnected fragments of Leonardo’s treatise on Perspective. His investigations into all branches of optics do not, of course, come under consideration here. Regarding the physiology of the eye, the reader will find in Nos. 24 and 28—39 passages which show that Leonardo understood how variations in the size of the pupil affect the perception of objects. The insertion of these passages seemed indispensable because they form the basis of certain general principles of Perspective. The same may be said about his explanation of the difference between seeing with one eye and seeing with two (No. 25—29), as well as his acute remarks on the apparent variation in the size of objects according to the amount of light in which they are seen (No. 30—39).*

# I.

<margin>Leicester Manuscript, 22 b]</margin>
<margin>The author's intention to publish his manuscripts.</margin>

How many people can stay underwater for a while with a certain device <note>original: "istrumēto"</note>. Why and how I do not write down my method for staying underwater, or how long I can remain without eating. I do not publish or reveal these things because of the evil nature of men, who would use them for murders <note>original: "assassinamēti"</note> at the bottom of the seas by breaking the hulls of ships and sinking them along with the men inside. Although I will explain other methods, those are not dangerous because the end of the breathing tube <note>original: "canna"</note> remains above water, supported by air sacs <note>original: "otri"</note> or cork.

How by a certain machine many may stay some time under water. And how and why I do not describe my method of remaining under water and how long I can remain without eating. And I do not publish or reveal these, because of the evil nature of men, who would use them for assassinations at the bottom of the sea by destroying ships and sinking them, together with the men in them. Nevertheless, I will share others which are not dangerous, because the mouth of the tube through which you breathe is above the water, supported on air sacs or cork.

---

# 2.

<margin>Manuscript F, 2 b]</margin>
<margin>The preparation of the manuscripts for publication.</margin>

When you put together the science of the motions of water, remember to include under each proposition its practical applications <note>original: "giouamēti"</note>, so that this science is not useless.

When you put together the science of the motions of water, remember to include under each proposition its application and use, in order that this science may not be useless.

---

# 3.

<margin>Windsor Anatomy, Vol. IV, 163 b]</margin>
<margin>Warning to readers.</margin>

Let no one who is not a mathematician read my principles <note>original: "nelli mia pricipi"</note>.

Let no man who is not a mathematician read the elements of my work.

**1.** The leaf on which this passage is written is headed with the words *39 Cases*, and most of these cases begin with the word "How," like the two given here, which are the 26th and 27th. 7. *Cork* <note>original: "Sughero"</note>. By the tube, the word "tube" <note>original: "channa"</note> is written, and by the disk, the word "cork."

--- Page 50 ---
GENERAL INTRODUCTION TO THE BOOK ON PAINTING.

# Br. M. 1 a]

4.

Begun in Florence, in the house of Piero di Braccio Martelli, on the 22nd day of March 1508. This is to be a collection without specific order, drawn from many papers that I have copied here, hoping later to arrange them in their proper places according to the subjects they address. I believe that before I finish this task, I will have to repeat the same things several times; for this, O reader, do not blame me, because the subjects are numerous and memory cannot retain them all and say, "I will not write this because I have written it before." If I wished to avoid falling into such an error, it would be necessary for me to reread everything I had previously written every time I wanted to copy a passage, so as not to repeat it; this is especially difficult given the long intervals of time between one period of writing and the next.

# F. 23 a]

5.

On digging a canal. Put this in the Book of Useful Inventions, and when proving them, cite the propositions already established. This is the proper order; for if you wished to demonstrate the usefulness of every proposition, you would also need to create new instruments to prove that utility. In doing so, you would confuse the order of the forty books as well as the order of the illustrations; that is, you would have to mix practice with theory, which would result in a confused and fragmented work.

# Br. M. 32 b]

6.

I should not be blamed for presenting, within the orderly progression of scientific inquiry, any general rule derived from a previously established conclusion.

--- Page 51 ---
7.

The book on the science of mechanics must precede the book on useful inventions; have your books on anatomy bound!

---

8.

The order of your book must proceed in this manner: first simple beams, then those supported from below, then those suspended in part, then those suspended entirely. Finally, those beams shall serve as supports for other weights.

---

9.

### INTRODUCTION.

Seeing that I cannot find a subject of great utility or pleasure—since the men born before me have already taken for themselves every useful and necessary theme—I will act like one who, due to poverty, arrives last at the fair. Being unable to provide for himself in any other way, he takes all the things already seen by other buyers and not accepted, but rather rejected because of their little value. I will take this despised and rejected merchandise, left behind by many buyers, and load it upon my humble pack. With it, I will go about distributing it, not in the great cities, but in poor villages, accepting such a reward as the things I offer may deserve.

---

10.

### INTRODUCTION.

I know that many will say this is a useless work; and these will be the ones of whom Demetrius said he took no account.

--- Page 52 ---
...more of the wind that came out of their mouths in words than of the wind they expelled from their lower parts. These are men who desire nothing but material riches and are entirely devoid of wisdom, which is the food and the only true riches of the soul. For just as the soul is more worthy than the body, so the possessions of the soul are more noble than those of the body. Often, when I see one of these men take this work in his hand, I wonder if he will put it to his nose like a monkey, or ask me if it is something good to eat.

## INTRODUCTION.

I am fully aware that, because I am not a literary man, certain presumptuous persons will think they may reasonably blame me, alleging that I am a man without letters. Foolish folk! Do they not know that I might retort as Marius did to the Roman Patricians, by saying: "They, who deck themselves out in the labors of others, will not allow me my own." They will say that because I have no literary skill, I cannot properly express the subject I wish to treat. But they do not know that my subjects are to be dealt with by experience rather than by the words of others; and experience has been the mistress of those who wrote well. And so, as my mistress, I will cite her in all cases.

--- Page 53 ---
# II.

Though I may not be able to quote other authors as they do, I shall rely on something much greater and more worthy: experience, the mistress of their own masters. These people go about puffed up and pompous, dressed and decorated not with the fruits of their own labors, but with those of others; yet they will not allow me my own. They will scorn me as an inventor, but how much more might they—who are not inventors but merely trumpeters and reciters of the works of others—be blamed.

### INTRODUCTION.

Those men who are inventors and interpreters between Nature and Man, as compared with the boasters and reciters of the works of others, must be judged and esteemed no differently than an object in front of a mirror compared to its image reflected in the mirror. For the first is something in itself, while the other is nothing. These are people little indebted to Nature, since they are only accidentally clothed in human form; without it, I might class them among the herds of beasts.

# 12.

Many will think they can reasonably criticize me by claiming that my proofs are contrary to the authority of certain men held in high reverence by their inexperienced judgments. They do not consider that my works are born of pure and simple experience, which is the only true mistress. These rules are the means of making you distinguish the true from the false, which enables men to pursue possible things with more moderation, and prevents you from wrapping yourself in ignorance—a thing that, having no result, would cause you to give yourself up to melancholy in despair.

# 13.

Among the studies of natural causes and reasons, light chiefly delights the beholder; and among the great features of mathematics...

--- Page 54 ---
The certainty of its demonstrations is what preeminently tends to elevate the mind of the investigator. Perspective, therefore, must be preferred to all the discourses and systems of human learning. In this branch of science, the beam of light is explained through those methods of demonstration which form the glory not so much of Mathematics as of Physics, and are graced with the flowers of both. But since its axioms are laid down at great length, I shall condense them into a conclusive brevity, arranging them according to the method of both their natural order and mathematical demonstration; sometimes by deducting the effects from the causes, and sometimes arguing the causes from the effects. I will also add to my own conclusions some which, though not included in them, may nevertheless be inferred from them. Thus, if the Lord—who is the light of all things—vouchsafes to enlighten me, I will treat of Light; for which reason I will divide the present work into 3 Parts.

---

### ON THE THREE BRANCHES OF PERSPECTIVE.

There are three branches of perspective: the first deals with the reasons for the apparent diminution of objects as they recede from the eye, and is known as Diminishing Perspective. The second contains the way in which colors vary as they recede from the eye. The third and last is concerned with the explanation of how objects in a picture ought to be less finished in proportion as they are remote. The names are as follows:

> Linear Perspective.
> The Perspective of Colour.
> The Perspective of Disappearance.

--- Page 55 ---
**15.**

### ON PAINTING AND PERSPECTIVE.

The divisions of Perspective used in painting are three: the first concerns the reduction in size of opaque objects; the second deals with the reduction and loss of the outlines of those opaque objects; the third concerns the reduction and loss of colors at a great distance.

---

**16.**

### THE DISCOURSE ON PAINTING.

Perspective, as it applies to painting, is divided into three principal parts: the first is the reduction in size of objects at various distances. The second part is that which deals with the reduction of the colors of such objects. The third deals with the diminished clarity of the shapes and outlines that these objects possess at various distances.

---

**17.**

### ON THE SECTIONS OF PAINTING.

The first principle of painting is that the objects depicted should appear in relief, and that the backgrounds surrounding them, with their respective distances, should appear to recede into the wall where the painting is created. This is achieved by means of the three perspectives: namely, the reduction in the definition of the shapes of objects, the reduction of their size, and the reduction of their colors. Of these three perspectives, the first originates from the eye, while the other two are derived from the air interposed between the eye and the object seen.

---

**15.** The division here is the same as in the previous chapter, No. 14, and this is noteworthy when we consider that a period of about 20 years must have passed between the writing of the two passages.

**17.** This and the two preceding chapters must have been written between 1513 and 1516. They undoubtedly indicate the plan that Leonardo wished to follow in organizing his research on Perspective as applied to Painting. This is important because it serves as evidence against the theory of H. Ludwig and others, who suggest that Leonardo had collected his principles of Perspective into a single book as early as 1500; a book which, according to that hypothesis, must have been lost very early or possibly destroyed by the French in 1500 (see H. Ludwig, *L. da Vinci: The Book on Painting*, Vienna 1882, III, 7 and 8).

--- Page 56 ---
...and the objects seen by that eye. The second essential in painting is appropriate action and a due variety in the figures, so that the men may not all look like brothers, etc.

---

**18.**

### THE USE OF THE BOOK ON PAINTING.

These rules are to be used only for correcting figures, since every man makes some mistakes in his first compositions; and he who does not recognize them cannot fix them. Therefore, in order to recognize your errors, you should examine your work, and where you find such mistakes, correct them; and keep it in mind never to fall into them again. But if you try to apply these rules while you are composing, you will never finish anything and will create confusion in your works.

These rules will enable you to possess a free and sound judgment; since good judgment is born of clear understanding, and clear understanding derives from reason drawn from sound rules, and sound rules are the daughters of sound experience—the common mother of all the sciences and arts. Therefore, by keeping the precepts of my rules well in mind, you will be able, through your corrected judgment alone, to judge and recognize every disproportionate work, whether in perspective, in figures, or in other things.

---

**19.**

### ON THE MISTAKE OF THOSE WHO USE PRACTICE WITHOUT SCIENCE.

Those who fall in love with practice without science are like a pilot who enters a ship without a rudder or a compass, and who can never be certain where he is going. Practice must always be built upon sound theory, for which perspective is the guide and the gateway; and without this, nothing is done well in matters of painting.

---

**20.**

The painter who draws by practice and the judgment of the eye alone, without reason, is like a mirror, which imitates all the things placed before it without any knowledge of them.

--- Page 57 ---

### 21.

<meta>Codex Atlanticus 117b; 361 b]</meta>

## PREFACE ON PERSPECTIVE: 
## THAT IS, ON THE FUNCTION OF THE EYE.
<note>original Italian: "Proemio di Prospettiva, cioè dell' ufitio dell' ochio"</note>

Look here, reader, at something we cannot believe from our ancestors—the ancients—who attempted to define the nature of the soul and of life. These are things that cannot be proven, while those things that can be clearly known and proven through experience at any moment have remained unknown or misunderstood for many centuries. The eye, whose function we know so certainly through experience, has been defined in one way by countless authors until my own time; however, I find through experience that it is something quite different. <note>Leonardo refers here to his discovery that images are inverted on the retina, challenging the "infinite number of authors" who preceded him.</note>

<margin>The function of the eye (21—23).</margin>

---

### 22.

<meta>Codex Atlanticus 337 b; 1026 b]</meta>

Here, in the eye, the forms, the colors, and every aspect of the parts of the universe are reduced to a single point. And that point is a thing of such wonder! O marvelous, O stupendous Necessity! By your laws, you compel every effect to arise from its cause by the shortest possible path. These are indeed miracles... 

...In such a tiny space, the world can be reborn and reconstructed in its full expansion. Write in your anatomy notes what the proportion is between the diameters of all the parts of the eye, and what the distance is between them and the **crystalline lens**. <term>crystalline lens: the natural lens of the eye, which Leonardo studied to understand how light is refracted</term>.

---

### 23.

<meta>Ashburnham I. 13a]</meta>

## ON THE TEN FUNCTIONS OF THE EYE, 
## ALL RELEVANT TO PAINTING.

Painting encompasses all ten functions of the eye: **darkness, light, solidity and color, shape and location, distance and closeness, motion and rest.** This small work of mine will be woven from these functions, reminding the painter of the rules and methods by which he should use his art to imitate all these things—the works of nature and the adornment of the world.

---

**21.** <note>In section 13, we already find it indicated that the study of Perspective and Optics is to be based on the functions of the eye. Leonardo also refers to the science of the eye in his astronomical researches; for instance, in Manuscript F, page 25b: "The order for proving that the earth is a star: first, define the eye," etc. <note>original Italian: "Ordine del provare la terra essere una stella: Imprima difinisce l'occhio"</note> Compare also Manuscript E, page 15b and Manuscript F, page 60b on the principles of astronomical perspective.</note>

**22.** <note>The manuscript leaf is damaged between lines 11 and 16; only fragments of words remain.</note>

| <margin>The variability of the eye.</margin> 1. The pupil of the eye decreases in size as much as the light shining into it increases. 2. The pupil of the eye increases in size as much as the brightness of the day (or any other light) shining into it decreases. 3. The eye perceives and recognizes the objects before it more intensely as its pupil dilates more widely. We can prove this by observing nocturnal animals, such as cats and certain birds like the owl and similar creatures, in which the pupil changes tremendously from large to small, etc., depending on whether they are in darkness or in light. 4. An eye positioned in a brightly lit area sees darkness inside the windows of lit houses. 5. All colors placed in shaded areas appear to be of equal darkness to one another. 6. All colors, when placed in the shade, appear to have an equal degree of darkness among themselves. 6th. But all colors, when placed in a full light, never vary from their true and essential hue. |

| <margin>Focus of sight.</margin> If the eye is required to see something that is too close, it cannot judge it well—as happens to someone who tries to look at the tip of their own nose. Therefore, as a general rule, Nature teaches that an object will never be seen perfectly unless the distance between the eye and the object is at least equal to the size of the face. | If the eye is required to look at an object placed too near to it, it cannot judge it well—as happens to a man who tries to see the tip of his nose. Hence, as a general rule, Nature teaches us that an object can never be seen perfectly unless the space between it and the eye is equal, at least, to the length of the face. |

<sup>1</sup> The movement of an observer looking at a stationary object often makes that <sup>2</sup> stationary object seem to move with the observer’s motion, <sup>3</sup> while the moving observer appears to be standing still.

> The motion of a spectator who sees an object at rest often makes it seem as though the object at rest had acquired the motion of the moving body, while the moving person appears to be at rest.

### PAINTING. <note>original Italian: "PITTURA"</note>

<sup>5</sup> Objects in relief <note>original: "rilievo"; referring to three-dimensional objects</note> seen from nearby with only one eye appear <sup>6</sup> similar to a perfect painting. If you <sup>7</sup> look with the eye at points **a** and **b** toward the point **c**, that point **c** will <sup>8</sup> appear to you to be at **d** and **f**. And if you look at it with <sup>9</sup> only eye **g**, it will appear at **m**. A painting will never <sup>10</sup> contain these two different perspectives within itself.

### ON PAINTING.

> Objects in relief, when seen from a short distance with one eye, look like a perfect picture. If you look with the eye **a**, **b** at the spot **c**, this point **c** will appear to be at **d**, **f**, and if you look at it with the eye **g**, it will appear to be at **m**. A picture can never contain in itself both aspects.

<margin>W. An. IV. 153 b]</margin>

->29.<-

<sup>1</sup> Let the object in relief, **t**, <sup>2</sup> be seen by two eyes. <sup>3</sup> If you <sup>4</sup> consider the <sup>5</sup> object with the right eye **m**, <sup>6</sup> while keeping the left eye **n** closed, <sup>7</sup> the object will appear to occupy space **a**. If you close the right eye and open the left, the object will occupy <sup>8</sup> space **b**. If you open both eyes, that object will no longer occupy **a** or **b**, but the space **e**, **r**, **f**. <sup>9</sup> Why is it that a painting seen with two eyes does not <sup>10</sup> demonstrate the same sense of relief as a three-dimensional object <sup>11</sup> seen with two eyes? And why does a <sup>12</sup> painting seen with one eye appear to have relief <sup>13</sup> just like a real object, provided it has the same <sup>14</sup> qualities of light and shadow?

> Let the object in relief **t** be seen by both eyes; if you look at the object with the right eye **m**, keeping the left eye **n** shut, the object will appear, or fill up the space, at **a**; and if you shut the right eye and open the left, the object (will occupy the) space **b**; and if you open both eyes, the object will no longer appear at **a** or **b**, but at **e**, **r**, **f**. Why will not a picture seen by both eyes produce the effect of relief, as [real] relief does when seen by both eyes; and why should a picture seen with one eye give the same effect of relief as real relief would under the same conditions of light and shade?

<margin>C. 7 b (9)]</margin>

->30.<-

<sup>1</sup> The eye will better hold and retain the images of luminous things than those of shaded things. The reason is this: <sup>2</sup> the eye is naturally in a state of total darkness. Because like things cannot be distinguished from one another, the night and other dark objects cannot be <sup>3</sup> retained or recognized by the eye. Light is entirely the opposite; it creates more contrast, provides more distinctness, and varies more from the <sup>4</sup> usual darkness of the eye, thus leaving its image impressed upon it.

<margin>The comparative size of the image depends on the amount of light (30—39).</margin>

> The eye will hold and retain in itself the image of a luminous body better than that of a shaded object. The reason is that the eye is in itself perfectly dark and since two things that are alike cannot be distinguished, therefore the night, and other dark objects cannot be seen or recognized by the eye. Light is totally contrary and gives more distinctness, and counteracts and differs from the usual darkness of the eye, hence it leaves the impression of its image.

***

<note>Note on Section 29: In the sketch, *m* is the left eye and *n* the right, while the text reverses this lettering. We must therefore suppose that the face in which the eyes *m* and *n* are placed is opposite to the spectator.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains the optical principles of parallax and stereoscopic vision, noting that while a painting can perfectly mimic three-dimensional relief when viewed with one eye, it cannot replicate the dual perspectives perceived by two eyes. He also theorizes that the eye retains images of light more effectively than darkness because of the inherent "darkness" of the eye itself.</summary>

--- Page 60 ---
31.

Every object we see will appear larger at midnight than at midday, and larger in the morning than at midday. This happens because the pupil of the eye is much smaller at midday than at any other time.

32.

The pupil that is larger will see objects as having a larger shape. This is demonstrated in the viewing of luminous bodies, especially celestial ones; when the eye emerges from darkness and suddenly looks at these bodies, they will appear larger to it and then diminish. And if you look at these same bodies through a small hole, you will see them as smaller because a smaller part of the pupil is employed for that task.

33.

The eye that, upon emerging from darkness, suddenly sees a luminous body, will find it appears much larger at the first glance than it does upon continued viewing. The luminous body will appear larger and more luminous with two eyes than with only one. That luminous object will show itself to be of a smaller size when it is seen by the eye through a smaller opening. A luminous body of a long shape will show itself to be of a rounder shape the further it is situated from the eye.

34.

Why, when the eye has seen the light, does a place of medium light seem dark to it, and similarly, having emerged from the darkness, does a place of medium light seem very bright to it?

35.

ON PAINTING.

The eye that stays in the luminous air and sees a shadowed place will find that the site appears to be of much greater darkness than it actually is. This happens only because the eye that stays in the air diminishes its pupil so much more—

--- Page 61 ---
...the pupil, as the atmosphere reflected in it is more luminous. And the more the pupil contracts, the less luminous the objects it sees appear to be. But as soon as the eye enters a shady place, the darkness of that shadow suddenly seems to diminish. This occurs because the greater the darkness into which the pupil goes, the more its size increases, and this increase makes the great darkness seem to lessen.

---

### 36.

ON PERSPECTIVE.

The eye that turns from a white object illuminated by the sun and goes into a place of less light will see everything as dark. This happens because the eye that rests on that illuminated white object causes its pupils to contract so much that, if they previously had a certain visual area, they now lose more than three-quarters of their size; and, lacking in size, they also lack power. Although you might say to me: "A small bird would then see very little, and because of its small pupils, white would appear black to it!" To this I would reply that here we must consider the proportion of the mass of that part of the brain dedicated to the sense of sight, and nothing else. Or—to return to our subject—this pupil grows and diminishes according to the brightness or darkness of its object. And because it takes some time to perform this growing and shrinking, it does not see as quickly when coming out of the light and going into the dark, and similarly from the dark into the light; and this very thing once deceived me in painting an eye, and from that I learned it.

---

### 37.

An experiment on the increase and decrease of the pupil caused by the motion of the sun or another luminous body. The darker the sky is, the larger the stars will appear to be; and if you were to light up the atmosphere...

--- Page 62 ---
these stars would look smaller; and this difference arises solely from the pupil, which dilates and contracts according to the brightness of the medium that is positioned between the eye and the luminous body. Let the experiment be made by placing a candle above your head at the same time that you look at a star; then gradually lower the candle until it is level with the line that comes from the star to the eye, and then you will see the star diminish so much that you will almost lose sight of it.

### 38.

The pupil of the eye, when in the open air, changes in size with every degree of motion made by the sun; and at every degree of its size, the same object seen will appear to be of different dimensions, although most frequently the comparison with surrounding objects does not allow us to detect these variations in a single object we are looking at.

### 39.

The eye—which sees all objects reversed—retains their images for some time. This conclusion is proved by the results, because the sight, in looking at light, retains some of it. Furthermore, after looking, images of the object perceived remain in the eye and make a place with less light appear dark, until the trace of the impression of the stronger light has vanished from the eye.

--- Page 63 ---
VOL. I.                                     D

# II.

## Linear Perspective.

We see clearly from the concluding sentence of section 49, where the author directly addresses the painter, that he must certainly have intended to include the elements of mathematics in his book on the art of painting. They are therefore placed here at the beginning. In section 50, the theory of the “Pyramid of Sight” is distinctly and expressly put forward as the fundamental principle of linear perspective, and sections 52 to 57 treat it fully. This theory of sight can scarcely be traced to any author of antiquity. Such passages as occur in Euclid, for instance, may, it is true, have proved suggestive to the painters of the Renaissance, but it would be rash to say anything decisive on this point.

Leon Battista Alberti treats the “Pyramid of Sight” at some length in his first book of painting; but his explanation differs widely from Leonardo's in the details. Leonardo, like Alberti, may have borrowed the broad lines of his theory from some views commonly accepted among painters at the time; but he certainly worked out its application in a perfectly original manner.

The axioms regarding the perception of the pyramid of rays are followed by explanations of its origin and proofs of its universal application (58—69). The author recurs to the subject with endless variations; it is evidently of fundamental importance in his artistic theory and practice. It is unnecessary to discuss how far this theory has any scientific value at the present day; at any rate, this much seems certain: that from the artist's point of view, it may still claim to be of immense practical utility.

According to Leonardo, on one hand, the laws of perspective are an inseparable condition of the existence of objects in space; on the other hand, by a natural law, the

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Leonardo da Vinci's writings on linear perspective, specifically focusing on his theories of optics and the mechanics of the eye.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>THE THEORY OF THE ART OF PAINTING.</header>

*The eye, whatever it sees and wherever it turns, perceives objects through a "pyramid of rays" in the form of a small target. Therefore, it sees objects in perspective regardless of the spectator's will, because the eye receives images by means of this pyramid of rays "just as a magnet attracts iron."*

*In connection with this, the function of the eye is explained by the <term>camera obscura</term> <note>Latin for "dark room"; an optical device that projects an image of its surroundings onto a screen or wall through a small hole</note>. This is all the more interesting and important because no writer before Leonardo had treated this subject (sections 70—73). Subsequent passages, which are of no less special interest, reveal his knowledge of refraction <note>the bending of light as it passes from one medium to another</note> and of the inversion of the image in the camera and in the eye (sections 74—82).*

*From the principle of how images are transmitted to the eye and to the camera obscura, he derives the method for creating an artificial construction of the pyramid of rays—or, what is the same thing, of the image itself. The fundamental principles regarding the angle of sight and the vanishing point are thus presented in a manner that is as complete as it is simple and easy to understand (sections 86—89).*

*Leonardo distinguishes between simple and complex perspective (sections 90, 91). The final sections treat the apparent size of objects at various distances and the way to estimate those sizes (sections 92—109).*

<summary>This page outlines Leonardo da Vinci's pioneering use of the camera obscura to explain human vision and his mathematical derivation of perspective from the physical behavior of light rays.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, camera obscura, optics, pyramid of rays, perspective, refraction, vanishing point, angle of sight, art theory</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
## 40.

**ON PAINTING.**
Perspective is the best bridle for the art of painting.

---

## 41.

Perspective is of such a nature that it makes a flat surface appear to be in relief, and relief appear flat.

---

## 42.

All the cases of perspective are understood by means of the five terms of mathematicians: namely, point, line, angle, surface, and body. Of these, the point is unique in its origin; and this point has neither height, nor width, nor length, nor depth, from which it is concluded to be indivisible and to have no location. The line is of three types: namely, straight, curved, and sinuous; and it has neither width, nor height, nor depth, so it is indivisible except in its length. Its ends are two points. An angle is the meeting of two lines at a point.

---

## 43.

A point is not a part of a line.

<margin>Ashburnham III. 27 b]</margin>
->44.<-

## CONCERNING THE NATURAL POINT <note>original: "DEL PŪTO NATURALE"</note>
## OF THE NATURAL POINT.

The smallest natural point is larger than all mathematical points. This is proven because the natural point is a continuous quantity, and anything that is continuous is infinitely divisible. However, the mathematical point is indivisible because it is not a quantity and has no size.

---

<margin>British Museum 131 b]</margin>
->45.<-

1. The surface <note>original: "superfities"</note> is the boundary of a body. 
2. The boundary of a body is not a part of that body itself. 
4. The boundary of one body is the beginning of another. 
3. That which is not part of any body is nothing. Nothing is that which occupies no space.

If a single point placed in a circle can be the starting point for an infinite number of lines, and the ends of an infinite number of lines separated from that point are themselves infinite points, then when these are brought back together into one, it follows that the part is equal to the whole.

---

Because the point is indivisible, it occupies no space. All things that occupy no space are nothing. The boundary of one thing is the beginning of another. 
2. That which is not part of any thing is called nothing. 
1. That which has no boundary has no shape at all. 
The boundaries of two bodies joined together are mutually the surfaces of one another. All boundaries of things are not actually parts of those things.

## DEFINITION OF THE NATURE OF THE LINE <note>original: "DIFINITIŌ DELL' ESSER DELLA LINIA"</note>
## DEFINITION OF THE NATURE OF THE LINE.

The line has no matter or substance in itself but may be called a conceptual entity <note>original: "cosa spirituale"</note> rather than a physical substance. Because it is defined this way, it occupies no space.

->HOW DO WE CONCLUDE THAT A SURFACE ENDS IN A POINT?<-
<note>original: "SUPERFITIE" or "SUPERFICIES"</note>

An angular surface is reduced to a point where it ends at a corner <term>angle</term>. If the sides of that angle are extended in a continuous straight line, then—beyond that point—another surface is created, which may be smaller, equal to, or larger than the first.

<margin>Ashburnham I. 21a</margin>

### ON THE OUTLINE OF A DRAWING
<note>original Latin/Italian: "DE PICTURA LINIALE"</note>

Carefully observe the boundaries of every body and the way they wind or curve <note>original: "serpeggiare"</note>. These undulations should be studied individually to judge whether the curves are made of arched convexities or angular concavities.

->49.<-

<margin>G. 37 a</margin>

The boundaries of objects are the smallest of all things. This is proven to be true because the boundary of an object is a surface, which is not part of the body enclosed by that surface, nor is it part of the air surrounding that body. Instead, it is the medium placed between the air and the body, as has been proven in its proper place. 

However, the side boundaries of these bodies are the lines forming the edge of the surface, and these lines have an invisible thickness. Therefore, you as a painter should not surround your figures with thick outlines—especially when depicting objects smaller than life size. In those cases, not only are the side boundaries impossible to see, but even the limbs themselves become invisible due to distance.

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s exploration of the geometry of vision. Having defined points and lines on the previous page, he now applies these concepts to explain "perspective" as the way light and images travel from an object to the eye in the shape of a pyramid.</meta>

<page-num>30, [51.]</page-num>

<header>THE THEORY OF THE ART OF PAINTING.</header>

<sig>C. A. 84b; 245 a] 51.</sig>

Perspective consists solely of receiving, in the form of a pyramid, the shapes and colors of all the objects placed before it. I say "in a pyramid" because there is no object so small that it will not be larger than the spot in the eye where these pyramids are received. Therefore, if you draw lines from the edges of any body and bring them together at a single point, those lines must necessarily form a pyramid.

[Perspective is nothing more than a demonstrative logic <note>original Italian: "ragione dimostrativa"</note> applied to consider how objects placed opposite the eye transmit their own likeness to it through a pyramid of lines. These lines are called a *Pyramid* because they start from the surface edges of each body and, through a distant convergence, are led to a single point.]

[Perspective is a demonstrative logic by which we may practically and clearly understand how objects transmit their own likeness <note>original: "similitudine," referring to the visual image or "species" emitted by an object</note> through lines forming a pyramid centered in the eye.]

¶ Perspective is a demonstrative logic by which experience confirms that every object sends its image to the eye by a pyramid of lines. Bodies of equal size will produce a larger or smaller angle in their pyramid depending on the distance between them. By a "pyramid of lines," I mean those which start from the surface and edges of bodies and, converging from a distance, meet in a single point. A point is said to be that which—having no dimensions—cannot be divided; and this point, being located in the eye, receives all the tips of these pyramids <term>cones of vision</term>.

***

# IN WHAT WAY THE EYE SEES OBJECTS PLACED IN FRONT OF IT.
<note>original Italian: "I CHE MODO L'OCCHIO VEDE LE COSE POSTELI DINĀZI"</note>

<margin>The perception of the object depends on the direction of the eye.</margin>

Let us assume that the sphere illustrated above is the eyeball, and let the small portion of the ball that is cut off by the line *s t*... <note>The text here refers to a diagram in Leonardo's original manuscript showing the geometry of the eye.</note>

31
51.]
LINEAR PERSPECTIVE.

Let *s t* be the pupil. All objects reflected on the center of the surface of the eye pass through the pupil at once and enter it, traveling through the crystalline humor, which does not obstruct the things revealed by light within the pupil. Once the pupil has received these objects by means of light, it immediately refers and transmits them to the intellect along the line *a b*. You should know that the pupil transmits nothing perfectly to the intellect or common sense unless the objects presented to it by light are directed along the line *a b*, just as you see with the line *c a*. Although the lines *m n* and *f g* are seen by the pupil, they are not fully considered because they are not aligned with the line *a b*. The proof is this: if the eye shown above wishes to count the letters placed before it, the eye must turn from letter to letter, because it could not discern them unless it aligned them with the line *a b*, just as the line *c a* does. All visible objects reach the eye along pyramidal lines, and the point of this pyramid ends and terminates in the center of the pupil, as illustrated above.

THE THEORY OF THE ART OF PAINTING.

# 52.

Experimental proof of the existence of the pyramid of sight (52-55).

Perspective is a rational demonstration by which experience confirms that all things transmit their likeness to the eye by means of pyramidal lines.

By pyramidal lines, I mean those which start from the edges of the surfaces of bodies and, by converging from a distance, are led to a single point; which point, in this case, I will show to be located in the eye, the universal judge of all bodies. By a point, I mean that which cannot be divided into any parts. Therefore, since this point is indivisible and located in the sight, no body would be seen by the eye that was not larger than that point. This being so, it is necessary that the lines coming from the body to the point be formed into a pyramid. And if anyone wished to prove that the visual faculty does not reside in that point, but rather in that black spot seen in the middle of the pupil, one could reply to them that a small thing could never diminish by any distance—such as a grain of millet or poppy seed or other similar thing—and that any object larger than the said point could never be seen in its entirety, as appears in the proof below. Let *a* be the visual faculty, and let *b* be the convergence

->[Diagram 1: A horizontal axis showing a circle m-n on the left, points e, d, c in the middle, and a pupil/eye on the right with points b and a.]<-

of the lines coming to the eye. Let *e* and *d* be the grains of millet within the said convergence; you see by reason that these would never diminish by distance, and the body *m n* could not be entirely understood by them. Therefore, it is necessary to confess that the eye has within itself a single indivisible point, *a*, to which all the tips of the pyramid converge, having started from the bodies, as appears

->[Diagram 2: A converging cone of lines from a large object e-f through circles d and c, through an aperture b, to a central point a within a larger circle representing the eye.]<-

here below. Let *a b* be the eye; let its center hold the aforementioned point. If the line *e f* has

--- Page 71 ---
To enter by likeness through such a small opening in the eye, one must confess that the smaller object cannot enter into what is smaller than itself unless it diminishes; and by diminishing, it must necessarily change into a pyramid.

---

# 53.

#### PERSPECTIVE.

Perspective provides assistance where judgment fails regarding objects that diminish. The eye can never be a true judge for determining with certainty how close one quantity is to another similar one, when the top of that other object is level with the eye of the person looking at those parts, except by means of the vertical plane, which is the master and guide of perspective.

Let *n* be the eye, and let *e f* be the aforementioned vertical plane. Let *a*, *b*, *c*, and *d* be the three divisions, one below the other. If the lines *a n* and *c n* are of equal length and the eye *n* is located in the center, then *a b* will appear the same as *b c*. *c d* is lower and farther away from *n*; therefore, it will appear smaller. This same effect appears in the three divisions of the face when the eye of the painter who is portraying it is at the same height as the eye of the person being portrayed.

---

# 54.

#### PROOF OF HOW OBJECTS REACH THE EYE.

If you look at the sun or another luminous object and then close your eye, you will see it similarly inside the eye for a long period of time. This is a sign that images enter within.

--- Page 72 ---

A. 36b]

### ELEMENTS OF PERSPECTIVE.

All objects transmit their image to the eye in the form of pyramids, and the closer to the eye these pyramids are intersected, the smaller the image of the objects that cause them will appear. Therefore, you should intersect the pyramid with a vertical plane that reaches the base of that pyramid, as is shown in the plane $a \cdot n$.

The eye $f$ and the eye $t$ are one and the same thing; however, the eye $f$ marks the distance—that is, how far you are standing to see the object—and the eye $t$ shows you the direction, which is whether you are in the center, to the side, or at an angle to the object you are looking at. And remember that the eye $f$ and the eye $t$ must always be positioned at the same height as one another; for example, if you lower or raise the eye of the distance point $f$, you must do the same with the eye of the direction point $t$. And if point $f$ shows how far the eye is distant from the square plane but does not show on which side...

If the eye $f$ sees a perfect square, each of whose sides is equal to the space between $s$ and $c$, and if at the beginning of the side facing the eye a pole or other straight object is established, standing fixed in a perpendicular line as shown at $r$ $s$: I say that if you look at the side of the square facing you, it will align with the base of the vertical plane $r$ $s$; and if you look at the second, opposite side, it will appear to rise to the height of the vertical plane at point $n$. Therefore, by this demonstration, you can understand that if the eye is higher than an infinite number of objects placed upon a level surface, one after another, the further they recede, the more they appear to rise until they reach the level of the eye's height, and no higher. This is because objects placed upon the level surface where you stand—provided it is flat, even if that surface were infinite—never rise higher than the eye, because the eye contains within itself that point toward which all the pyramids direct and join themselves, which carry the images of the objects to the eye. And this point always aligns with the point of diminution, which appears at the end of the things seen; and from the base of the first pyramid to the point of diminution, there are only bases without pyramids, which constantly diminish up to that point. And from the first base where the vertical plane is situated toward the point of the eye, there will be only pyramids without bases, as is shown.

...as shown in the example given above.
