And each point is the apex of a pyramid, all having a common base with the intervening vertical plane. But although their bases are equal, their angles are not equal, because the diminishing point is the termination of a smaller angle than that of the eye. If you ask me: "By what practical experience can you show me these points?" I reply—so far as concerns the diminishing point which moves with you—when you walk by a plowed field, look at the straight furrows whose ends meet the path where you are walking, and you will see that each pair of furrows will look as though they tried to get closer and meet at the farther end.

**How to measure the pyramid of vision.**
As regards the point in the eye, it is made more intelligible by this: If you look into the eye of another person, you will see your own image. Now imagine two lines starting from your ears and going to the ears of that image which you see in the other person's eye; you will understand that these lines converge in such a way that, if they continued a little way beyond your own image mirrored in the eye, they would meet in a point. And if you want to measure the diminution of the pyramid in the air which occupies the space between the object seen and the eye, you must do it according to the diagram illustrated below. ### 61.

All bodies together, and each one by itself, fill the surrounding air with an infinite number of their own images. These images are present everywhere in their entirety and yet are complete in every part, carrying with them the quality, color, and shape of the body that produced them.

It can be clearly demonstrated that bodies are present through their images throughout all the surrounding air, and are complete in every part in terms of substance, shape, and color. This is shown by the images of many different bodies which are all reproduced at a single small opening. There, by means of intersecting lines, they transmit the objects through opposing pyramids, appearing upside down on the first dark surface they encounter. The reason for this is—

***

### 62.

Every point is the starting point of an infinite number of lines which spread out to form a base; immediately from that base, those same lines converge back into a pyramid, preserving both the color and the form. As soon as a form is created or composed, it suddenly generates infinite angles and lines from itself. As these lines spread and intersect through the air, they result in an infinite number of angles opposite to one another. Given a base, each opposite angle will form a triangle that possesses a shape and proportion similar to the larger angle; and if the base fits twice into each of the two lines of the pyramid, the small triangle will maintain that same proportion.

<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s exploration of linear perspective and optics, specifically focusing on how light and images travel through the air. <margin>Ashburnham I. 27 II a]</margin>
### 63.

Every shadowed body <note>original Italian: "corpo ombroso"</note> fills the surrounding air with infinite images of itself. These images, spread through the air by infinite pyramids, represent that body as a whole in every part, and as a whole in each individual part. Every pyramid composed of a long convergence <note>original Italian: "concorso," referring to the meeting of light rays</note> of rays contains within itself infinite pyramids; each individual pyramid has the same power as the whole group, and the whole group has the power of each individual. The equidistant circuit of the pyramidal convergence will provide its object with an equal quality of angles, and the object will be received by the eye <note>original: "obietto"</note> in an equal size. 

The body of the air is full of infinite pyramids composed of radiating straight lines, which are caused by the edges of the surfaces of shadowed bodies placed in the air. The further they move from their source, the more acute <note>In this context, "acute" means the angle of the pyramid becomes sharper or narrower</note> they become. Although their paths intersect and interweave, they nevertheless do not confuse one another; with a spreading convergence, they continue to amplify and spread through all the surrounding air. They are of equal power among themselves—all as much as each, and each as much as all. Through these, the image of the body is carried as a whole everywhere and as a whole in every part; each pyramid by itself receives in every tiny part of its structure the entire form of its source.

Every body in light and shade fills the surrounding air with infinite images of itself; and these, by infinite pyramids diffused in the air, represent this body throughout space and on every side. Each pyramid that is composed of a long assemblage of rays includes within itself an infinite number of pyramids, and each has the same power as all, and all as each. A circle of equidistant pyramids of vision will give to their object angles of equal size; and an eye at each point will see the object as the same size. The body of the atmosphere is full of infinite pyramids composed of radiating straight lines, which are produced from the surface of the bodies in light and shade existing in the air; and the farther they are from the object which produces them, the more acute they become. Although in their distribution they intersect and cross, they never mingle together, but pass through all the surrounding air, independently converging, spreading, and diffused. And they are all of equal power [and value]; all equal to each, and each equal to all. By these, the images of objects are transmitted through all space and in every direction, and each pyramid, in itself, includes, in each minutest part, the whole form of the body causing it.

---

<margin>Codex Atlanticus 100 b; 313 a]</margin>
### 64.

The body of the air is full of infinite radiating pyramids caused by the objects placed within it. These pyramids, intersecting and interweaving without blocking <note>original Italian: "occupatione"</note> one another, spread through all the surrounding air with a divergent convergence; they are of equal power, and all...

The body of the atmosphere is full of infinite radiating pyramids produced by the objects existing in it. These intersect and cross each other with independent convergence without interfering with each other and pass through all the surrounding atmosphere; and...

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci describes his theory of "pyramids of light," explaining how images of objects travel through the air as converging and intersecting rays that remain distinct and carry the full form of the object to any point in space.</summary>

are of equal force and value—all being equal to each, and each to all. And by means of these, images of the body are transmitted everywhere and on all sides, and each receives in itself every minute portion of the object that produces it.

## 65.

### PERSPECTIVE.

The air is filled with endless images of the objects distributed within it; and all are represented in all, and all in one, and all in each. From this it happens that if two mirrors are placed in such a manner as to face each other exactly, the first will be reflected in the second and the second in the first. The first, being reflected in the second, carries to it the image of itself along with all the images represented within it, among which is the image of the second mirror; and so, image within image, they continue to infinity in such a manner that each mirror has within it a mirror, each smaller than the last and one inside the other. Thus, by this example, it is clearly proved that every object sends its image to every spot from which the object itself can be seen; and conversely, that the same object is capable of receiving within itself all the images of the objects that are in front of it. Consequently, the eye transmits through the atmosphere its own image to all the objects that are opposite to it and receives them into itself—that is to say, on its surface—from where the common sense takes them and considers them, and if they are pleasing, commits them to memory. Therefore, I am of the opinion that the spiritual power of the images in the eyes is directed toward the object, just as the images of the object are directed toward the eye. That the images of all objects are disseminated through the air is evidenced by the example of several mirrors placed in a circle, which will reflect each other endlessly. When an image has reached the other mirror, it is returned to the object that produced it, and from there—being diminished—it is returned again to the object and then comes back once more, and this happens endlessly. If you put a light at night between two flat mirrors with a distance of one braccio between them, you will see in each

--- Page 81 ---

| ...each of those mirrors, an infinite number of lights, one smaller than the other. If at night you place a light between the walls of a room, all parts of those walls will be tinted with the images <note>original: *similitudini*</note> of that light. Those parts will be seen by the light, and the light will likewise see them—that is to say, when there is no obstacle between them to interrupt the transmission <note>original: *concorso*</note> of the images. This same example is seen to a greater degree in the convergence of solar rays which, all together and each by itself, convey to the object the image of the body that causes it. That each body by itself alone fills the entire surrounding atmosphere with its images, and that this same air is capable, at the same time, of receiving the images of infinite other bodies that might be within it, is clearly demonstrated by these examples. Every object is visible throughout the entire atmosphere, and the whole is in every smallest part of it; all are in the whole, and all in each smallest part; each in the whole, and all in the part. | ...of them an infinite number of lights, one smaller than another, to the last. This same example is seen in a greater degree in the distribution of the solar rays which all together, and each by itself, convey to the object the image of the body which causes it. That each body by itself alone fills with its images the atmosphere around it, and that the same air is able, at the same time, to receive the images of the endless other objects which are in it, this is clearly proved by these examples. And every object is everywhere visible in the whole of the atmosphere, and the whole in every smallest part of it; and all the objects in the whole, and all in each smallest part; each in all and all in every part. |

### 68.

**That the contrary is impossible.**
It is impossible that the eye should project from itself, by visual rays, the visual virtue, because as soon as it opens, that first part which would begin the emission and would have to travel to the object could not do so without time. This being the case, it could not travel as high as the sun in a month's time when the eye wanted to see it. And if it did reach the sun, it would necessarily follow that it must be continuous throughout the entire path from the eye to the sun, and that it would always widen so that the space between the sun and the eye would form the base and the apex of a pyramid. This being the case, even if the eye consisted of a million worlds, it would not prevent it from being entirely consumed in this visual virtue. And if this virtue had to travel through the air as scent does, the winds would distort it.

--- Page 83 ---
...would bend it and carry it to another place. But we do, in fact, see the mass of the sun with the same speed as an object at the distance of a yard, and the power of sight is not disturbed by the blowing of the winds nor by any other accident.

### 69.

Just as a stone flung into the water becomes the center and cause of many circles, and as sound diffuses itself in circles in the air, so any object placed in the luminous atmosphere diffuses itself in circles and fills the surrounding air with infinite images of itself. And the whole is repeated everywhere, and the whole in every smallest part. This can be proved by experiment, since if you shut a window that faces west and make a hole...

### 70.

If the object in front of the eye sends its image to the eye, the eye, on the other hand, sends its image to the object; and no portion whatever of the object is lost in the images it throws off, for any reason either in the eye or the object. Therefore, we may rather believe it to be the nature and power of our luminous atmosphere—which absorbs the images of the objects existing in it—than the nature of the objects to send their images through the air. If the object opposite to the eye were to send its image to the eye, the eye would have to do the same to the object, which would imply that these images were spiritual emanations. But if this were so, it would be necessary to admit that every object would rapidly diminish, because every object appears by its images in the surrounding atmosphere. That is: the whole object in the whole atmosphere and in each part; and all the objects in the whole atmosphere and all of them in each part; speaking of that atmosphere which is able to receive within itself the straight and radiant lines of the images sent from the objects.

--- Page 84 ---
to contain in itself the straight and radiating lines of the images projected by the objects. From this, it seems necessary to admit that it is in the nature of the atmosphere—which exists between the objects and which attracts the images of things to itself like a magnet—to be positioned between them.

## PROOF OF HOW ALL OBJECTS PLACED IN ONE POSITION ARE PRESENT EVERYWHERE AND IN EVERY PART.

I say that if the front of a building—or any open square or field—which is illuminated by the sun has a dwelling opposite to it, and if you make a small round hole in the wall that does not face the sun, all the illuminated objects will project their images through that hole. They will be visible inside the dwelling on the opposite wall, which should be white; and there, in fact, they will appear upside down. If you make similar openings in several places in the same wall, you will have the same result from each. Hence, the images of the illuminated objects are present everywhere on this wall and in every smallest part of it. The reason, as we clearly know, is that this hole must admit some light into the dwelling, and the light it admits is derived from one or many luminous bodies. If these bodies are of various colors and shapes, the rays forming the images will be of various colors and shapes, and the representations on the wall will be the same.

### 75.

If the visual perception <note>original: *giuditio*; literally "judgment," referring here to the eye's ability to process visual information</note> of the eye is located within it, the straight lines of the images <note>original: *spetie*; a term Leonardo used to describe the visual forms or "species" of objects transmitted through light</note> <gloss>2</gloss> are refracted on its surface because they pass from a rarer medium to a denser one. <gloss>3</gloss> If you are underwater and look at an object in the air, you will see that object <gloss>4</gloss> outside of its true position; the same happens to an object underwater when viewed from the air.

<margin>British Museum, 171 b]</margin>

### 76.

<margin>The inversion of the images.</margin>
All the images <note>original: *similitudini*; likenesses or visual representations</note> of objects that pass through a window <gloss>2</gloss> from the open air into the air confined within walls are seen <gloss>3</gloss> on the opposite side. An object that moves in the open air <gloss>4</gloss> from east to west will appear, through its shadow on the walls <gloss>5</gloss> illuminated by this confined air, to move in the opposite direction.

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>LINEAR PERSPECTIVE.</header>

78.]

### ON THE MOVEMENT OF THE RIGHT, LEFT, UPPER, OR LOWER EDGE.
<note>original: DEL MOTO DEL LABRO DESTRO O SINISTRO, O SUPERIORE, O INFERIORE</note>

If you move the right side of the opening <note>original: *spiracolo*; an aperture or small hole</note>, then the left image <note>original: *impressione*</note> of the object on the right, which entered through that opening, will move. The same result will happen with all the other sides of such an opening. This is proved with the help of the second rule of this [treatise], which states: "All the rays which carry the images <note>original: *spetie*; the visual forms or 'species' of objects</note> of bodies through the air move in straight lines." Therefore, as the images of very large bodies must pass through very small holes, and beyond these holes recover their large size, the lines must necessarily intersect.

---

<margin>Windsor Library, 145; B. a]</margin>

# 78.

Necessity has provided that all the images of objects placed in front of the eye shall intersect in two places. One of these intersections is created in the pupil, and the other inside the crystalline lens <note>the lens of the eye</note>. If this were not the case, the eye could not see so great a number of objects as it does. 

This can be proved because all lines which intersect do so at a point. Since nothing is seen of objects except their surfaces—the edges of which are lines (following the converse of the definition of a surface)—and every minute part of a line is equal to a point (for "smallest" refers to that which nothing can be smaller than, and this definition is equivalent to the definition of a point), it is therefore possible for the entire circumference of a circle to transmit its image to its point of intersection. This is shown in the fourth rule of this [treatise], which states: "All the smallest parts of the images cross each other..."

---

<note>
Note on Italian transcription: 17. *chongiugnie chon* (joins with). 19. *desstro* (right). 20. *sinisstra* (left). 22. *2^adi* (second [rule]). 23. *recti* (straight). 24. *chorpi* (bodies). 78. 1. *antipossti* (placed before). 3. *popilla* (pupil). 4. *faciesse* (made/done). 13. *circūferentia* (circumference).
</note>

<vocab>spiracolo, intersegatione, popilla, spera cristallina, superfitie, linear perspective, rays, optics, Leonardo da Vinci</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains that light rays carrying visual images must intersect when passing through small apertures, and he applies this principle to the human eye, arguing that images must intersect in the pupil and the lens to allow for wide-field vision.</summary>
<keywords>optics, light rays, geometry of vision, human eye, pupil, crystalline lens, camera obscura, intersection of rays, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
All the smallest parts of the images penetrate one another without interfering with each other. These demonstrations are to illustrate the eye. No image, even of the smallest object, enters the eye without being turned upside down; but as it penetrates into the crystalline lens, it is once more reversed, and thus the image is restored to the same position within the eye as that of the object outside the eye.

---

# 79.

**W. L. 145; D. 6]**

## ON THE CENTRAL LINE OF THE EYE.

Only one line among the images that reach the visual faculty does not intersect itself; and this has no perceptible dimensions because it is a mathematical line which originates from a mathematical point, which has no dimensions.

According to my opponent, necessity requires that the central line of every image that enters through small and narrow openings into a dark chamber shall be turned upside down, together with the images of the bodies that surround it.

---

# 80.

**Wind. L. 145; C. 8]**

## ON WHETHER THE CENTRAL LINE OF THE IMAGE CAN INTERSECT ITSELF WITHIN THE OPENING OR NOT.

It is impossible for the line to intersect itself; that is, for the right side of one of its fronts to cross over to the left side of the opposite front, and likewise for its left side to become its right side. Because such...

--- Page 89 ---
<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>LINEAR PERSPECTIVE.</header>
<meta>Section ID: 81.]</meta>

An intersection demands two lines, one from each side; for there can be no motion from right to left or from left to right in itself without such extension and thickness as admit of such motion. And if there is extension, it is no longer a line but a surface, and we are investigating the properties of a line, and not of a surface. And as the line, having no center of thickness, cannot be divided, we must conclude that the line can have no sides to intersect each other. This is proved by the movement of the line a f to a b and of the line e b to e f, which are the sides of the surface a f e b. But if you move the line a b and the line e f, with the front ends a e, to the spot c, you will have moved the opposite ends f b towards each other at the point d. And from the two lines you will have drawn the straight line c d, which cuts the middle of the intersection of these two lines at the point n, without any intersection. For, if you imagine these two lines as having breadth, it is evident that by this motion the first will entirely cover the other—being equal with it—without any intersection, in the position c d. And this is sufficient to prove our proposition.

## 81.

## HOW THE INNUMERABLE RAYS FROM INNUMERABLE IMAGES CAN CONVERGE TO A POINT.

Just as all lines can meet at a point without interfering with each other—being without breadth or thickness—in the same way all the images of surfaces can meet there; and as each given point faces the object opposite to it, and each object faces an opposite point, the converging rays of the image can pass through the point and diverge again beyond it to reproduce and re-magnify the real size of that image. Just as all mathematical lines can meet at a single point without interfering with one another—since they have no width or thickness—in the same way, all the images of various surfaces can meet at that same point. The smaller the opening is compared to the shaded body, the less the images transmitted through that opening will overlap with one another.

The sides of images that pass through openings into a dark room intersect at a point that is closer to the opening in proportion to how narrow that opening is. To prove this, let $a$ $b$ be the shaded body which sends not its shadow, but the image of its dark form and shape through the opening $d$ $e$. This opening is as wide as the shaded body itself; and since its sides $a$ $b$ are straight lines (as has been proven), they must intersect between the shaded body and the opening. However, they will intersect closer to the opening the more that opening is narrower than the shaded body. This is demonstrated on your right and left sides in the two diagrams $a$ $b$ $c$ $n$ $m$ $o$. In the right-hand diagram, where the opening $d$ $e$ is equal in width to the shaded body $a$ $b$, the intersection of the sides of that shaded body occurs exactly halfway between the opening and the shaded body at point $c$. This cannot happen in the left-hand diagram because the opening $o$ is much smaller than the shaded body $n$ $m$.

### 82.

An experiment showing how, without moving the pupil from its position, the objects seen by it appear to move from their places.

If you look at an object some distance away from you that is lower than the eye, and fix both your pupils upon it, and with one of your hands you open and hold the upper lid firmly, and with the other you raise...

--- Page 93 ---
the lower lid—still keeping your eyes fixed on the object gazed at—you will see that object double; one image remaining steady, and the other moving in a direction opposite to the pressure of your finger on the lower eyelid. This proves how false the opinion is of those who say that this happens because the pupil of the eye is displaced from its position.

How the above-mentioned facts prove that the pupil acts upside down in the process of seeing.

### 83.

**THE PLANE OF GLASS.**

Perspective is nothing other than seeing a place or objects behind a plane of glass that is quite transparent, on the surface of which the objects behind that glass are to be drawn. These can be traced as pyramids to the point in the eye, and these pyramids are intersected on the glass plane.

### 84.

Pictorial perspective can never make an object at the same distance look the same size as it appears to the eye. You see that the apex of the pyramid $f c d$ is as far...

--- Page 94 ---
from the object $c$ $d$ as the same point $f$ is from the object $a$ $b$; and yet $c$ $d$, which is the base formed by the painter's point, is smaller than $a$ $b$, which is the base formed by the lines of the objects converging in the eye and refracted at $s$ $t$, the surface of the eye. This can be proven by experiment, using the lines of vision and then the lines of the painter's plumb line, by intersecting those real lines of vision on one and the same plane and measuring the same object on it.

---

## 85.

### PERSPECTIVE.

The vertical plane is a perpendicular line, imagined as being in front of the common point where the apexes of the pyramids converge. This plane performs the same function for that point as a flat pane of glass would, through which you might view various things and draw them upon it. The objects drawn would be smaller than the originals in proportion to how much the distance between the glass and the eye was smaller than the distance between the glass and the object.

### PERSPECTIVE.

The convergence of the pyramids produced by objects will show, on the plane, the variety of sizes and distances of the objects causing them.

### PERSPECTIVE.

Regarding all those horizontal planes whose edges are met by perpendicular lines forming right angles: if they are of equal width, it is necessary that the more they rise toward the level of the eye, the less of them is seen; and the more the eye is above them, the more their true width is seen.

### PERSPECTIVE.

The farther a spherical body is from the eye, the more of it you see.

---

## 86.

A simple and natural method; that is, how objects appear to the eye without any other medium.

--- Page 95 ---
87. 88.] LINEAR PERSPECTIVE. 55

The object that is nearer to the eye always appears larger than another of equal quality that is more distant.
The eye $m$, which sees the spaces $o \ v \ x$, recognizes almost no difference between one and the other, and this arises from being close to them; and if you transfer these spaces onto the vertical plane $n \ o$, the space $o \ v$ will appear in the part of the plane $o \ r$, and likewise the space $v \ x$ will appear in $r \ q$. And if you were to put this into practice in some place where one could walk around it, it would seem like a discordant thing because of the great variety between the space $o \ r$ and $r \ q$; and this results from the eye being so far below the plane that the plane is foreshortened to it. Therefore, if you still wished to put it into practice, it would be necessary for that perspective to be viewed from a single hole which was at the location $m$, or truly that you stood away at least 3 times the size of the thing you see. The plane $o \ p$, by being always equidistant to the eye in one way, will render things well and fit to be seen from place to place.

### 87.

How every large quantity sends forth its images, which have the potential to diminish infinitely.
The images of every large quantity, being infinitely divisible, are infinitely diminutive.

---

### 88.

Those bodies of equal size situated in various places will be seen through different pyramids, which will be so much narrower as their cause is farther away.

--- Page 96 ---
## 89.

**Opposite pyramids in juxtaposition.**

Perspective, in dealing with distances, makes use of two opposite pyramids, one of which has its apex in the eye and its base as distant as the horizon. The other has its base toward the eye and its apex on the horizon. Now, the first includes the visible universe, embracing the entire mass of objects that lie in front of the eye; just as a vast landscape might be seen through a very small opening; for the more remote the objects are from the eye, the greater the number of things that can be seen through that opening. Thus, the pyramid is constructed with its base on the horizon and its apex in the eye, as has been said. The second pyramid extends to a specific point which appears smaller in proportion as it is farther from the eye; and this second perspective pyramid results from the first.

---

## 90.

**On simple and complex perspective.**

### SIMPLE PERSPECTIVE.

Simple perspective is that which is constructed by art on a vertical plane which is equally distant from the eye in every part. Complex perspective is that which is constructed on a ground-plan in which none of the parts are equally distant from the eye.

---

## 91.

**The proper distance of objects from the eye.**

### PERSPECTIVE.

No surface will be seen exactly as it is if the eye observing it is not equally distant from all its edges.

---

## 92.

### WHY AN OBJECT PLACED CLOSE TO THE EYE LEAVES ITS EDGES INDISTINCT.

When any object opposite the eye is brought too close to it, its edges must necessarily become too blurred to be distinguished; just as happens with objects close to a light, which cast a large and indistinct shadow, so it is with the eye when it judges objects in front of it; in all cases of linear perspective, the eye functions in a similar way.

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Section 92 regarding the mechanics of vision and begins a series of axioms (93–95) on how distance affects the perceived size of objects.</meta>

...spective, the eye acts in the same way as light. The reason is that the eye possesses one "leading line" <note>original: *linia maestra*; the central axis of the visual cone</note> which expands with distance and embraces with true understanding large objects far away just as it does small ones close by. However, because the eye sends out a multitude of lines that surround this central one—and because those lines furthest from the center of this cone of vision are less capable of discerning the truth—it follows that an object placed too close to the eye is not at a sufficient distance for the central line to grasp its boundaries. Consequently, those boundaries fall within the lines of weaker perception. These are to the function of the eye like hunting dogs that can flush out the prey but cannot catch it; similarly, these peripheral lines cannot "capture" the object, but instead cause the central "leading line" to turn toward the things they have detected. Therefore, the objects whose boundaries are judged by these peripheral lines appear blurred.

---

### 93.

<margin>A. 8 b]</margin>

#### PERSPECTIVE.

<margin>The relative size of objects with regard to their distance from the eye (93–98).</margin>

A small object close by and a large one far away, if seen within equal visual angles, will appear to be of the same size.

#### PERSPECTIVE.

No object is so large that, at a great distance from the eye, it does not appear smaller than a much smaller object that is closer.

Among objects of equal size, the one that is further from the eye will appear to be a smaller shape.

98.

### PERSPECTIVE.

No second object can be so much lower than the first that it will not appear higher than the first to the eye, provided the eye is positioned above that second object.

### PERSPECTIVE.

And a second object will never be so much higher than the first that it will not appear lower than the first to the eye, provided the eye is positioned below them.

### PERSPECTIVE.

If the eye views a second square through the center of a smaller one that is closer, the second, larger square will appear to be enclosed by the smaller one.

### PERSPECTIVE—PROPOSITION.

Secondary objects can never be so large that the primary, smaller objects in front of them will not hide or surround them.

--- Page 99 ---
#### DEFINITION.
This proposition is proved by experiment. For if you look through a small hole, there is nothing so large that it cannot be seen through it, and the object seen will appear surrounded and enclosed by the outer edges of that hole. And if you block it, that small obstruction will be what blocks the view of the large object.

#### DEFINITION.
This proposition can be proved by experiment. For if you look through a small hole there is nothing so large that it cannot be seen through it and the object so seen appears surrounded and enclosed by the outline of the sides of the hole. And if you stop it up, this small stopping will conceal the view of the largest object.

---

### OF LINEAR PERSPECTIVE.
Linear perspective operates through the function of visual lines to prove by measurement how much smaller a second object is than the first, and how much a third is smaller than the second, and so on by degrees until the limit of visible things. I find by experience that if a second object is as far distant from the first as the first is distant from your eye, then—even though they are of equal size—the second will be exactly half as small as the first. And if a third object, equal in size to the second and third before it, is as far from the second as the second is from the third, it will be half the size of the second; and so on, degree by degree, at equal distances, they will always diminish by half from the previous one, provided that the interval does not exceed the distance of 20 braccia. Within those 20 braccia, a figure similar to yourself will lose 2/4 of its size; at 40 braccia it will lose 9/10; and then 19/20 at 60 braccia; and so it will continue its diminution step by step, provided the picture plane is distant from you by twice your height. For having it at only one height makes a great difference between the first braccia and the second.

### OF LINEAR PERSPECTIVE.
Linear Perspective deals with the action of the lines of sight, in proving by measurement how much smaller is a second object than the first, and how much the third is smaller than the second; and so on by degrees to the end of things visible. I find by experience that if a second object is as far beyond the first as the first is from the eye, although they are of the same size, the second will seem half the size of the first and if the third object is of the same size as the 2nd, and the 3rd is as far beyond the second as the 2nd from the first, it will appear of half the size of the second; and so on by degrees, at equal distances, the next farthest will be half the size of the former object. So long as the space does not exceed the length of 20 braccia. But, beyond 20 braccia figures of equal size will lose 2/4 and at 40 braccia they will lose 9/10, and 19/20 at 60 braccia, and so on diminishing by degrees. This is if the picture plane is distant from you twice your own height. If it is only as far off as your own height, there will be a great difference between the first braccia and the second.

### 100.

#### OF THE DIMINUTION OF OBJECTS AT VARIOUS DISTANCES.

A second object as far distant from the first as the first is from the eye will appear half the size of the first, even though they are actually the same size.

#### OF THE DEGREES OF DIMINUTION.

If you place the vertical plane one braccio from the eye, the first object, located at a distance of 4 braccia from your eye, will diminish to 3/4 of its height on that plane; if it is 8 braccia from the eye, it will diminish to 7/8; and if it is 16 braccia away, it will diminish to 15/16 of its height. Thus, as the distance doubles step by step, the diminution will also double.

---

### 101.

Begin first from the line *m f* with the eye positioned below; then move up and do the same with the line *n f*. Next, with the eye positioned above and close to the two gauges on the ground, look at *m n*; then, as the ratio of *c m* is to *m n*, so will *n m* be to *n s*. If *a n* fits three times into *f b*, *m p* will do the same into *p g*. Then move backward until *c d* fits twice into *a n*, and *p g* will then be equal to *g h*. Finally, *m p* will fit into *h p* as many times as *d c* fits into *o p*.

---

### 102.

I PROVIDE THE DEGREES OF OBJECTS SEEN BY THE EYE JUST AS THE MUSICIAN PROVIDES THE NOTES HEARD BY THE EAR.

Although objects seen by the eye do, in fact, touch one another as they recede, I will nevertheless base my rule on intervals of 20 braccia.

### 106.

| Between two similar and equal objects placed one behind the other at a set distance, there will appear to be a greater difference in their sizes when they are closer to the eye. | If two similar and equal objects are placed one beyond the other at a given distance, the difference in their size will appear greater in proportion as they are closer to the eye. |

<summary>Leonardo explains that visual diminution follows strict geometric proportions similar to musical scales, where the perceived size of equal objects changes more drastically when they are close to the observer than when they are far away.</summary>
<keywords>linear perspective, diminution, proportions, music analogy, geometry of vision, braccia, vertical plane</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
to the eye that sees them. And conversely, there will be less variety in their size the further they are from the said eye.

This is proved by the proportions of their distances among themselves; for, if the distance from the eye to the first object is the same as the distance from the first to the second, this is called a proportion. For example, if the first is one braccio away from the eye and the second is two braccia away, two is double one, and for this reason the first object will appear twice as large as the second. And if you place the first a hundred braccia from you and the second a hundred and one braccia, you will find the first to be larger than the second only by the same ratio that one hundred is less than one hundred and one; and the same applies conversely. Furthermore, the same thing is proved by the fourth rule of this book, which says that for equal objects, the proportion of their sizes is the same as the proportion of their distances from the eye that sees them.

---

### 107.

## OF EQUAL OBJECTS, THE MOST REMOTE LOOK THE SMALLEST.

The practice of perspective is divided into . . . parts, the first of which depicts all things seen by the eye at any distance; and this part shows all those things just as the eye sees them diminished. A person is not required to stand in one specific spot rather than another, provided that the wall does not foreshorten the image a second time.

But the second practice is a mixture of perspective created partly by art and partly by nature; and the work created by its rules has no—

--- Page 103 ---
...part of it that is not a mixture of natural perspective and artificial perspective. By natural perspective, I mean that the plane on which this perspective is represented is a flat surface; and this plane, although it is parallel in both length and height, is forced to diminish in its remote parts more than in its nearer parts. This is proved by the first rule above, and its diminution is natural. But artificial perspective—that is, the kind created by art—does the opposite within itself; for on the foreshortened plane, objects that are equal in size increase more as the eye is more natural and closer to the plane, and as the part of that plane where the image is depicted is farther from the eye.

And let this plane be *d e*, on which are depicted three equal circles that are behind this plane *d e*, namely the circles *a b c*. Now you see that the eye *h* sees on the vertical plane the sections of the images as larger at the greater distances and smaller at the closer ones.

Here follows what is missing in the margin at the bottom of the back of this page. Natural perspective acts in the opposite way, because at greater distances the object seen appears smaller, and at a smaller distance the object appears larger. But this invention requires the spectator to stand with his eye at a...

--- Page 104 ---
...small hole, and then, from that small hole, it will appear correctly. But because many eyes happen to see at the same time one and the same work produced with such art, and only one of them sees the effect of such perspective correctly while all the others remain confused, one must therefore avoid such complex perspective and hold to simple perspective. Simple perspective does not wish to see a surface in foreshortening, but in as proper a form as possible. Regarding this simple perspective—in which the plane intersects the pyramids that carry the images to the eye at an equal distance from the visual faculty—our experience of it is provided by the curved surface of the eye, upon which such pyramids are intersected at an equal distance from the visual faculty, etc.

### ON THE MIXTURE OF NATURAL PERSPECTIVE WITH ARTIFICIAL PERSPECTIVE.

This demonstration distinguishes natural perspective from artificial perspective; but before I proceed any further, I will define what is natural and what is artificial. Natural perspective states that of objects of equal size, the most remote appears smaller, and conversely, the nearest appears larger; and the proportion of diminution to diminution is the same as the proportion of distance to distance. But artificial perspective places unequal objects at various distances, keeping the smaller one closer to the eye than the larger one, with such a distance that the larger one appears to be smaller than all the others. The cause of this is the wall upon which such a demonstration is depicted, which is at an unequal distance from the eye at every point of its length.

--- Page 105 ---
The diminution of the plane is natural, but the perspective shown upon it is artificial, since it nowhere agrees with the true diminution of the said plane. It follows from this that when the eye is moved somewhat away from the station point of the perspective it has been viewing, all the represented objects look monstrous; this does not occur in natural perspective, which was defined above. Let us say, then, that the square *a b c d* illustrated above is foreshortened, being seen by the eye situated in the center of the side facing it. However, a mixture of artificial and natural perspective will be seen in this tetragon called *el main*—that is to say, *e f g h*—which must appear to the spectator’s eye to be equal to *a b c d*, provided the eye remains in its first position between *c* and *d*. This will be seen to produce a good effect, because the natural perspective of the plane will conceal the defects that would otherwise seem monstrous.

--- Page 106 ---

## III.

### *Six books on Light and Shade.*

*Linear Perspective* cannot be immediately followed by either the "prospettiva de' perdimenti," the "prospettiva de' colori," or *aerial perspective*; since these branches of the subject presuppose a knowledge of the principles of *Light and Shade*. No apology, therefore, is needed here for placing these immediately after *Linear Perspective*.

We have various plans suggested by *Leonardo da Vinci* for the arrangement of the mass of materials treating of this subject. Among these, I have given preference to a scheme proposed in No. III because, in all probability, we have here the expression of a final and definite purpose. Several authors have expressed the opinion that the *Paris Manuscript C* is a complete and finished treatise on *Light and Shade*. Certainly, the *Principles of Light and Shade* form by far the larger portion of this manuscript, which consists of two separate parts; still, the materials are far from being finally arranged. It is also evident that he investigates the subject here from the point of view of the *Physicist* rather than from that of the *Painter*.

The plan of an arrangement scheme suggested in No. III and adopted by me has been strictly followed for the first four Books. For the last three, however, few materials have survived; and it must be admitted that these three Books would find a far more appropriate place in a work on Physics than in a treatise on *Painting*. For this reason, I have collected all the chapters on *Reflections* in Book V, and in Book VI, I have gathered and arranged all the sections of MS. C that belong to the book on Painting, so far as they relate to *Light and Shade*, while the sections of the same manuscript which treat of the "Prospettiva de' perdimenti" have, of course, been excluded from the series on *Light and Shade*.

### 110.

<margin>Introductory remarks <note>original: "Prolegomena"</note></margin>

| You must describe the theory and then the practice. You will first describe the shadow and light of dense bodies, and then of transparent bodies. <note>original: "Bisognia ti descrivere la teorica e poi la pratica; discriuerai primo de ōbra e lumi de' corpi densi e poi de' corpi trasparenti."</note> | You must first explain the theory and then the practice. 70 [112.]
THE THEORY OF THE ART OF PAINTING.

Upon this proposition I build the first Book. Besides this, shadows possess various degrees of darkness within themselves because they are caused by the absence of varying amounts of luminous rays; these I call Primary Shadows because they are the first shadows and are inseparable from the bodies to which they are attached. Upon this I will found my second Book. From these primary shadows, shaded rays result which spread through the air; these vary in quality as much as the varieties of the primary shadows from which they derive. For this reason, I call these Derived Shadows because they are produced by other shadows, and I will create the third Book on this subject. Furthermore, where these derived shadows strike surfaces, they produce as many different effects as there are varieties in the places where they strike; here I will create the fourth Book. And because the point where a derived shadow strikes is always surrounded by the impact of luminous rays—which, through reflected convergence, bounce back toward their source, meet the original shadow, and mingle with it, somewhat altering its nature—I will build my fifth Book upon this. Beyond this, I will create the sixth Book, which will contain the many and various diversities of the resulting reflected rays; these will alter the original shadow with as many different colors as there are varieties in the places from which those reflected luminous rays derive. Additionally, I will make the seventh division concerning the various distances that may exist between the impact of a reflected ray and the place where it originates, and how the likenesses of the colors it attaches to an opaque body upon impact will vary.

112.

Different principles and plans of treatment (112—116).

First, you shall treat of the light coming through windows, which you shall name Restricted Light; then you shall treat of light in the open countryside, which you shall name Free Light; finally, you shall treat of the light of luminous bodies.

ON PAINTING.

The conditions of shadow and light as perceived by the eye are three. Of these, the first occurs when the eye and the light are on the same side of the object being viewed. The second occurs when the eye is in front of the object and the light is behind it. The third occurs when the eye is in front of the object and the light is to one side, in such a way that a line drawn from the object to the eye and a line drawn from the object to the light form a right angle where they meet.

ON PAINTING.

### 117.

**OF PAINTING.**

**OF THE THREE KINDS OF LIGHT THAT ILLUMINATE OPAQUE BODIES.**

The first kind of light which may illuminate opaque bodies is called Direct light—such as that of the sun or any other light from a window or flame. The second is Diffused light, such as we see in cloudy weather or in mist and the like. The 3rd is Subdued light, which occurs when the sun is entirely below the horizon, either in the evening or morning.

---

### 118.

**OF LIGHT.**

The lights which may illuminate opaque bodies are of 4 kinds. These are: diffused light, such as that of the atmosphere within our horizon; and Direct light, such as that of the sun, or of a window or door or other opening. The third is Reflected light; and there is a 4th, which is the light that passes through semi-transparent bodies, such as linen or paper or the like, but not transparent like glass, or crystal, or other diaphanous bodies, which produce the same effect as though nothing intervened between the shaded object and the light that falls upon it; and we will discuss these fully in our discourse.

---

### 119.

**WHAT LIGHT AND SHADOW ARE.**

Shadow is the absence of light, merely the obstruction of the luminous rays by an opaque body. Shadow is of the nature of darkness. Light on an object is of the nature of a luminous body; one conceals and the other reveals. They are always associated and inseparable from all objects. But shadow is of greater power than light, for it can impede and entirely deprive objects of light, while light can never entirely expel shadow from bodies—that is, from opaque bodies.

--- Page 113 ---
Shadow is a more powerful agent than light, for it can impede and entirely deprive bodies of their light, while light can never entirely expel shadow from a body—that is, from an opaque body.

### 120.

Shadow is the reduction of light by the intervention of an opaque body. Shadow is the counterpart of the luminous rays which are cut off by an opaque body.

This is proved because the shadow cast is the same in shape and size as the luminous rays were which are transformed into a shadow.

---

### 121.

Shadow is the reduction of both light and darkness, and stands between darkness and light. A shadow may be infinitely dark, and also have infinite degrees of the absence of darkness. The beginnings and ends of shadow lie between the light and darkness, and may be infinitely diminished or infinitely increased. Shadow is the means by which bodies display their form. The forms of bodies could not be understood in detail without shadow.

---

### 122.

**OF THE NATURE OF SHADOW.**

Shadow partakes of the nature of universal matters. All such matters are more powerful at their beginning and grow weaker toward the end; I mean at the beginning of every form and condition, whether visible or invisible, and not of things that grow from a small beginning to a great size over time, such as a great oak which has a feeble beginning from a small acorn. Yet I may say that the oak is most powerful at its birth from the earth, which is where it is largest. Darkness, then, is the strongest degree of shadow, and light is its least. Therefore, O Painter, make your shadow darkest close to the object that causes it, and make the end of it fade into light, appearing to have no end.

C. 14 b]
On the various kinds of shadows (123-125).

# 123.

Darkness is the absence of light <note>original: *privatio di luce*</note>. Shadow is the reduction of light <note>original: *diminutio di luce*</note>. 

*   <term>Primitive shadow</term> <note>original: *ombra primitiva*</note> is that which is attached to the body that is in the shade. 
*   <term>Derivative shadow</term> <note>original: *ombra dirivativa*</note> is that which detaches from the body in shadow and travels through the air. 
*   <term>Reflected shadow</term> <note>original: *ombra ripercossa*</note> is that which is surrounded by an illuminated surface <note>original: *parite* - wall or surface</note>. 
*   A <term>simple shadow</term> <note>original: *l'ombra senplice*</note> is one that does not see any part of the light source that causes it. 

A simple shadow begins at the line that originates from the edges of the light-emitting bodies $a$ and $b$.

---

<margin>Br. M. 248 b]</margin>

# 124.

A <term>simple shadow</term> is one where no light source at all can be seen. A <term>compound shadow</term> <note>original: *ombra composta*</note> is one that is partially illuminated by one or more light sources.

---

<margin>Ash. I. 13 b]</margin>

# 125.

### WHAT IS THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN A SHADOW ATTACHED TO A BODY AND A SEPARATE SHADOW?

An <term>attached shadow</term> <note>original: *ombra congiunta*</note> is one that is never separated from the illuminated body. For example, consider a ball: as long as it stays in the light, it will always have one part covered by shadow. This shadow never leaves the ball, regardless of how the ball is moved or repositioned. 

A <term>separate shadow</term> <note>original: *ombra separata*</note>—often called a cast shadow—may or may not be created by the body. Suppose this ball is one cubit <note>original: *braccio*, a Renaissance unit of measurement approximately 23 inches</note> away from a wall, with a light on the opposite side. That light will cast onto the wall a shadow exactly as wide as the shadow found on the side of the ball facing the wall. 

A portion of the separate shadow will become invisible when the light is placed below the ball; in this case, the shadow is thrown upward toward the sky. Because it finds no surface or "resistance" in its path, the shadow is lost.

->Separate and attached shadow <note>original: *ombra separata e congiunta*</note><-

->Invisible separate shadow <note>original: *ombra separata inevidente*</note><-

# 126.

<margin>British Museum 171 a]</margin>
### HOW THERE ARE TWO KINDS OF LIGHT: ONE SEPARATE FROM AND THE OTHER ATTACHED TO BODIES.

Separate light is that which illuminates the body. Attached light is that part of the body which is illuminated by that very light. One kind of light is called primary <note>original: *primitivo*</note> and the other derived <note>original: *dirivativo*</note>. Similarly, there are two natures of shadows: one is the primary shadow and the other the derived shadow. The primary shadow is that which is attached to bodies; the derived shadow is that which is separated from bodies, carrying the shape of its cause to the surfaces of walls.

<margin>Of the various kinds of light (126. 127).</margin>
### HOW THERE ARE 2 KINDS OF LIGHT, ONE SEPARABLE FROM, AND THE OTHER INSEPARABLE FROM BODIES.

Separate light is that which falls upon the body. Inseparable light is the side of the body that is illuminated by that light. One is called primary, the other derived. And, in the same way, there are two kinds of shadow: one primary and the other derived. The primary is that which is inseparable from the body; the derived is that which proceeds from the body, conveying to the surface of the wall the form of the body causing it.

# 129.

## MEMORANDUM OF THE THINGS WHICH I REQUIRE TO BE GRANTED TO ME AS AXIOMS IN THE PROOFS OF THIS, MY PERSPECTIVE.

I ask to have it granted to me to assert that every ray, passing through air of uniform density, travels in a straight line from its source to the object or point of impact.

--- Page 117 ---
130.

W. L. 146a]

FIRST BOOK ON LIGHT AND SHADE.

The reason why a light possesses a single center is this: we clearly perceive that a large light often surpasses a small object in size; nevertheless, even though the light's rays surround the object by much more than half its circumference, the shadow always appears on the nearest surface and is always visible.

Let *c f* be the large light, and let *n* be the object placed opposite it which generates the shadow on the wall, and let *a b* be the wall. It clearly appears that the large light as a whole would not project the shadow *n* onto the wall; but because the light contains a center within itself, I prove by experiment that the shadow is projected to the wall, as illustrated by *m o t r*.

Why, to two eyes or in front of two eyes, do three objects presented to you appear as two?

Why, when aligning a straight line with two sights, does the first appear deceptive? I say that...

--- Page 118 ---
appears confused. I say that the eye projects an infinite number of lines which mingle or join those reaching it from the object being viewed. Only the central and sensory line is capable of discerning and judging objects and colors; all the others are false and deceptive. If you place two objects at a distance of one cubit from each other, and the first is close to the eye, the surface of the first will appear much more confused than the second. The reason is that the first is overwhelmed by a greater number of false lines than the second, and therefore its appearance is more uncertain.

Light acts in the same manner, for in the behavior of its rays—and especially in works of perspective—it is very similar to the eye. Its center carries the truth in the demonstration of shadows. When an object placed before it is too quickly overwhelmed by weak rays, it will cast a large, disproportionate, and poorly defined shadow. However, when the object that is to generate the shadow intercepts the light rays and is close to the surface where they strike, then the shadow becomes distinct, especially when the light source is far away; this is because the central ray, over a long distance, is less accompanied by false rays. Because the lines of the eye, solar rays, and other luminous lines travel through the air, they must follow a perfectly straight path. Unless they are obstructed by denser or thinner air, they would remain bent in some part; but if the air is clear of thickness or moisture, they will preserve their straight nature, always carrying back to their point of origin the cause of their interruption. If this origin is the eye, the interruption will be judged by color as well as by shape or size. But if the wall of the said interruption has a small hole in it which leads into a dark dwelling—dark not by paint but by the absence of light—you will see the lines entering through this hole, carrying to the second wall the entire form of their source, both in color and in shape.

--- Page 119 ---
...shape. But everything will be upside down. However, when the distance from the aperture to the final impact of the lines is equal to the distance from their origin, the impact will be equal in size to the reconstruction of the lines, intersecting and generating two pyramids with their points together and their bases opposite. Let $a$ $b$ be the origin of the lines; let $d$ $e$ be the first wall; let $c$ be the aperture where the intersection of the lines occurs; let $f$ $g$ be the final wall. You will find that $a$ at the final wall of impact remains below in the position of $g$, and $b$ from below rises up to the position of $f$. It will appear clear to experimenters that every luminous body has within itself a hidden power which is the center, from which and to which all the lines generated by the luminous surface travel; and from there they return or leap outward, and if they have no obstruction, they will spread through the air.

# 131.

## THE RAYS OF SHADOW AND LIGHT ARE OF GREATER POWER AND STRENGTH AT THEIR POINTS THAN AT THEIR SIDES.

Although the points of the luminous pyramids extend into shaded places and those of the shaded pyramids travel into illuminated places, and though among the luminous ones one may arise from a larger base than another, nevertheless, if by reason of various lengths these luminous pyramids reach equal thicknesses of angles, they will be of equal light among themselves. The shaded pyramids will do the same, as is demonstrated in the intersected pyramids $a$ $b$ $c$ and likewise $d$ $e$ $f$, which, although they arise from various sizes of bases, are nonetheless similar in size and in light.

The difference between light and lustre (132—135).

### 132.

What the difference is between light and lustre, and how lustre is not included among colors, but is a saturation of whiteness and originates on the surfaces of wet bodies; light partakes of the color of the object where it originates, such as gold or silver or similar things.

Of the difference between light and lustre; and that lustre is not included among colors, but is a saturation of whiteness, and derived from the surface of wet bodies; light partakes of the color of the object which reflects it (to the eye) as gold or silver or the like.

### 133.

#### OF THE HIGHEST LIGHTS WHICH TURN AND SHIFT ACCORDING TO THE SHIFTING OF THE EYE VIEWING THAT BODY.

#### OF THE HIGHEST LIGHTS WHICH TURN AND MOVE AS THE EYE MOVES WHICH SEES THE OBJECT.

Suppose that the mentioned body is this round object illustrated here, and that the light is at point *a*, and that the illuminated part of the body is *b c*, and that the eye is at point *d*: I say that because lustre is everywhere and complete in every part, if one stands at point *d*, the lustre will appear at point *c*; and as much as the eye moves from *d* to *a*, the lustre will move from *c* to *n*.

Suppose the body to be the round object figured here and let the light be at the point *a*, and let the illuminated side of the object be *b c* and the eye at the point *d*: I say that, as lustre is everywhere and complete in each part, if you stand at the point *d* the lustre will appear at *c*, and in proportion as the eye moves from *d* to *a*, the lustre will move from *c* to *n*.

### 134.

#### OF PAINTING.

#### OF PAINTING.

The light of lights—that is, the lustre of any object—will not be situated in the middle of the illuminated part, but rather will make as many shifts as the eye watching it makes.

High light or lustre on any object is not situated [necessarily] in the middle of an illuminated object, but moves as and where the eye moves in looking at it.

---

### 135.

#### OF LIGHT AND LUSTRE.

#### OF LIGHT AND LUSTRE.

What is the difference between light and the lustre that appears on the polished surface of opaque bodies?

The lights that are produced on the polished surfaces of opaque bodies will be stationary on stationary bodies, even if the eye of their viewer moves; but the lustres will be upon those same bodies in as many places on their surface as there are positions to which the eye moves.

What is the difference between light and the lustre which is seen on the polished surface of opaque bodies?

The lights which are produced from the polished surface of opaque bodies will be stationary on stationary objects even if the eye on which they strike moves. But reflected lights will, on those same objects, appear in as many different places on the surface as different positions are taken by the eye.

--- Page 121 ---
### WHICH BODIES HAVE LIGHT UPON THEM WITHOUT LUSTRE?

Opaque bodies that have a dense and rough surface will never generate lustre in any part of their illuminated side.

---

### WHICH BODIES WILL HAVE LUSTRE BUT NO APPARENT ILLUMINATED PART?

Opaque, dense bodies with a smooth surface are those that have all their lustre in as many places on the illuminated side as there are positions that can receive the angle of incidence of the light and the eye. However, because such a surface mirrors all the surrounding objects, the illuminated area is not recognizable as such on that part of the body.

---

Every center of shadow and light joined to shaded bodies is directed toward the center of its primary light. All light and shadows are cast in pyramidal lines. It is necessary that the center of each shadow corresponds to the center of its light along a straight line that passes through the center of that body. The center of the light shall be *a*, and that of the shadow shall be *b*. [Furthermore, in shaded bodies surrounded by shadow and light, the center of each must be directed toward the center of that body, and there must be a straight line from one center to the other passing through the body's center.]

**The relations of luminous to illuminated bodies.**

### PROOF OF HOW EVERY PART OF LIGHT CREATES A SINGLE POINT.

**Experiments on the relation of light and shadow within a room.**

Although the balls *a*, *b*, and *c* receive light from one window, nevertheless, if you follow the lines of their shadows, you will see them intersect and form a point at angle *n*.

### 138.

Every shadow cast by a body has a central line directed to a single point produced by the intersection of luminous lines in the middle of the opening and thickness of the window. The proposition stated above is plainly seen by experiment. Thus, if you draw a place with a window looking northwards, and let this be $s$ $f$, you will see a line starting from the horizon to the east, which, touching the two angles of the window $o$ $f$, reaches $d$; and from the horizon on the west another line, touching the other two angles $r$ $s$, and ending at $c$; and their intersection falls exactly in the middle of the opening and thickness of the window. Again, you can still better confirm this proof by placing two sticks, as shown at $g$ $h$; and you will see the line drawn from the center of the shadow directed to the center $m$ and prolonged to the horizon $n$ $f$.

--- Page 123 ---
### 139.

Every shadow, with all its variations, which increases in width more than its source as it moves further away, has its exterior lines joining together between the light and the shaded body. This proposition clearly appears and is confirmed by experience; for if a. b. is a window without any partition, the luminous air that is on the right at a. is seen on the left at d., and the air that is on the left illuminates on the right at point c., and these lines intersect at point m.

### 141.

<margin>Light and shadow in relation to the position of the eye (141-145).</margin>

<summary>This page explores how the positioning of the eye relative to a light source and an object changes the perception of shadows and highlights. Leonardo demonstrates that an object appears entirely dark if it blocks the light source from the eye, and entirely lit if the eye is between the light and the object.</summary>

85
145. 146.] FIRST BOOK ON LIGHT AND SHADE.

...surrounding the body in shadow, one will see that the deepest shadows on those bodies will meet the eye at equal angles—that is, at the same angle as that of the visual incidence.

---

### ON THE DISTINCTION OF SHADOWS IN VARIOUS LOCATIONS AND OF THE OBJECTS PLACED WITHIN THEM.

If the sun is in the east and you look toward the west, you will see all illuminated things entirely devoid of shadow, because you see what the sun sees; and if you look toward the south or north, you will see all bodies surrounded by shadow and light, because you see both what the sun does not see and what it does see; and if you look toward the path of the sun, all bodies will show you their shaded side, because that side cannot be seen by the sun.

---

### 146.
### The law of the incidence of light.

The edges of a window that are illuminated by two different lights of equal brightness will not cast light of equal quality into the dwelling within.

If *b* is a candle and *a c* is our hemisphere, both illuminate the edges of the window *m n*; but the light *b* only illuminates as far as *f g*, while the hemisphere *a c* will illuminate as far as *d e*.

Ash. I. 22 a]
148.

THAT PORTION OF A SHADED BODY WILL BE LEAST LUMINOUS WHICH IS SEEN UNDER THE LEAST AMOUNT OF LIGHT.

The part of the body marked *m* represents the first degree of light because it sees the entire window *a d* along the line *a f*. The part *n* is the second degree because it sees the light *b d* along the line *b e*. The part *o* is the third degree because it sees the light *c d* along the line *c h*. The part *p* is the second to last degree because it sees *c d* along the line *d v*. The part *q* is the final degree because it sees no part of the window.

Gradations of strength in the shadows (148. 149).

That part of the object marked *m* is in the highest light because it faces the window *a d* along the line *a f*; *n* is in the second grade because the light *b d* strikes it along the line *b e*; *o* is in the third grade, as the light falls on it from *c d* along the line *c h*; *p* is the lowest light but one as *c d* falls on it along the line *d v*; *q* is the deepest shadow for no light falls on it from any part of the window.

In the same proportion that *c d* is contained within *a d*, *n r s* will be darker than *m*, and all the rest is space without shadow.

Every light that falls upon shaded bodies at equal angles holds the first degree of brightness. That part will be darker which receives the light at less equal angles; and both the light and the shadows perform their functions by means of pyramids. The angle *c* holds the first degree of brightness.

### 150.

That body appears most in shadow when it is illuminated by a smaller light source, and that light source will illuminate a smaller part of the shaded body the closer it is to it; conversely, it will illuminate a much greater quantity the farther away it is.

A light source that is smaller than the shaded body will illuminate a smaller quantity of it the closer it is to it, and will do the opposite when it is more remote. But when the light source is larger than the shaded body, it will then see more of the shaded body the closer it is, and will do the opposite when it is more remote.

--- Page 131 ---
### 151.

That part of an illuminated object will be the brightest which is nearest to the source of its light.

### 152.

That part of the primary shadow will be the least dark which is farthest from its edges.

The derived shadow that borders the primary shadow will be darker than that primary shadow.

### 153.

That part of an opaque body will be more shaded or more illuminated which is nearer to the dark body that obscures it or the luminous body that illuminates it.

Objects seen between light and shadow will appear in greater relief than those which are entirely in light or in shadow.

### 154.

OF PERSPECTIVE.

The shaded and illuminated parts of opaque bodies will be in the same proportion of brightness and darkness as those of the objects facing them.

### 155.

OF PAINTING.

The boundaries and shapes of any part of shaded bodies are difficult to distinguish in their shadows and in their highlights; but in the parts positioned between the lights and the shadows, the parts of these bodies are perceived with the highest degree of clarity.

### 156.

PAINTING.

Among bodies of various darkness, deprived of the same light, such proportion will be...

157.

A specific light source creates better relief in shaded bodies than a universal light; this is demonstrated by comparing a part of a landscape illuminated by the sun with one shaded by clouds, which is illuminated only by the universal light of the atmosphere.

158. The theory of the **derived shadow** <note>original: "ombra dirivativa"</note>—a technical expression for which there is no precise English equivalent—is elaborately treated by Leonardo. But both text and diagrams (as seen in Plate IV, 1—3 and Plate V) must at once convince the student that the distinction he makes between **primary shadow** <note>original: "ombra primitiva"</note> and derived shadow is not merely justifiable but scientific. "Derived shadow" is by no means a mere abstract idea. This is easily proved by repeating the experiment made by Leonardo, and by filling with smoke the room in which the existence of the derived shadow is investigated; in such conditions, the volume of the shadow becomes visible. Nor is it difficult to perceive how much of Leonardo’s teaching depended on this theory. The recognized but extremely complicated science of cast shadows—<note>original: "percussione dell’ ombre dirivative"</note> or "the impact of derived shadows" as Leonardo calls them—is thus rendered more intelligible, if not actually simpler. We must assume this theory as our chief guide through the investigations which follow.

<summary>In this section, Leonardo defines "primary shadow" (the shadow on an object) and "derived shadow" (the shadow cast into space), explaining them as modifications of light and darkness.</summary>
<keywords>light and shade, primary shadow, derived shadow, ombra dirivativa, optics, darkness, Leonardo da Vinci, light theory</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
The darkness of the derived shadow diminishes in proportion to its distance from the primary shadow.

---

### 160.

Different sorts of derived shadows (160–162).
SHADOW AND LIGHT.

There are three forms of shadows: because if the solid body that casts the shadow is equal in size to the light, the shadow resembles a column and has no end in length. If the body is larger than the light, its shadow resembles a truncated and inverted pyramid, and its length also has no defined end. But if the body is smaller than the light, the shadow will resemble a pyramid and come to an end, as is seen in eclipses of the moon.

---

### 161.

OF SIMPLE DERIVED SHADOWS.

The simple derived shadow is of two kinds: one kind which has a defined length, and two kinds which are undefined. The defined shadow is pyramidal. Of the two undefined shadows, one is columnar and the other spreads out; and all three have straight-lined outlines. But the converging, or pyramidal, shadow originates from a body smaller than the light; the columnar shadow originates from a body equal in size to the light; and the spreading shadow originates from a body larger than the light, etc.

--- Page 135 ---
### 162.

OF COMPOUND DERIVED SHADOWS.

Compound derived shadows are of two kinds: that is, they are formed by two different lights. The first is produced when a body is placed between two lights and casts two shadows, which are called compound because they are formed by more than one light. These shadows, where they overlap or intersect, become darker than in the parts where they are separate. The second kind is produced when a single light is larger than the body that casts the shadow; in this case, the shadow is surrounded by a secondary, lighter shadow, which is also called a compound shadow because it is created by the partial obstruction of the light source.

--- Page 136 ---

->162.<-

| ### OF SHADOW. | ### OF SHADOW. |
| Derived shadows are of three kinds: the first is spreading, the second is columnar, and the third converges to the point where its two sides meet and intersect. Beyond this intersection, the sides extend infinitely as straight lines. And if you were to say that such a shadow must terminate at the angle where the sides meet and extend no further, I deny this. Because earlier, in the first section on shadow, I proved that a thing is completely terminated only when no part of it exceeds its boundary lines. Now, in the case of this shadow, we see the opposite, because as this derived shadow originates, it clearly creates the shape of two shadowy pyramids which are joined at their angles. Therefore, if—as the opponent claims—the first shadowy pyramid terminates the derived shadow at the angle where it begins, then the second shadowy pyramid (the opponent says) must be caused by the angle and not by the object casting the shadow. This is disproved by the second principle of this work, which states: "Shadow is a condition created by opaque bodies placed between the location of that shadow and the luminous body." By this, it is made clear that the shadow is not generated by the angle of the derived shadow, but only by the body casting the shadow, etc. If a spherical solid body is illuminated by an elongated light source, the shadow generated by the longest part of that light will have less distinct edges than the shadow generated by the width of the same light. This is proved by the previous rule, which stated that a shadow will have less distinct edges when it is created by a larger light source, and conversely, the edges will be more distinct when it is illuminated by a smaller light source. | Derived shadows are of three kinds: of which one is spreading, the second columnar, the third converging to the point where the two sides meet and intersect, and beyond this intersection the sides are infinitely prolonged or straight lines. And if you say, this shadow must terminate at the angle where the sides meet and extend no farther, I deny this, because above in the first on shadow I have proved: that a thing is completely terminated when no portion of it goes beyond its terminating lines. Now here, in this shadow, we see the converse of this, in as much as where this derived shadow originates we obviously have the figures of two pyramids of shadow which meet at their angles. Hence, if, as [my] opponent says, the first pyramid of shadow terminates the derivative shadow at the angle whence it starts, then the second pyramid of shadow—so says the adversary—must be caused by the angle and not from the body in shadow; and this is disproved with the help of the 2nd of this which says: Shadow is a condition produced by a body casting a shadow, and interposed between this shadow and the luminous body. By this it is made clear that the shadow is not produced by the angle of the derived shadow but only by the body casting the shadow; &c. If a spherical solid body is illuminated by a light of elongated form the shadow produced by the longest portion of this light will have less defined outlines than that which is produced by the breadth of the same light. And this is proved by what was said before, which is: That a shadow will have less defined outlines in proportion as the light which causes it is larger, and conversely, the outlines are clearer in proportion as it is smaller. |

**94**
**THE THEORY OF THE ART OF PAINTING. [163—166.**

**On the relation of derived and primary shadow (163–165).**

**163.**
The derived shadow can never resemble the body from which it originates unless the light is of the same shape and size as the body casting the shadow.
The derived shadow cannot be of the same shape as the primary shadow unless it strikes a surface at equal angles.

---

**164.**
**HOW A CAST SHADOW CAN NEVER BE OF THE SAME SIZE AS THE BODY THAT CASTS IT.**
If the rays of light proceed, as experience confirms, from a single point and spread out in a circular path from that point, dispersing through the air, the farther they travel, the wider they spread. Consequently, an object placed between the light and a wall is always represented by a larger shadow, because the rays that touch the object have become wider by the time they reach the wall.

---

**165.**
Any shadow cast by a body in light and shade is entirely of the same nature and character as the shadow that is attached to that body. The center of the length of every shadow always aligns with the center of the luminous body. It is necessary that the center of every shadow corresponds to the center of its light source.

---

**On the shape of derived shadows (166–174).**

**166.**
**OF THE PYRAMIDAL SHADOW.**
The pyramidal shadow produced by a parallel-sided body will be narrower than the body itself in proportion to how far the simple derived shadow is intersected from the body casting it.

---

**166.** Compare the first diagram to No. 161. If we imagine the outlines of the shadow pyramid on the ground extending beyond its apex, this creates a second pyramid; this is what is discussed at the beginning of No. 166.
