### 171.

**OF PAINTING.**

Among different shadows of equal strength, the one that is nearest to the eye will seem the least dark.

Why is the shadow $e$ $a$ $b$ in the first grade of strength, $b$ $c$ in the second, and $c$ $d$ in the third? The reason is that from $e$ $a$ $b$ the sky is nowhere visible; it receives no light whatever from the sky, and so is deprived of direct [primary] light. $b$ $c$ faces the portion of the sky $f$

### 172.

When the light of the atmosphere is restricted by an opening and illuminates shaded bodies, if these bodies are equally distant from the center of that window, the one placed most obliquely will cast the largest shadow behind it.

When the light of the atmosphere is restricted [by an opening] and illuminates bodies which cast shadows, these bodies being equally distant from the center of the window, the one that is most obliquely placed will cast the largest shadow beyond it.

### 173.

Those scattered bodies situated in a room illuminated by a single window will produce a derivative shadow that is more or less short depending on how directly they face that window. Among the shadows cast by bodies of equal size at unequal distances from the opening that illuminates them, the longest shadow will belong to the body that is least in the light. And one body will be more illuminated than another in proportion to how much shorter its shadow is than the other. The proportion that $n \ m$ and $e \ v \ k$ bear to $r \ t$ and $v \ x$ is the same as the proportion of shadow $x$ to $4 \ y$.

The reason why shaded bodies placed most directly in front of the middle of the window cast shorter shadows than those situated obliquely is that they see the window in its true shape, whereas the oblique bodies see it foreshortened. To the one in the middle, the window appears large; to the oblique ones, it appears small. The one in the middle faces the entire hemisphere, which is $e \ f$, while those on the side face only a strip; that is, $q \ r$ faces $a \ b$.

¶ Where the shadow is larger, smaller, or equal to the body that casts it.

[First, concerning the character of divided lights.

---

### OF THE COMPOUND SHADOW $F, R, C, H$ CAUSED BY A SINGLE LIGHT.

The shadow $f\ r\ c\ h$ is subject to such conditions that where it is farthest from its inner side, it loses depth proportionately. To prove this:

Let $d\ a$ be the light and $f\ n$ the solid body, and let $a\ e$ be one of the side walls of the window, which is $d\ a$. Then I say—according to the second proposition—that the surface of any body is affected by the tone of the objects surrounding it; therefore, the side $r\ c$, which faces the dark wall $a\ e$, must participate in its darkness. In the same way, the outer surface which faces the light $d\ a$ participates in the light; thus we define the outlines of the extremes on each side of the center included between them.

This is divided into four parts: first, the extremes which include the compound shadow; second, the compound shadow between these extremes.

---

### THE ACTION OF THE LIGHT AS FROM ITS CENTRE.

If it were the whole of the light that caused the shadows beyond the bodies placed in front of it, it would follow that any body much smaller than the light would cast a pyramidal shadow; but since experience does not show this, it must be the center of the light that produces this effect.

--- Page 145 ---
### PROOF.

Let $a$ $b$ be the width of the light from a window, which falls on a stick set upright. Let $a$ $c$ and $a$ $d$ be the space where the entire window is fully visible. At $c$ $e$, that part of the window between $l$ $b$ cannot be seen. In the same way, $a$ $m$ cannot be seen from $d$ $f$, and for this reason, the light begins to diminish in these two areas.

---

## 180.

An opaque body placed between two equal lights will cast as many shadows as there are lights. These shadows will be darker than one another in proportion to how much closer the light on the opposite side is to the body than the other lights.

An opaque body situated at an equal distance between two lights will cast two shadows, one deeper than the other in proportion to how much brighter the light that causes it is than the other.

---

## 181.

A light that is smaller than the opaque body causes the shadows to terminate upon that body, produces little compound shadow, and illuminates less than half of it. A light that is larger than the opaque body illuminates more than half of it and produces a great deal of compound shadow.

102
THE THEORY OF THE ART OF PAINTING. [182. 183.

### 182.

### ON THE SHADOW CAST BY A BODY PLACED BETWEEN 2 EQUAL LIGHTS.

The effect of light at different distances.
A body placed between 2 equal lights will cast 2 shadows of itself in the direction of the lines of the 2 lights; and if you move this body, placing it nearer to one of the lights, the shadow cast towards the nearer light will be less deep than that which falls towards the more distant one.

### 183.

Further complications in the derived shadows (183–187).
The greatest depth of shadow is in the simple derived shadow, because it is not lighted by either of the two lights *a b, c d*.

The next less deep shadow is the derived shadow *e f n*; and in this, the shadow is less by half, because it is illuminated by a single light, that is *c d*.

This is uniform in natural tone because it is lighted throughout by only one of the two luminous bodies. But it varies with the conditions of shadow, inasmuch as the farther it is away from the light, the less it is illuminated by it.

The third degree of depth is the middle shadow. But this is not uniform in natural tone; because the nearer it gets to the simple derived shadow, the deeper it is, and it is the uniformly gradual diminution by increase of distance which modifies it: that is to say, the depth of a shadow increases in proportion to the distance from the two lights.

The fourth is the shadow *k r s*, and this is all the darker in natural tone in proportion as it is nearer to *k s*, because it gets less of the light *a b*; but by the circumstance of distance, it is rendered less deep because it is nearer to the light *c d*, and thus is always exposed to both lights.

## OF SIMPLE SHADOWS.

Why is a simple shadow produced at the intersections $a$ and $b$ of the two compound shadows $e$ $f$ and $m$ $c$, as well as at $e$ $h$ and $m$ $g$, while no such simple shadow is produced at the other two intersections $c$ and $d$ made by the very same compound shadows mentioned above?

### ANSWER.

Compound shadows are a mixture of light and dark, while simple shadows consist of pure darkness. Therefore, of the two lights $n$ and $o$, one light reaches the compound shadows from one side and the other light reaches the compound shadows from the other side; however, neither light reaches the intersections $a$ and $b$, and for that reason, it is a simple shadow. But in a compound shadow, one or the other light is visible. 

Here a doubt arises for my opponent, because he says that at the intersections of compound shadows, you must necessarily see the two lights that cause those shadows, and for this reason, such shadows ought to be canceled out. This is because where the two lights are not visible, we call the shadow "simple"; where only one of the two lights is visible, we call that shadow "compound"; and where both lights are visible, the shadow is "canceled." Since both lights are visible there, no shadow of any kind is generated; instead, it merely forms the brightness of the background surrounding the shadows. 

To this, I respond that my opponent’s statement is true, but he only mentions the truth that favors his own argument. If he adds the rest, he will conclude that my proposition is true: namely, that if the two lights were visible at such an intersection, those shadows would indeed be canceled.

--- Page 150 ---
I confess this to be true when we do not see the two shadows in the same spot; because where you see one shadow and one light, a compound shadow is generated, and where you see two shadows and two similar lights, that shadow cannot vary in any part, since the shadows are equal and the lights are equal. This is proved in the eighth proposition on proportion, which says: a single power with a single resistance has the same proportion as a double power with a double resistance.

---

### 185.

#### DEFINITION.
The intersection $n$ is made by the shadows created by light $b$, because this light $b$ generates shadow $x\ b$ and shadow $s\ b$; but the intersection $m$ is made by light $a$, which generates shadow $s\ a$ and shadow $x\ a$.

But if you uncover the two lights $a$ and $b$, then the two shadows $n$ and $m$ are generated at the same time; and besides these, two others are generated as simple shadows—namely $r$ and $o$—in which neither of the two light sources is visible. The degrees of darkness that compound shadows acquire are greater in proportion to how few light sources can see them.

---

### 186.

Why the intersection $n$, being composed of two compound derived shadows...

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s technical analysis of optics from page 186, focusing on how different types of shadows—simple, derived, and compound—interact and overlap. It also begins section 187, which examines how the shadows cast by the walls of a window frame influence the light entering a room.</meta>

...forms a **compound shadow** <term>original: *ombra composta*; a shadow created by the interaction of multiple light sources or overlapping shadows</term> and not a **simple shadow**, as occurs with other intersections of compound shadows. 

This happens according to the second diagram of this proposition, which states: "The intersection of **derived shadows** <term>original: *ombre derivative*; shadows that project into the air behind an object</term>—when produced by the intersection of **columnar shadows** caused by a single light source—does not result in a simple shadow." 

This is the corollary to the first proposition, which states: "The intersection of simple derived shadows never results in a deeper shadow." This is because all the deepest shadows added together cannot be darker than one by itself. If many "deepest" shadows increased in darkness by being stacked together, they could not be called "deepest" shadows, but only "partial" shadows. 

However, if such intersections are illuminated by a second light source placed between the eye and the intersecting objects, then those shadows would become compound shadows and have a uniform darkness at the intersection as well as throughout the rest. In the first and second examples above, the intersections marked *i* and *k* will not double in depth, even though they double in size. But in this third example, at the intersections *g* and *n*, the shadows will be double in both depth and size.

<margin>Codex Atlanticus 187 II a; 562 a]</margin>

->**187.**<-

## HOW AND WHERE DARK OBJECTS MIX WITH THE LIGHT DERIVED FROM A LUMINOUS BODY.

The derived shadows from the dark walls on either side of a bright window are what mingle their various levels of shade with the light coming from that window. These varying depths of shade modify every part of the light, except where it is at its strongest, at point *c*. 

To prove this, let *d a* represent the **primary shadow** <term>original: *ombra primitiva*; the shadow on the object itself</term> which is directed toward point *e*, darkening it with its derived shadow. This is demonstrated by the triangle $\triangle$ *a e d*, in which the angle at *e* faces the entire dark base *d a c*. Point *v* faces the dark shadow *a s*, which is only a part of *a d*. Since the whole is greater than a part, point *e* [is darker than point *v*].

# FOURTH BOOK ON LIGHT AND SHADE.

On the shape of cast shadows (188—191).

**188.**
The shape of the shadow cast by any body of uniform thickness can never be identical to the body that produces it.

**189.**
No cast shadow can produce the true image of the body that casts it upon a wall unless the center of the light source is at an equal distance from all the edges of that body.

**190.**
If a window *a b* admits sunlight into a house, that sunlight will increase the apparent size of the window and decrease the shadow of a man. This happens in such a way that when the man brings his faint shadow near to the one that represents the true size of the window, he will see—at the point where the shadows meet—that the shadows, though faint and blurred by the intensity of the light, close together and do not allow the sun’s rays to pass. The shadow cast by the man at this point of contact will produce exactly the effect illustrated above.

--- Page 154 ---
### 191.

A shadow will never appear to have a uniform darkness on the surface where it is intercepted unless every part of that surface is at an equal distance from the light source. This is proven by the 7th rule, which states: a shadow will appear lighter or darker depending on whether it is surrounded by a darker or lighter background. And by the 8th rule of this section: the background will have parts that are darker or lighter in proportion to how far or near it is to the light source. Furthermore, among areas at an equal distance from the light source, the one that receives the light rays at the most equal angles will appear the most illuminated. The outline of a shadow cast upon any uneven surface will appear to have contours identical to the body that casts it, provided the eye is positioned exactly where the center of the light source was.

A shadow appears darkest where it is farthest from the body that casts it. The shadow $c\ d$, produced by the body $a\ b$ (which is at an equal distance throughout), does not appear uniform in darkness because it is situated on a background of varying brightness.

---

The edges of a derived shadow will be most distinct where the shadow is cast closest to the original object.

### 193.

The further a derived shadow moves from the original object, the more its outlines will differ from those of the original object.

### ON SHADOWS WHICH ARE NEVER SHARP.

The larger the light source is in comparison to the body casting the shadow, the more blurred the outlines of that body's shadow will be.

The derivative shadow will have its edges most blurred where it strikes a surface that is farthest from the object casting the shadow.

### 195.

**What is the cause that makes the edges of a shadow blurred and indistinct?**
Whether it is possible to give sharp and definite edges to the boundaries of shadows.

---

### 196.

## THE BODY WHICH IS NEAREST TO THE LIGHT CASTS THE LARGEST SHADOW, AND WHY?

If an object placed in front of a single light source is very close to it, you will see that it casts a very large shadow on the opposite wall; and the farther you move the object away from the light, the more the size of that shadow will decrease.

## WHY A SHADOW LARGER THAN THE BODY THAT PRODUCES IT BECOMES DISPROPORTIONATE.

The disproportion of a shadow that is larger than the body producing it occurs because the light, being smaller than the object, cannot be at an equal distance from the edges of that object; and the part that is more distant increases more than the nearer parts, and therefore it grows larger.

## WHY A SHADOW LARGER THAN THE BODY CAUSING IT HAS BLURRED EDGES.

The air that surrounds the light is almost like the light itself in its brightness and—

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>Continuing from the discussion on how the distance between an object and a light source affects the size and clarity of the resulting shadow.</meta>

...and in color; but the farther away it is, the more it loses this resemblance. An object which casts a large shadow and is near to the light is illuminated both by that light and by the luminous atmosphere; consequently, this diffused light makes the edges of the shadow look blurred and ill-defined.

---

<margin>E. 31 b]</margin>
### 197.

A light source <note>original: "luminoso"</note> that is long and narrow in shape creates more blurred edges for the derived shadow <term>The shadow cast by an object onto another surface</term> than a spherical light does. This fact contradicts the following proposition: "A shadow's edges will be more clearly defined the closer they are to the primary shadow <term>The shadow on the object itself</term> (the body casting the shadow)." The cause of this contradiction is the elongated shape of the light source $a-c$, etc. <note>The letters refer to Leonardo's diagrams of light rays passing an object.</note>

---

<margin>E. 32 a]</margin>
### 198.

->OF MODIFIED SHADOWS<-
-><note>original: "De ombre corrotte"</note><-

Modified shadows are those that are seen against light-colored walls or other illuminated surfaces.

<margin>How the tone of the background affects cast shadows.</margin>

A shadow appears darkest when it is seen against a lighter background. The outlines of a derived shadow <term>a shadow cast onto a surface</term> will be clearer the closer they are to the primary shadow <term>the object itself</term>. A derived shadow will have its shape most clearly defined where it is intercepted by a surface at a perpendicular angle <note>original: "infra angoli più equali," literally "at more equal angles," referring to a 90-degree intersection</note>.

# 199.

## THE OPINION OF SOME THAT A TRIANGLE CASTS NO SHADOW ON A PLANE SURFACE.

Certain mathematicians have maintained that a triangle, with its base turned toward the light, casts no shadow on a plane; and they prove this by saying that no spherical body smaller than the light source can reach the center with its shadow. The lines of radiant light are straight lines; therefore, suppose the light to be $g$ $h$ and the triangle $l$ $m$ $n$, and let the plane be $i$ $k$. They say the light $g$ sees the side of the triangle $l$ $n$ and the portion of the plane $i$ $q$. Likewise, $h$, like $g$, sees the side $l$ $m$, and then sees $m$ $n$ and the plane $p$ $k$. If the entire plane is thus seen by the lights $g$ and $h$, it follows that the triangle has no shadow; and that which has no shadow can cast none. In this case, the argument appears credible. However, if the triangle $n$ $p$ $g$ were not seen by the two lights $g$ and $h$, but $i$ $p$ and $g$ $k$ were each seen by only one single light—that is, $i$ $p$ cannot be seen by $h$ and $g$, and $k$ will never be seen by $g$—then $p$ $q$ will be twice as bright as the two visible spaces that contain shadow.

# 200.

## On the relative depth of cast shadows.

A place is more shaded when it is seen by a greater quantity of shaded rays. The place that is struck by a wider angle formed by shaded rays will be darker; $a$ will have double the darkness of $b$ because it originates from a base twice as large at an equal distance. A place will be more luminous when it is struck by a greater quantity of luminous rays. $d$ is the beginning of the shadow $df$, and it colors $c$ only slightly; $de$ is half of the shadow $df$ and colors the impact at $b$ more than at $f$. And the entire shaded interval $e$ completely colors the place $a$ with its tone.

### 201.

<margin>Tr. 29]</margin>

### 202.

<margin>Ash. I. 28b]</margin>

To the extent that a shadow cast by an object upon a wall <note>original: *pariete*</note> is smaller than the object itself <note>original: *sua cagione*, literally "its cause"</note>, that object will be illuminated by weaker rays of light.

Let *d e* be the object and *d c* be the wall surface. The number of times that *d e* fits into *f g* determines how much more light there is at *f h* than at *d c*. A ray of light will be weaker in proportion to its distance from its aperture <note>original: *spiracolo*, meaning a small hole or vent through which light passes</note>.

<page-num>114</page-num>
<header>THE THEORY OF THE ART OF PAINTING. [206. 207.</header>

### WHERE THERE CAN BE NO LUMINOUS REFLECTION <note>original: *Dove non può essere rinverberatione luminosa*</note>

---

All solid bodies have their surfaces covered by various qualities of light and shadow. Light is of two types: one is called "original," and the other is "derived" <note>original: *derivativo*</note>. I define "original light" as that which comes from a flame, the sun, or the atmosphere. "Derived light" is reflected light. 

To return to the definition I promised: I say that luminous reflection does not occur on parts of a body that are facing dark objects, such as dark places, fields of grass of various heights, or woods (whether green or dry). Even though the side of every branch facing the original light is illuminated, there are so many shadows cast by each individual branch, and so many shadows cast by one branch onto another, that the overall result is darkness. In these cases, the light is effectively nonexistent; therefore, such objects cannot cast any reflected light onto the surfaces opposite them.

---

<margin>H.² 28 b]</margin>
**206.**

->PERSPECTIVE<- <note>original: *Prospectiva*</note>

<margin>Reflection on water (206. 207).</margin>

A shadow or an object mirrored in moving water—specifically in small ripples—will always appear larger than the actual object creating the reflection.

---

<margin>Br. M. 93 b]</margin>
**207.**

It is impossible for an object reflected in water to match the exact shape of the real object, because the viewer's eye is positioned above the water’s surface.

### 208.
Experiments with the mirror (208—210).

#### THE MIRROR.

If the illuminated object is the same size as the light source and the surface where that light is reflected, the quality of the reflected light will have the same proportion to the intermediate light as this second light has to the first, provided these bodies are flat and white.

---

### 209.

Describe how no object is limited within the mirror itself, but is limited by the eye that sees it inside that mirror. For if you represent your face in the mirror, the part is like the whole, since the part is present throughout the entire mirror and is entirely in every part of that same mirror; and the same occurs with the entire image of any object placed opposite that mirror, etc.

---

### 210.

No man will see the image of another man in a mirror in the exact place where it is reflected, because every object falls upon the mirror at equal angles. If the man who sees the other in the mirror is not positioned along the line of the visual images, he will not see him in the place where the reflection falls; and if he enters into those lines, he blocks the other man and places himself over his image. Let n o be the mirror, let b be...

--- Page 164 ---
by his image. Let $n$ be the mirror, $b$ the eye of your friend, and $d$ your own eye. Your friend’s eye will appear to you at $a$, and to him it will seem that yours is at $c$. The intersection of the visual rays will occur at $m$, so that if either of you touches $m$, you will touch the eye of the other person that is open. And if you touch the eye of the other person in the mirror, it will seem to him that you are touching your own.

### 211.
#### ON THE SHADOW AND ITS MOTION.

When two bodies casting shadows are placed one behind the other between a window and a wall with some space between them, the shadow of the body nearest to the wall will move if the body nearest to the window is moved transversely across that window. To prove this, let $a$ and $b$ be two bodies placed between the window $n$ $m$ and the wall surface $o$ $p$, with a certain amount of space between them, which is the space $a$ $b$. I say that if the body $a$ moves toward $s$, the shadow of body $b$, which is at $c$, will move toward $d$.

---

### 212.
#### ON THE MOTION OF SHADOWS.

the motion of a shadow is always faster than the motion of the body that produces it, provided the light source is stationary. To prove this, let $a$ be the light source, $b$ the body casting the shadow, and $d$ the shadow. I say that in the same amount of time that the body $b$ moves to $c$, the shadow $d$ moves to $e$; and the ratio of one speed to the other in that same time is the same as the ratio of the length of one movement to the length of the other. Therefore, by the ratio that the length of the movement made by the body $b$ to $c$ has to the length of the movement made by the shadow $d$ to $e$,

->**PERSPECTIVE**<-
<note>original: Prospettiva</note>

<margin>The effect of rays passing through holes (213. 214).</margin>

If you cause the rays of the sun to pass through a hole <term>original: spiraculo; an aperture or small opening</term> shaped like a star, you will see beautiful effects of perspective in the spot where the passing sunlight strikes.

---

No aperture can so alter the convergence of light rays that they do not, over a long distance, project onto the object an image resembling their source <term>original: cagione; cause or origin</term>. It is impossible for rays of light passing through a parallel opening not to show the form of their source on the object; for all effects of luminous bodies demonstrate their causes. The moon, in the shape of a boat <note>original: naviculare; boat-shaped, referring to a crescent moon</note>, when its light passes through a hole, will project a boat-shaped body onto the surface it strikes. Why does the eye see distant bodies as larger than they measure on a flat surface <term>original: pariete; a wall or vertical plane used in perspective drawing</term>?

---

### 215.

Although the breadth and length of light and shadow will appear narrower and shorter due to foreshortening, the quality and quantity of their brightness or darkness will neither increase nor decrease.

The function of light and shade when diminished by foreshortening will be to cast shadow upon or to illuminate the opposite body, according to the quality and quantity that appears on that body.

The closer a derived shadow approaches its penultimate extremities, the deeper its darkness will appear. Beyond the intersection, *g z* faces only the part of the shadow *y z*; this, by intersection, receives the shadow *m n*, but by a direct line it receives the shadow *a m*; therefore, it has twice as much shadow as *g z*. *Y x*, by intersection, receives the shadow *n o*, and by a direct line the shadow *n m a*; therefore, *x y* is shown to have three times as much shadow as *z g*. *X f*, by intersection, faces *o b*, and by a direct line faces *o n m a*; therefore, we must say that the shadow between *f x* will be four times as dark as the shadow *z g*, because it faces four times as much shadow.

Let *a b* be the part of the primary shadow, *b c* the primary light, *d* the point of intersection, *f g* the derived shadow, and *f e* the derived light.

And this should be at the beginning of the explanation.

---

### 216.

That part of the surface of a body which is struck by a larger angle of the images from opposite bodies will be more strongly tinted by their color. In the diagram below, angle 8 is larger than angle 4, because its base *a n* is larger than *e n*, the base of angle 4. This diagram below should be bounded by *a n*, 4, and 8. That part of the illuminated surface...

--- Page 168 ---
The illuminated surface on which a shadow is cast will be brightest where it lies adjacent to the cast shadow. Just as an object that is lit by a greater quantity of luminous rays becomes brighter, so one on which a greater quantity of shadow falls will be darker.

Let 4 be the side of an illuminated surface 4 8, surrounding the cast shadow g e 4. This spot 4 will be lighter than 8, because less shadow falls on it than on 8. Since 4 faces only the shadow i n, while 8 faces and receives the shadow a e as well as i n, which makes it twice as dark. The same thing happens when you substitute the atmosphere and the sun for shade and light.

The distribution of shadow, originating in and limited by plane surfaces placed near to each other, equal in tone and directly opposite, will be darker at the ends than at the beginning, which will be determined by the incidence of the luminous rays.

You will find the same proportion in the depth of the derived shadows a n as in the proximity of the luminous bodies m b that cause them; and if the luminous bodies were of equal size, you would further find the same proportion in the light cast by the luminous circles and their shadows as in the distance of those luminous bodies.

---

### 217.

THAT PART OF THE REFLECTION WILL BE BRIGHTEST WHERE THE REFLECTED RAYS ARE SHORTEST.

The darkness caused by the casting of combined shadows will correspond to its cause, which will originate and terminate between two plane surfaces near together, alike in tone and directly opposite each other.

--- Page 169 ---
In proportion as the source of light is larger, the paths of the shadow and luminous rays will be more mixed together. This effect occurs because wherever there is a greater quantity of luminous rays, there is more light, and where there are fewer, less light results; consequently, the shadow rays come to mingle with them.

---

### 218.

In all the proportions I establish, it must be understood that the medium existing between the bodies is uniform throughout. The smaller the luminous body, the more distinct the convergence of the shadow will be.

When two opposite shadows produced by the same body are twice as dark as one another, yet similar in shape, the two lights causing them will have twice the diameter of each other and be at twice the distance from that opaque body. If an object is moved slowly in front of a luminous body, and the projection of that object's shadow is far from the object itself, the motion of the derived shadow will have the same proportion to the motion of the primary shadow as the distance between the object and the light has to the distance between the object and the shadow's projection; in this way, while the object moves slowly, the shadow moves quickly.

---

### 219.

A luminous body will appear to have less brilliance when it is surrounded by a brighter field.

I have found that those stars which are nearer to the horizon appear larger than the others because they see and are seen by a greater portion of the solar body than when they are above us; and by seeing more of the sun, they have more light, and the body which...

--- Page 170 ---
is more luminous appears to be of a larger size. This is seen with the sun through a mist and overhead; it appears larger where there is no mist, and its size decreases through the mist. No part of the luminous body is ever visible from any point within the pyramid of pure derived shadow.

---

### 220.

A body on which the solar rays fall after passing between the thin branches of trees will, at a distance, cast only a single shadow.

If an opaque body and a luminous body are both spherical, the base of the pyramid of light will have the same proportion to its body as the base of the pyramid of shadow has to its opaque body.

When the shadow cast by the convergence of the opaque body is intercepted by a wall placed opposite to it, the farther it is from the luminous body and the closer it is to its point of origin, the darker it will appear and the more distinct its edges will be.

---

### 221.

A body illuminated by solar rays passing through the thick branches of trees will produce as many shadows as there are branches positioned between the sun and itself.

Where the shadow-rays originating from a pyramidal opaque body are intercepted, they will form a bifurcated shape and varying degrees of darkness at their points. A light which is wider than the apex but narrower than the base of the body placed opposite to it...

--- Page 171 ---
Where the shadow-rays originating from a pyramidal opaque body are intercepted, they will form a bifurcated shape and varying degrees of darkness.

If an opaque body smaller than the light casts two shadows, and if an opaque body of the same size as the light or larger casts only one, it follows that a pyramidal body—which has one part smaller, one part equal to, and one part larger than the luminous body—will cast a bifurcated shadow.

--- Page 173 ---
### IV.

## *Perspective of Disappearance.*

The theory of the “*Prospettiva de' perdimenti*” would, in many important details, be quite unintelligible if it had not been introduced by the principles of light and shade on which it is based. The word “*Prospettiva*” in the language of that time included the principles of optics; what Leonardo understood by “*Perdimenti*” will be clearly seen in the early chapters, Nos. 222—224. It is in the very nature of the case that the further explanations given in the subsequent chapters must be limited to general rules. The sections given as 227—231 “On indistinctness at short distances” have, it is true, only an indirect bearing on the subject; but on the other hand, the following chapters, 232—234, “On indistinctness at great distances,” go fully into the matter, and in chapters 235—239, which treat “Of the importance of light and shade in the *Perspective of Disappearance*,” the practical applications are distinctly emphasized in their relation to the theory. This is naturally followed by the statements regarding “the effect of light or dark backgrounds on the apparent size of bodies” (Nos. 240—250). At the end I have placed, in the order of the original, those sections from the *MS. C* which treat of the “*Perspective of Disappearance*” and serve to some extent to complete the treatment of the subject (251—262).

--- Page 174 ---
[The page appears to be a blank leaf or the original ink has faded beyond legibility. Significant bleed-through from the reverse side of the page is visible (mirrored text), but there is no readable text intended for this specific side.]

--- Page 175 ---
### PERSPECTIVE OF THE LOSS OF DISTINCTNESS IN THE OUTLINES OF OPAQUE BODIES.

If the real outlines of opaque bodies are indistinguishable at even the shortest distance, they will be even more invisible at long distances; and, since it is by its outlines that we recognize the true shape of any opaque body, when we fail to perceive the object as a whole due to distance, we will even more certainly fail to perceive its parts and outlines.

**Definition (222. 223).**
If the real outlines of opaque bodies are indistinguishable at even a very short distance, they will be more so at long distances; and, since it is by its outlines that we are able to know the real form of any opaque body, when by its remoteness we fail to discern it as a whole, much more must we fail to discern its parts and outlines.

---

### ON THE DIMINISHING PERSPECTIVE OF OPAQUE BODIES.

Among opaque bodies of equal size, the apparent diminution of their shapes will be in proportion to their distance from the eye that sees them; but this proportion is inverse, because where the distance is greater, the opaque body appears smaller, and where the distance is less, that body will appear larger. From this arises linear perspective, and it follows that every body at a great distance first loses that part of itself which is thinnest. For example, one will lose sight of a horse's legs before its head, because the legs are thinner than the head; and one will lose the neck before the torso for the same reason stated above. Therefore, it follows that the last part of the horse which would be discernible to the eye would be the mass of the body in an oval shape.

--- Page 176 ---
...which, once the recognition of the horse is reserved only to the eye, will remain in an oval form, or rather in a cylindrical form; and this would lose its apparent thickness before its length—according to the 2nd rule given above, etc.

If the eye remains stationary, the perspective terminates in the distance at a point. But if the eye moves in a straight line, the perspective terminates in a line; the reason is that this line is generated by the motion of the point and our sight. Therefore, it follows that as we move our sight, the point moves, and as we move the point, the line is generated, etc.

Every visible body, in so far as it affects the eye, includes three attributes: mass, form, and color. The mass is recognizable at a greater distance from its place of origin than either color or form. Again, color is discernible at a greater distance than form, but this law does not apply to luminous bodies.

The above proposition is plainly shown and proved by experiment; because if you see a man close to you, you discern the exact appearance of the mass, the form, and also the coloring. If he moves to some distance, you will not recognize who he is because the character of the details will disappear. If he goes still farther, you will not be able to distinguish his coloring; instead, he will appear as a dark object. From even farther away, he will appear as a very small, dark, rounded object. He appears rounded because distance so greatly diminishes the various details that nothing remains visible but the larger mass. And the reason is this: we know very well that all the images of objects reach the senses through a small aperture in the eye. Hence, if the whole horizon *a d* is admitted through such an aperture, and the object *b c* is but a very small fraction of this horizon, what space can it fill in that...

--- Page 177 ---
...occupy the smallest representation of so vast a hemisphere? And because luminous bodies are more powerful in darkness than any others, it is necessary—since the aperture of sight is quite dark, as is the nature of all cavities—that the images of distant bodies should be confused within such great light from the sky; and if they appear at all, they seem dark and black, as every small body does when seen in the brightness of the air.

---

### 225.

#### ON THE ATMOSPHERE INTERPOSED BETWEEN THE EYE AND THE VISIBLE OBJECT.

An object will appear more or less distinct at the same distance depending on whether the air interposed between the eye and that object is more or less thin. Therefore, since I know that the greater or lesser quantity of air interposed between the eye and the object makes the outlines of those bodies more or less confused to the eye, you must make the loss of definition of those bodies in the same proportion to one another as the proportion of their distances from the eye of the spectator.

---

### 226.

When I was in a seaside location at an equal distance between the shore and the mountain, the distance to the shore appeared much longer than the distance to the mountain.

---

### 227.

If you place an opaque body before your eyes at a distance of four fingers' breadth, and if it is smaller than the distance from one eye to the other, it will not block the view of anything that is behind it. Nothing situated beyond the visual object of the eye can be blocked by that object if it is smaller than the space that exists between the eyes.

--- Page 178 ---
### 228.

The eye cannot perceive a luminous angle that is too close to it.

---

### 229.

That part of a surface will be more brightly illuminated where the light falls at a larger angle. Conversely, that area where the shadow falls at a larger angle will receive the least benefit from the light rays.

---

### 230.

**Of the Eye.**

The edges of an object placed in front of the pupil of the eye will appear less distinct the closer they are to that pupil. This is proven by the edge of object $n$ placed in front of the pupil $d$; when the pupil looks at this edge, it also sees the entire space $a$ $c$ which lies beyond that edge. The images coming from that space mingle with the images of the edge itself, and thus one image confuses the other; such confusion prevents the pupil from clearly perceiving that edge.

---

### 231.

The outlines of an object will be less clear when they are closer to the eyes; it follows that more distant outlines will be clearer. Among objects smaller than the pupil of the eye, those that are closer to the eye will be the least distinct.

--- Page 179 ---

At point *b*, I say that since the light *c* falls on the whole area *m n* <gloss>6</gloss>, the raised parts <note>original Italian: "rilievi"</note> on that side will all be illuminated. Therefore, an eye positioned at *c* <gloss>7</gloss> will not be able to see any contrast between light and shadow <gloss>8</gloss>. Because no such contrast is visible, every part will appear to be the same color <gloss>9</gloss>; consequently, the distinct shapes of the prominent <gloss>10</gloss> or rounded parts will not be visible.

### 236.

<margin>Ashburnham I. 21 a]</margin>

#### ON PAINTING. <note>original Latin: "De Pittura"</note>
<gloss>2</gloss> When you represent shadows in your work that you can only discern with difficulty, and whose edges you cannot distinguish—so that you perceive <gloss>4</gloss> and record them with a degree of uncertainty <note>original: "confuso givditio," meaning confused judgment</note>—do not make them sharp or clearly defined. If you do, <gloss>4</gloss> your work will result in a stiff, "wooden" appearance.

### 237.

<margin>E. 17 a]</margin>

#### PAINTING. <note>original Italian: "Pittura"</note>
<gloss>2</gloss> In your drawing, you will observe that among <gloss>3</gloss> the shadows there are subtle <note>original: "insensibili," meaning imperceptible</note> gradations of darkness <gloss>4</gloss> and shape. This is proven by the third principle <gloss>5</gloss> which states: Rounded <note>original: "globulenti"</note> surfaces display <gloss>6</gloss> as many different levels of light and shade <gloss>7</gloss> as there are variations in the brightness and <gloss>8</gloss> darkness of the objects facing them.

An object which is so brilliantly illuminated as to be almost as bright as light itself will be visible at a greater distance, and will have a larger apparent size than is natural for objects so remote.

--- Page 181 ---
### 240.

The effect of light or dark backgrounds on the apparent size of objects (240—250).

A shadow will appear darker in proportion to the brilliance of the light surrounding it, and conversely, it will be less conspicuous when it is created against a darker background.

---

### 241.

#### ON ORDINARY PERSPECTIVE.

An object of uniform thickness and color that is seen against a background of varied color will appear to be of varied thickness.

And if an object of uniform thickness but of various colors is seen against a background of uniform color, that object will appear to be of various thickness. And the more the colors of the background or of the object seen against the background vary, the more varied the thickness will seem, even though the objects seen against the background are of equal thickness.

---

### 242.

A dark object seen against a bright background will appear smaller than it actually is.

A light object will appear larger when it is seen against a background of a darker color.

---

### 243.

#### ON LIGHT.

A luminous body that is obscured by a dense atmosphere will appear smaller in size, as is shown by the moon or sun when obscured by mist.

#### ON LIGHT.

Among luminous bodies of equal size, distance, and brilliance, the one that is surrounded by the darkest background will appear to be the largest in form.

#### ON LIGHT.

I find that every luminous body, when seen through a dense and thick mist, diminishes in size.

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci's observations on optical illusions in painting, specifically focusing on how the contrast between an object and its background affects its perceived size and clarity.</meta>

The further a light recedes from the eye, the more it diminishes; this is true of the sun by day, as well as the moon and the other eternal lights by night. <note>original: "quanto più s'allontana dall'occhio più diminuisce, e cosi fa il sole di dì come la luna e li altri immortali lumi di notte."</note> And when the air is clear, the further these luminaries are from the eye, the larger they appear to be. <note>original: "E quando l'aria è pura quanto essi lumi più s'allontano dall'occhio, più pare si faccino di maggiore forma."</note>

---

## 244.

<margin>F. 22 a]</margin>
That part of a dark object of uniform thickness will appear thinner when it is seen against a brighter background.

[4] **e** is a given dark body of uniform thickness; **a b** and **c d** are backgrounds, one darker than the other; **b c** is a bright background, such as a spot of light created by a sunbeam entering through a vent <term>spiracolo</term> <note>original: "spiracolo di sole"; a small opening or aperture</note> into a dark room <note>original: "camera oscura"</note>. I say that the object **e g** will appear thicker at **e f** than at **g h**, because **e f** has a darker background than **g h**. Furthermore, the part **f g** will seem thin because it is seen by the eye **o** against the background **b c**, which is bright. [12] That part of a luminous body of uniform thickness and brilliance will appear thicker when seen against a darker background; in this case, the luminous body will appear to be glowing <term>ifocato</term> <note>original: "ifocato"; literally "on fire" or incandescent</note>.

### 246.

| | |
|----------|----------|
| An opaque body will appear smaller when it is surrounded by a very bright background, and a light body will appear larger when it is seen against a darker background. This can be seen in the height of buildings at night: when lightning flashes behind them, it suddenly seems as if the flash diminishes the height of the building. For this same reason, these buildings look larger in a mist or at night than when the atmosphere is clear and well-lit. | An opaque body will appear smaller when it is surrounded by a highly luminous background, and a light body will appear larger when it is seen against a darker background. This may be seen in the height of buildings at night, when lightning flashes behind them; it suddenly seems, when it lightens, as though the height of the building were diminished. For the same reason such buildings look larger in a mist, or by night than when the atmosphere is clear and light. |

### 247.

| **ON LIGHT WITHIN SHADOWS.** | **ON LIGHT BETWEEN SHADOWS.** |
|---------------------------|-------------------------------|
| When you draw any object, remember when comparing the intensity <term>potētia</term> <note>original: "potētia"; literally "power" or "potency" of light</note> of the light in its illuminated parts that the eye is often deceived, seeing what is less bright as brighter. This happens because of the comparison with the parts that border <note>original: "confinano"</note> them. If you have two parts of unequal brightness, and the less bright part borders a dark area while the brighter part borders a light area—such as the sky or similar brightness—then the part that is less bright (or I should say, less radiant) will appear more radiant, and the brighter part will appear darker. | When you are drawing any object, remember, in comparing the grades of light in the illuminated portions, that the eye is often deceived by seeing things lighter than they are. And the reason lies in our comparing those parts with the contiguous parts. Since if two [separate] parts are in different grades of light and if the less bright is conterminous with a dark portion and the brighter is conterminous with a light background—as the sky or something equally bright—, then that which is less light, or I should say less radiant, will look the brighter and the brighter will seem the darker. |

### 249.

**PROOF OF HOW LUMINOUS BODIES APPEAR LARGER AT A DISTANCE THAN THEY ACTUALLY ARE.**

If you place two lighted candles side by side half a cubit apart, and move away from them to a distance of 200 cubits, you will see that due to the visual expansion of each, they will merge into a single luminous body, appearing as one large light one cubit wide.

**PROOF OF HOW TO SEE THE REAL SIZE OF LUMINOUS BODIES.**

If you wish to see the real size of these luminous bodies, take a thin board and make a hole in it no larger than the metal tip of a lace. Place it as close to your eye as possible so that when looking through this hole at the aforementioned light, you can see a wide space of air around it. Then, by rapidly moving this board toward and away from your eye, you will see the light appear to increase and diminish in size.

---

### 250.

Among bodies of equal quality that are equally distant from the eye, the one surrounded by a more luminous field will appear to be of smaller shape.

Every visible body is surrounded by light and shadow. A perfectly round body surrounded by light and shadow will appear to have one part larger than the other in proportion to how much more one side is illuminated than the other.

---

### 251.

**PERSPECTIVE.**

No visible body can be properly understood or judged by human eyes except by the variation of the background where the edges of these bodies end and meet. No object, regarding the lines of its edges, will appear to be separated from its background [without this contrast]. The moon, although it is very distant...

--- Page 185 ---
...from the body of the sun, when, during an eclipse, it is found between our eyes and the sun, appears to human eyes to be joined and attached to the sun, because the moon is then positioned against the sun as its background.

**252.** A luminous body will appear more brilliant in proportion to how much it is surrounded by deeper darkness.

**253.** The straight edges of bodies will appear broken when they end in a dark space streaked by the impact of luminous rays.

**254.** Among bodies of equal size and distance, the one that is more brightly illuminated will appear to the eye to be closer and larger.

**255.** If many luminous bodies are seen from a great distance, although they are divided among themselves, they will appear united and joined together.

**256.** If many shaded bodies in close proximity are seen against a luminous background at a great distance, they will appear separated by a large interval.

**257.** Among objects of equal size and color, the one that is farther away will appear lighter and of a smaller shape.

***

**253. 254.** Here again the diagrams in the original have no connection with the text.

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s observations on the deceptive nature of visual perception, specifically how the brightness of an object or its background can distort our sense of its true size and how the geometry of light angles dictates the intensity of illumination on a surface.</meta>

### 258.
<margin>C. 12 a]</margin>

Among objects of equal size, background brightness, and length, the one with the brightest <note>original: "chiara superfītie"; a variant in the manuscript uses "piana," meaning flat or plain</note> surface will appear to be of a larger size. A bar of iron of uniform thickness, half of which is red hot, provides proof of this; the glowing part appears thicker than the rest.

---

### 259.
<margin>C. 8 b]</margin>

Among bodies of equal size, length, shape, and darkness, the one surrounded by the most luminous background will appear the smallest. <note>This describes the "irradiation" effect, where bright light seems to bleed over the edges of dark objects, making them look thinner.</note>

---

### 260.
<margin>C. 8 b]</margin>

**THAT PART OF THE WALL SURFACE WILL BE DARKER OR BRIGHTER DEPENDING ON WHETHER IT IS STRUCK BY A LARGER DARK OR LUMINOUS ANGLE.**

The preceding proposition can be clearly proved in this way. Let us say that *m-q* is the luminous body, and *f-g* will be the opaque <note>original: "corpo öbroso", a body that casts a shadow</note> body; and let *a-e* be the wall surface mentioned above on which these angles fall, showing the nature and character of their bases. 

Then: point *a* will be more luminous than point *b*. The base of the angle at *a* is larger than that at *b*, and it therefore creates a larger angle, which is *a-m-q*. The pyramid <note>Leonardo uses the term "pyramid" to describe the geometric cone of light rays</note> *b-p-m* will be narrower, and *m-o-c* will be even thinner, and so on by degrees. In proportion as they are closer to *e*, the pyramids will become narrower and darker. That portion of the wall will be the darkest where the breadth <note>The text likely concludes with "of the luminous angle is smallest."</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains optical illusions where brightness increases the perceived size of an object and demonstrates geometrically how the angle of light rays determines the intensity of illumination on a wall.</summary>

the pyramid of shadow exceeds the breadth of the pyramid of light.

At point $a$, the pyramid of light will be equal in power to the pyramid of shadow, because the base $fg$ is similar to the base $rf$. And at point $d$, the pyramid of light will be as much thinner than the pyramid of shadow as the base $sf$ is smaller than the base $fg$.

Divide the aforementioned proposition into two diagrams: one with the pyramids of shadow and light, and the other with the pyramids of light alone.

### 261.

Among shadows of equal quality, the one that is nearest to the eye will appear to be of less darkness.

### 262.

The greater the brilliance of the luminous body, the greater will be the darkness of the shadows cast by the bodies illuminated by it.

--- Page 188 ---
PRACTICAL PERSPECTIVE.

At the tip of the pyramid of light is the base of the pyramid of shadow. At the tip of the pyramid of shadow is the base of the pyramid of light. At the tip of the pyramid of light, the pyramid of shadow is equal to the base of the light; and at the tip of the pyramid of shadow, the pyramid of light is equal to the base of the shadow. For just as the shadow is produced by the light, the pyramid of shadow is always directly opposite the pyramid of light and follows the same proportions, as shall be shown in greater detail in the following section.

Objects of equal length, at an equal distance, will appear to be of equal length.

Given the same distance from the light source, those objects that are nearest to the light will be most strongly illuminated, and vice versa.

1. Objects equal in themselves, and at equal distances, will appear equal.
2. Objects equal in themselves, but at unequal distances, the nearest will appear largest.
3. Objects unequal in themselves, but at the same distance, the largest will appear largest.
4. Objects equal in themselves, but at different distances, the furthest away will appear smallest.
5. Objects unequal in themselves, and at unequal distances, the largest will appear largest.
6. Objects unequal in themselves, and at unequal distances, the nearest will appear largest.

<meta>This page introduces Section V of the collection, focusing on Leonardo da Vinci’s "Theory of Colors." It transitions from the previous discussion on the geometry of light and shadow into how those principles apply to the use of color in painting.</meta>

### V.

## *Theory of colors.*

*Leonardo’s theory of colors is even more closely connected to his principles of light and shade than his Perspective of Disappearance <note>original: *Prospettiva de' perdimenti*; Leonardo’s term for the way objects become less distinct and lose color as they move further into the distance.</note>. It is, in fact, merely an appendix or supplement to those principles. We can see this from the titles of sections 264, 267, and 276, while other sections (Numbers 281 and 282) are simply titled "Perspective" <note>original: *Prospettiva*</note>.*

*Only a very few of these chapters are found in the oldest copies and editions of the Treatise on Painting <note>original: *Trattato della Pittura*; a collection of Leonardo's notes on art compiled after his death.</note>. Although the material they provide is sparse and the connections between them are thin, we must still grant them special theoretical value as well as practical usefulness. This is especially true because our knowledge of the theory and use of colors during the Renaissance <note>The era of "rebirth" in European art and learning, roughly from the 14th to the 17th century.</note> is still extremely limited.*

<summary>This page introduces Leonardo da Vinci's theory of colors, explaining that it serves as a supplement to his broader studies on light, shadow, and perspective. It highlights the rarity and importance of these notes for understanding Renaissance artistic techniques.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, color theory, light and shade, Perspective of Disappearance, Renaissance art, Treatise on Painting, optics</keywords>

### 263.

**ON PAINTING.**

The color of an illuminated object is influenced by the color of the light source.

### 264.

**ON SHADOW.**

The surface of every opaque body is influenced by the color of the object facing it.

### 265.

A shadow is always influenced by the color of the object facing it.

### 266.

The image reflected in a mirror is influenced by the color of that mirror.

### 267.

**ON LIGHT AND SHADE.**

Every part of the surface surrounding a body is partially transformed into the color of whatever object is placed opposite to it.

--- Page 192 ---
**EXAMPLE.**

If you place a spherical body among various objects—that is to say, with direct sunlight on one side of it, and on the opposite side a wall illuminated by the sun (which wall may be green or any other color), while the surface on which the sphere rests is red, and the two lateral sides are in shadow—you will see that the natural color of that body will take on the hues of the objects surrounding it. The strongest influence will be the light source; the second will be the illuminated wall; the third will be the shadows. There will still be a portion that takes a tint from the color of the edges.

### 268.

The surface of every opaque body is influenced by the color of the object facing it. But this impression will be stronger or weaker in proportion to whether that object is near or remote, and whether it is of greater or lesser intensity.

### 269.

**OF PAINTING.**

The surface of every opaque body takes on the color of the object facing it.

The surface of an opaque body is colored more intensely by the color of its object in proportion to how much the rays carrying the images of those objects strike the body at more equal angles.

And the surface of an opaque body takes on the color of its object more strongly in proportion to how much whiter that surface is, and how much more luminous or illuminated the color of the object is.

### 270.

**OF THE RAYS WHICH CONVEY THE IMAGES OF BODIES THROUGH THE AIR.**

All the smallest parts of the images penetrate one another without obstructing each other.

--- Page 193 ---
To prove this, let $r$ be one of the sides of the hole, opposite to which let $s$ be the eye that sees the lower end $o$ of the line $n$ $o$. The other extremity cannot transmit its image to the eye $s$ because it would have to strike the edge $r$; the same occurs with point $m$ at the middle of the line. The case is the same regarding the upper extremity $n$ and the eye $u$. If the end $n$ is red, the eye $u$ on that side of the hole $r$ will not see the green color of $o$, but only the red of $n$, according to the seventh proposition of this work, which states: every form projects images of itself along the shortest possible line, which is necessarily a straight line, etc.

---

### 271.

**OF PAINTING.**

The surface of every body takes on the color of the object facing it. The colors of illuminated objects are impressed upon the surfaces of one another in as many different spots as there are variations in the positions of those objects. For example, let $o$ be a blue object in full light which, standing alone, faces the space $b$ $c$ of the white sphere $a$ $b$ $c$ $d$ $e$ $f$; it will tint that space with a blue color. Let $m$ be a yellow object that illuminates the space $a$ $b$ along with the blue object $o$; it will tint that space green, according to the second proposition of this work, which proves that blue and yellow create a very beautiful green, etc. The remainder will be discussed in the Book on Painting. In this book, it will be proven by passing the images of bodies and the colors of objects illuminated by the sun through a small round hole into a dark place onto a flat wall that is itself white, etc.

But everything will be upside down.

---

### 272.

That which casts a shadow does not see it, because shadows are produced by a light source that generates and surrounds them. The shadow caused by the light source $e$, which is yellow,

---

--- Page 194 ---
which is yellow, has a blue tinge, because the shadow of the body $a$ is cast upon the floor at $b$, where it is seen by the blue light; and the shadow produced by the light source $d$, which is blue, will be yellow at the site $c$, because it is seen by the yellow light. The background surrounding these shadows $b$ and $c$ will (in addition to its natural color) be tinted with a color composed of yellow and blue, because it is seen and illuminated by the yellow light and by the blue light at the same time.

Shadows of various colors according to the lights that fall on them. That light which creates the shadow does not see it.

---

### 273.

The edges of every color that pass through apertures are more distinct than their centers. The edges of the images of any color that penetrate through a narrow aperture into a dark place will always be of a more powerful color than their center.

---

### 274.

**ON THE INTERSECTION OF IMAGES IN THE PUPIL OF THE EYE.**

The intersections of the images at the entrance of the pupil do not blend into one another in that space where such an intersection joins them. This is evident because if the rays of the sun pass through two panes of glass that are in contact with each other—one being blue and the other yellow—then the ray penetrating them will not be tinted blue or yellow, but a beautiful green. The same would happen to the eye if the images of the colors yellow and green, at the intersection they make with each other at the entrance of the pupil, had to blend into one another; since this does not happen, such a mixing does not exist.

--- Page 195 ---
275. THEORY OF COLOURS.

## ON THE NATURE OF THE RAYS COMPOSED OF THE IMAGES OF OBJECTS, AND THEIR INTERSECTION.

The straightness of the rays which transmit through the air the form and color of the bodies from which they proceed does not stain the air, nor can the rays affect one another through contact where they intersect. Instead, they only affect the place where they vanish and cease to exist, because that spot faces and is seen by the source of these rays; no other object surrounding that source can be seen from the place where such a ray, being cut off, is destroyed, leaving there the data it has conveyed.

And this is proved by the fourth proposition on the color of bodies, which says: The surface of every opaque body is affected by the color of its object. Therefore, it is concluded that the spot which—by means of the ray carrying the image—faces and is seen by the source of that image, takes on the color of that object.

---

### 275.

## ANY SHADOW CAST BY AN OPAQUE BODY SMALLER THAN THE ORIGINAL LIGHT SOURCE WILL PRODUCE DERIVED SHADOWS TINGED WITH THE COLOR OF THEIR SOURCE.

Let $n$ be the source of the shadow $ef$; it will take on its color. Let $o$ be the source of $he$, which will similarly be tinged by its color; likewise, the color of $vh$ will be tinged by the color of $p$ because it originates from it. The shadow of the triangle $zky$ will be tinged by the color of $q$ because it is derived from it. In the same proportion that $cd$ enters into $ad$, $nrs$ is darker than $m$ and all the rest of the field without shadow. $fg$ is the first degree of light because the entire window $ad$ illuminates it there; similarly, on the opaque body, $me$ is of a similar brightness. $zky$ is a triangle which contains within itself the first...

### ON THE COLORS OF SIMPLE DERIVED SHADOWS
#### <gloss>original: Semplici</gloss>

The colors of derived shadows <note>shadows cast by an object onto a surface, rather than the shadow on the object itself</note> will always be influenced by the colors of the light sources that illuminate the surrounding area. To prove this: let an opaque body be placed between a wall <term>s-c-t-d</term> and two light sources—a blue light <term>d-e</term> and a red light <term>a-b</term>. 

I say that the blue light <term>d-e</term> will illuminate the entire wall <term>s-c-t-d</term> except for the area <term>o-p</term>, which is blocked by the shadow of the opaque body <term>q-r</term>, as shown by the straight lines <term>d-q-o</term> and <term>e-r-p</term>. The same occurs with the red light <term>a-b</term>, which illuminates the entire wall <term>s-c-t-d</term> except for the spot obscured by the shadow of body <term>q-r</term>, as shown by the lines <term>d-q-o</term> and <term>e-r-p</term>. 

From this, we can conclude that the shadow at <term>n-m</term> is exposed to the blue light <term>d-e</term>. However, because the red light <term>a-b</term> cannot reach it, the area <term>n-m</term> remains a blue-tinted shadow set against a red background (which is also mixed with blue, since both lights fall on the rest of the wall <term>s-c-t-d</term>). In these specific shadow areas, only one of the two lights can reach; for this reason, these shadows are of a "medium" depth. If no light at all reached them, they would be shadows of the "maximum" degree <note>total darkness</note>. Furthermore, in the shadow at <term>o-p</term>, the blue light is not visible because the body <term>q-r</term>, by its position...

## 277.

No white or black is transparent.

---

## 278.

OF PAINTING

Because white is not a color, but is the potential recipient of every color, when it is in the high open country all its shadows are blue; and this arises from the fourth [proposition] which says: the surface of every opaque body partakes of the color of its object. Therefore, such white being deprived of the light of the sun by the interposition of some object placed between the sun and that white, all the white that sees the sun and the air remains partaking of the color of the sun and the air; and that part which does not see the sun remains in shadow, partaking of the color of the air. And if such white did not see the green of the countryside all the way to the horizon, nor see the whiteness of that horizon, without doubt that white would appear to be of the simple color which the air shows itself to be.

--- Page 198 ---
### 279.

**On gradations in the depth of colors.**

Since black, when painted next to white, looks no blacker than when it is next to black; and white next to black looks no whiter than it does next to white—as is seen in images transmitted through a small hole or by the edges of any opaque screen...

---

### 280.

#### ON COLORS.

Of several colors of equal whiteness, the one that will appear whitest is the one placed against the darkest background; and black will appear most intense when it is against the whitest background.

And red will appear most vivid when it is against the yellowest background; and the same is true for all colors when they are surrounded by their direct opposite colors.

---

### 281.

#### PERSPECTIVE.

**On the reflection of colors.**

Every object that is devoid of color in itself is colored, either entirely or in part, by the color placed opposite to it. This is seen through experience, because every object that reflects takes on the color of the object facing it. And if the surface that is partially colored is white, the part illuminated by a red reflection will appear red, as will any other color, whether bright or dark.

#### PERSPECTIVE.

Every opaque and colorless body partakes of the color of the object facing it; this happens, for example, with a white wall.

--- Page 199 ---
### 282.

**PERSPECTIVE.**

That side of an object in light and shade which is toward the light transmits the images of its details more distinctly and immediately to the eye than the side which is in shadow.

**PERSPECTIVE.**

The solar rays reflected from a square mirror will be thrown back to distant objects in a circular form.

**PERSPECTIVE.**

Any white and opaque surface will be partially colored by reflections from surrounding objects.

### 283.

**WHAT PORTION OF A COLORED SURFACE OUGHT IN REASON TO BE THE MOST INTENSE.**

If *a* is the light, and *b* is illuminated by it in a direct line, *c*, on which the light cannot fall, is lighted only by reflection from *b* which, let us say, is red. Hence the light reflected from it will be affected by the hue of the surface causing it and will tinge the surface *c* with red. And if *c* is also red, you will see it much more intense than *b*; and if it were yellow, you would see there a color between yellow and red.

### 284.

**WHY BEAUTIFUL COLORS MUST BE IN THE HIGHEST LIGHT.**

Since we see that the quality of color is known only by means of light, it is to be supposed that where there is most light, the true character of a color in light will be best seen; and where there is most shadow, the color will be affected by the tone of that shadow. Therefore, O Painter, remember to show the true quality of colors in the bright lights.

| An object that is painted in white and black will appear with better relief <term>the illusion of three-dimensional depth on a flat surface</term> than any other color. Therefore, I remind you, O Painter, to dress your figures in the lightest colors you can. If you make them in dark colors, they will have little relief and will be inconspicuous from a distance. This is because the shadows of all objects are dark; if you make a garment dark, there will be very little difference between the lights and the shadows, whereas in light colors there will be a great variety of gradients. | An object represented in white and black will display stronger relief than in any other way; hence I would remind you, O Painter, to dress your figures in the lightest colors you can. If you put them in dark colors, they will be in too slight relief and inconspicuous from a distance. The reason is that the shadows of all objects are dark. If you make a dress dark, there is little variety between the lights and shadows, while in light colors there are many grades of light. |

| In bodies covered in shadow and light, the illuminated part shows its true color. | It is the light side of an object in light and shade which shows the true color. |

<margin>On the colors of the rainbow (287. 288).</margin>

<summary>Leonardo explains that light colors provide better three-dimensional relief than dark ones because shadows are naturally dark, and he observes that deep shadows unify all colors into a single dark tone. He also outlines his plan to explain the physics of the rainbow through the study of pigment mixing.</summary>

<keywords>relief, color theory, shadow, lighting, rainbow, art technique, optical perception</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
288.

### WHETHER THE COLOURS OF THE RAINBOW ARE PRODUCED BY THE SUN.

The colours of the rainbow are not produced by the sun, because these colours are generated in many ways without sunshine. This happens, for example, when you hold a glass of water up to your eye; if there are tiny bubbles in the glass—as is common in poorly refined glass—each bubble will produce all the colours of the rainbow on one of its sides, even if the sun is not visible. You can see this by placing such a glass between the open air and your eye so that it is in contact with the eye. The glass should have one part through which the light of the air penetrates, and on the other side, the shadow of the side wall of the window, whether to the right or left, as it does not matter which side it is. By turning the glass around, you will see the aforementioned colours around those bubbles in the glass, etc. We shall discuss the other ways in their proper place.

---

### THAT THE EYE HAS NO PART IN PRODUCING THE COLOURS OF THE RAINBOW.

In the experiment described above, the eye seems to have a part in producing the colours of the rainbow because those bubbles in the glass do not show such colours on their own except through the observation of the eye. However, if you place that glass full of water on the window sill so that the sun's rays strike it from the opposite side, you will then see the aforementioned colours generated in the impression made by the sun's rays that have passed through the glass and ended upon the floor in a dark spot at the foot of the window. Since the eye is not used here, we can clearly state with certainty that the eye has no part in these colours.

--- Page 202 ---
154
THE THEORY OF THE ART OF PAINTING.
[288.

### ON THE COLORS IN THE FEATHERS OF CERTAIN BIRDS.

There are many birds in various regions of the world on whose feathers we see the most beautiful colors produced as they move, as we see in our own country in the feathers of peacocks or on the necks of ducks or pigeons, etc.

Furthermore, on the surface of ancient glass found underground and on the roots of radishes that have remained for a long time at the bottom of springs or other stagnant waters, each of these roots is surrounded by arcs similar to the rainbow. They are also seen in oil spread upon water, and in the solar rays reflected from the surface of a diamond or beryl. Again, through the angle of a beryl, every dark object that borders the air or another bright thing is surrounded by such an arc positioned between the air and the dark object. There are many other ways as well, which I omit because these are sufficient for this discussion.

--- Page 203 ---
## VI.

# ‘*Prospettiva de’ colori*’ (Perspective of Colour)

#### and

# ‘*Prospettiva aerea*’ (Aerial Perspective).

Leonardo clearly distinguishes between these branches of his subject, as can be seen at the beginning of section 295. Attempts have been made to cast doubt on the results Leonardo reached through his experiments on the perspective of color, but these criticisms are unjustified, as is evident from the original text of section 294.

The question regarding the composition of the atmosphere, which is inseparable from any discussion on Aerial Perspective, forms a separate theory that is treated at considerable length. Indeed, the author goes into such detail that we cannot avoid the conclusion that he must have taken particular pleasure in this part of his subject, and that he attached great importance to making it universally applicable.

--- Page 204 ---

<margin>C. 12 b]</margin>

### 289.

<margin>General rules (289–291).</margin>
The variety of colors in objects <note>original Italian: *varietà de' colori de' corpi*</note> cannot be discerned from a great distance, except in those parts that are struck by direct sunlight <note>original: *soli razzi percosse*, literally "struck by solar rays"</note>.

---

<margin>C. 13 a]</margin>

### 290.

Regarding the colors of objects: at long distances, no difference is perceptible in the parts that are in shadow.

---

<margin>Ash. I. 17 b]</margin>

### 291.

**ON THE VISIBILITY OF COLORS.**

Which color is most visible <note>original: *evidēte*</note>? An object at a distance is most conspicuous when it is lightest, and the darkest object is the least visible.

---

<margin>W. 232 b]</margin>

### 292.

On the edges <note>original: *termini*</note> of shadows: some have smoky <note>original: *fumosi*</note> and ill-defined edges, while others have distinct ones.

<margin>An exceptional case.</margin>
No opaque <term>opaque</term><note>An object that does not allow light to pass through it.</note> body can be without light and shadow, unless it is in a mist, on ground covered with snow, or in similar conditions—such as when it is snowing in the open countryside, which has no direct light and is surrounded by darkness.
