--- Page 206 ---
And this occurs only in spherical bodies, because in other bodies which have limbs and parts, those sides of the limbs that face each other reflect the accidental hue and tone of their surfaces onto one another.

---

293.

## ALL COLOURS ARE INDISTINGUISHABLE AND UNDISCERNIBLE AT A DISTANCE.

All colours at a distance are indistinguishable in shadow, because an object that is not touched by the principal light is incapable of transmitting its image to the eye through an atmosphere more luminous than itself, since the lesser brightness is overcome by the greater. For example: when we are inside a house, we can see that all the colours on the surface of the walls are clearly and readily visible when the windows of that dwelling are open; but if we go outside the house and look in through the windows from a short distance to see the paintings on those walls, instead of the paintings we will see a continuous darkness.

---

294.

## HOW THE PAINTER SHOULD PUT THE PERSPECTIVE OF COLOURS INTO PRACTICE.

To put into practice this perspective of varying, losing, or diminishing the essential character of colours, take objects placed in the countryside at intervals of every hundred braccia, such as trees, houses, men, and specific sites. Regarding the first tree, you shall have a very steady pane of glass, and keep your eye just as steady; and on that glass, you shall draw a tree over the form of that tree. Then move it...

--- Page 207 ---
side so far that the real tree is close by the side of the tree you have drawn; then color your drawing in such a way that in color and form the two may be compared with one another, and that both, if you close one eye, seem to be painted on the glass and at the same distance. Then, by the same method, represent a second tree, and a third, at intervals of a hundred braccia one after another. And these will serve as your authorities and guides whenever you work on your own pictures where they apply, and they will enable the work to recede properly. But I have found as a rule that the second is 4/5 the size of the first when it is 20 braccia beyond it.

---

### 295.

### OF AERIAL PERSPECTIVE.

There is another kind of perspective which I call Aerial Perspective, because through the variations in the atmosphere we are able to distinguish the different distances of various buildings that are bounded at their bases by a single line; as, for instance, when we see many buildings beyond a wall, all of which appear above the top of that wall to be of the same size, and you wish in a painting to make one seem more remote than another and to represent a somewhat dense atmosphere. You know that in such an atmosphere the farthest objects seen through it, such as mountains, appear blue—almost the color of the atmosphere itself—because of the great quantity of air that exists between your eye and the mountain when the sun is in the east. Therefore, you should make the first building above the wall its own color; make the one that is more distant less defined and bluer; the one that you wish to be even farther away, make it proportionately bluer; and the one you want to be five times farther away, make it five times bluer. By this rule, the buildings that appear to be the same size above a single line will be clearly distinguished as to which is more distant and which is larger than the others.

--- Page 208 ---
## 296.

The medium lying between the eye and the object seen tinges that object with its own color, just as the blueness of the atmosphere makes distant mountains appear blue, and red glass makes objects seen beyond it look red. The light shed around them by the stars is obscured by the darkness of the night which lies between the eye and the radiant light of those stars.

## 297.

Take care that the perspective of color does not disagree with the size of your objects; that is to say, the colors should diminish from their natural vividness in the same proportion as the objects at various distances diminish from their natural size.

## 298.

**WHY THE ATMOSPHERE MUST BE REPRESENTED AS PALER TOWARDS THE LOWER PORTION.**

Because the atmosphere is dense near the earth, and the higher it is, the thinner it becomes. When the sun is in the East, if you look toward the West and a little way to the South and North, you will see that this dense atmosphere receives more light from the sun than the thinner air does, because the rays meet with greater resistance. And if the sky, as you see it, ends at a low plain, that lowest portion of the sky will be seen through a denser and whiter atmosphere, which will weaken its true color as seen through that medium; there, the sky will look whiter than it does above you, where the line of sight travels through a smaller amount of air charged with heavy vapor. And if you turn to the East, the atmosphere will appear darker as you look lower down, because the luminous rays pass less freely through the lower atmosphere.

## 299.

**ON THE METHOD OF TREATING REMOTE OBJECTS IN PAINTING.**

It is easy to perceive that the atmosphere which lies closest to the level ground is denser than the rest.

--- Page 209 ---
161.

300.] AERIAL PERSPECTIVE.

...is level, and the higher it rises, the thinner and more transparent it becomes. Regarding large and elevated objects that are far away from you, their lower parts will be less visible because you see them along a line that passes through a denser and more continuous section of the atmosphere. The summits of these heights are seen along a line which, although it originates from your eye in the dense air, nevertheless ends at the highest point of the object seen, and thus terminates in air that is much thinner than that at its base. For this reason, the further this line extends from you, it changes quality from point to point, passing through increasingly thinner air. Therefore, you as a painter, when you represent mountains, ensure that from hill to hill the bases are always lighter than the summits; and the further you make them from one another, make the bases lighter. The higher the mountain rises, the more it should show the truth of its form and color.

---

->300.<-

### ON THE COLOR OF THE ATMOSPHERE.

I say that the blue in which the atmosphere shows itself is not its own natural color, but is caused by warm moisture evaporated into tiny and imperceptible atoms. This moisture catches the impact of the solar rays and becomes luminous against the obscurity of the immense darkness of the region of fire which serves as a cover above it. Anyone will see this, as I saw it, who goes up Monboso, a peak of the Alps that divides France from Italy. This mountain has its base giving birth to the four rivers that flow in four opposite directions through all of Europe, and no mountain has its base at such a height. This peak rises to such an altitude that it almost passes all the clouds, and snow rarely falls there, but only hail in summer when the clouds are at their greatest height. This hail is preserved there in such a way that, if it were not for the rarity of clouds falling and rising there—which does not happen twice in a lifetime—there would be an enormous quantity of ice...

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s observations on atmospheric phenomena and light, specifically focusing on his climb of "Monboso" (Mount Rosa) to explain why the sky appears blue.</meta>

<page-num>162</page-num>
<page-num>[300.]</page-num>

->**THE THEORY OF THE ART OF PAINTING**<-

...lifetime, I found a huge mass of ice accumulated from layers of hail. I saw the sky above me was dark, and the sun striking the mountain was far brighter there than in the low plains below, because a thinner layer of atmosphere lay between the summit of the mountain and the sun. 

As a further example of the color of the atmosphere, we can look at the smoke produced by old, dry wood. As it rises from a chimney, it appears to turn a deep blue <note>original: "azzureggiare"</note> when it is positioned between the eye and a dark space. However, as it rises higher and comes between the eye and the bright sky, it immediately appears as an ashy gray color. This happens because it no longer has darkness behind it, but is instead seen against the luminous atmosphere. 

If the smoke comes from young, green wood, it will not appear blue; because it is not transparent and is full of excessive moisture, it acts like a dense cloud that catches distinct lights and shadows as if it were a solid body. The same thing happens with the atmosphere: excessive moisture makes it appear white, while a small amount of moisture infused with heat makes it look dark and deep blue. This should be sufficient for our definition of the color of the atmosphere. 

However, one might also argue that if this transparent blue were the natural color of the atmosphere, it would follow that wherever a larger mass of air existed between the eye and the "element of fire" <note>The "element of fire" refers to the outermost sphere of the Earth's atmosphere in the Aristotelian/Ptolemaic system of the universe</note>, the blue color would be more intense. We see this in blue glass and in sapphires, which appear darker the thicker they are. 

But in this case, the atmosphere behaves in the exact opposite way. Where a greater quantity of air lies between the eye and the sphere of fire, the sky appears much whiter—this is what we see toward the horizon. Conversely, the less atmosphere there is between the eye and the sphere of fire, the deeper the blue appears, even when we are standing on low plains. 

Therefore, it follows as I have said: the atmosphere assumes this blue hue because of the particles of moisture which catch the sun’s rays. We can also see this difference in the particles of dust or smoke in sunbeams that pass through holes <note>original: "spiraculi"</note> in the walls of dark rooms; one beam will appear to be ash-colored...

<summary>Leonardo explains that the sky’s blue color is not its "natural" color but is caused by light hitting moisture particles against a dark background, using observations from a mountain climb and the behavior of smoke to prove his point.</summary>

<page-num>163</page-num>
# AERIAL PERSPECTIVE.

...and thin smoke appears to be a most beautiful blue. This can also be seen in the dark shadows of mountains far away from the eye: the air between the eye and those shadows looks very blue, while the sunlit parts of those mountains do not differ much from their true color. 

If anyone wishes for a final proof, let them paint a board with various colors, including an intense black. Over all of these, apply a very thin and transparent layer of white lead <note>original: "biacca" (lead white pigment)</note>. You will then see that this transparent white will nowhere show a more beautiful blue than over the black—provided the paint is very thin and finely ground.

---

### 301.

<margin>Leicester Codex, 36 a</margin>
Experience shows us that the air appears blue because there is darkness beyond it. If you produce a small amount of smoke from dry wood and let the sun's rays strike it, and then place a piece of black velvet (which the sun does not touch) behind the smoke, you will see that all the smoke between your eye and the darkness appears as a beautiful blue color. 

If you use a white cloth instead of the velvet, the effect is lost: thick smoke hinders it, and thin smoke is not enough to form a perfect blue. Therefore, a moderate amount of smoke produces the finest blue. Similarly, when water is sprayed as a fine mist into a dark room where a sunbeam is admitted, that beam appears blue—especially if distilled water is used and the mist is very fine. I mention this to show that the blueness of the atmosphere is caused by the darkness beyond it. These examples are provided for those who cannot confirm this for themselves through the experience I had on Monboso <note>likely Monte Rosa, where Leonardo observed the darkening of the sky as he climbed higher</note>.

---

### 302.

<margin>F. 18 a</margin>
When smoke from dry wood is seen between the viewer's eye and a dark space, it appears blue. Therefore, the sky looks blue because of the darkness beyond it. And if you look toward the horizon...

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci explains that the blue color of the sky is an optical effect caused by thin, illuminated matter (like air, smoke, or mist) being seen against a dark background. --- Page 212 ---
...of the sky, you will see that the air is not blue, and this is caused by its density. And thus, at every degree that you raise your eyes above the horizon toward the sky over your head, you will find the air becoming darker; this is because a smaller mass of air is interposed between your eye and that darkness. And if you find yourself on top of a high mountain, the air will become so much darker above you as it has become thinner between you and the said darkness; and this will continue at every degree of height, so that in the end, it will remain dark.

That smoke will appear bluest which rises from the driest wood, is nearest to its source, and is seen against a darker background while the light of the sun shines upon it.

### 303.

A dark object will appear bluest when a greater mass of luminous air is interposed between it and the eye, as can be demonstrated by the color of the sky.

### 304.

The air is blue because of the darkness above it, because black and white produce blue.

### 305.

In the morning, the mist is denser toward the heights than at its base because the sun draws it upward; therefore, with tall buildings, even if the summit is as far from you as the foundation, that summit will be hidden from you. For this reason, the sky appears darker toward the heights, while toward the horizon it does not appear blue, but is instead between the color of smoke and dust.

The air filled with mist is entirely deprived of blue, but only appears the color of clouds that look white when the weather is clear; and the more you look toward the west, the darker you will find it, and more lustrous and clear toward the east. The greenery of the fields appears somewhat bluish in a moderate mist, but turns blackish in a thicker one.

--- Page 213 ---
Buildings toward the west show only their illuminated side where the sun reveals them, while the rest is hidden by the mist. When the sun rises and dispels the mist, the hills begin to clear on the side from which the mist departs; they turn blue and seem to steam toward the retreating haze. The buildings then show their light and shadow; through the thinner mist, they show only their highlights, and in the thicker mist, nothing at all. This occurs when the movement of the mist is horizontal; then the edges of the mist will be indistinct against the blue of the sky, and toward the ground, it will look almost like rising dust. The denser the air is, the fewer the buildings of the city and the trees of the countryside will appear, because only the most prominent and largest will be visible.

Darkness tints everything with its own color, and the further an object is removed from that darkness, the more its true and natural color is seen. Mountains will appear few in number because only those with the greatest distances between them will be visible; in such a space, the density of the air increases so much that it creates a brightness which breaks up the darkness of the hills and clears away toward their summits. Between small and nearby hills, there is not as much air interposed, and therefore they are less discernible, especially at their bases.

---

### G. 53 b] 306.

The surface of every body partakes of the color of the light that illuminates it, and of the color of the air that is interposed between the eye and that body—that is, the color of the transparent medium placed between the object and the eye. Among colors of the same quality, the second will be the same color as the first, and this arises from the multiplication of the color of the medium interposed between the object and the eye.

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s observations on atmospheric perspective and how the density of the air affects the perception of color at a distance. It specifically focuses on why certain colors shift toward blue more than others when viewed from afar.</meta>

<page-num>166</page-num>

### 307.

#### ON PAINTING <note>original: "DE PICTURA"</note>
<margin>Manuscript L. 75 b]</margin>

Among various colors that are none of them blue, the one that will look most blue at a great distance is the color nearest to black. Conversely, the color that is least like black will best preserve its own natural color even at a great distance.

Therefore, the green of the fields will change into a bluer hue more than yellow or white will; and conversely, yellow or white will change less than green, and red will change even less than those.

---

### VII.

## *On the Proportions and on the Movements of the Human Figure.*

Leonardo's research on the proportions and movements of the human figure must have been mostly completed and written before the year 1498. We know this because Luca Paciolo, in the dedication to Ludovico il Moro for his book *Divine Proportion* <note>original: *Divina Proportione*</note>, which was published in that year, writes: "Leonardo da Vinci... having already with all diligence brought to an end the worthy book on painting and human movements" <note>original: "Leonardo da venci . . . hauēdo gia cō tutta diligētia al degno libro de pictura e movimenti humani posto fine"</note>.

The selection of Leonardo's axioms contained in the Vatican copy attributes these words to the author: "and the truth will be told in the universal measurement of man" <note>original: "e il recto si dirà nella universale misura del huomo"</note>. (Manzi, p. 147; Ludwig, No. 264). Likewise, Lomazzo, in his *Idea of the Temple of Painting* <note>original: *Idea del Tempio della Pittura*, Milan 1590, chapter IV</note>, says: "Leonardo Vinci... also demonstrated in drawings all the proportions of the members of the human body" <note>original: "Lionardo Vinci . . . dimostrò anco in figura tutte le proporzioni dei membri del corpo umano"</note>.

The Vatican copy includes only a very few sections of the "Universal measurement of man," and until now, nothing has been known of the original <term>manuscripts</term> <note>original: MSS.</note> on the subject which supplied the extensive materials for this portion of the work. The collection at Windsor, belonging to Her Majesty the Queen, includes by far the most important part of Leonardo's investigations on this subject, constituting about half of the total materials published here; the large number of original drawings adds greatly to the interest that the subject itself commands. Luca Paciolo seems to have had these <term>manuscripts</term> (which I have distinguished by the initials <term>W. P.</term> <note>W. P. stands for "Windsor Proportions," referring to the specific group of manuscripts in the Royal Collection</note>) in mind when he wrote the passage quoted above. Still, certain notes of a later date—such as numbers 360,

<summary>This page introduces Leonardo da Vinci’s comprehensive studies on human proportion and movement, noting that most of this work was completed by 1498 and is largely preserved in the Royal Collection at Windsor.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the introductory commentary on Leonardo da Vinci’s research into human proportions and movement, noting that despite earlier claims of completion, he continued to refine these studies alongside his later anatomical work.</meta>

<page-num>168</page-num>

# THE THEORY OF THE ART OF PAINTING.

*Entries 362 and 363, from Manuscript E <term>MS. E</term> <note>original: MS. E; one of Leonardo's notebooks now held at the Institut de France</note>, written in 1513–1514, provide enough evidence to show that Leonardo did not consider his earlier studies on the proportions and movements of the human figure to be final and complete—as one might suppose from Luca Paciolo's <note>modern spelling: Luca Pacioli</note> statement. Alternatively, he may have returned to the subject at a later period, since his earlier research had been conducted in Milan between 1490 and 1500. In fact, it is very likely that the anatomical studies he was pursuing with such passion between 1510 and 1516 led him to reconsider the subject of proportion.*

->[A decorative floral ornament consisting of symmetrical scrollwork and foliage.]<-

## 308.

Every man, at three years old, is half the full height he will eventually reach.

## 309.

If a man of two braccia is too small, and one of four is too tall, the middle path being the most admirable, then the midpoint between two and four is three. Therefore, take a man of three braccia in height and measure him by the rule I will give you. If you should tell me that I might be mistaken by judging a man to be well-proportioned when the opposite is true, I respond to this by saying that you must look at many men of three braccia; from the majority who are consistent in their limbs, choose one of those with the best grace and take your measurements. The length of the hand is one-third of a braccio, and it fits nine times into the man. The face is the same, as is the distance from the pit of the throat to the shoulder, from the shoulder to the nipple, from one nipple to the other, and from each nipple to the pit of the throat.

| <sup>11</sup>dal mento al dirieto del collo *s t* è quel medesimo spatio ch'è infra la bocca e 'l nascimēto de' capegli cioè <sup>12</sup>i tre quarti della testa; | From the chin to the back of the neck *s t* is the same distance as that between the mouth and the hairline, which is three-quarters of the head. |
| <sup>13</sup>dal mēto alla ganascia *v x* è mezza testa ed è simile alla grossezza del collo in proffilo; | From the chin to the jawbone *v x* is half the head and equal to the thickness of the neck in profile. |
| <sup>14</sup>la grossezza del collo entra una volta e 3/4 dal ciglio alla nuca. | The thickness of the head from the brow to the nape is one and three-quarters times that of the neck. |

<page-num>171</page-num>
<header>311—313.] THE PROPORTIONS OF THE HUMAN FIGURE.</header>

### 311.

The distance from the attachment of one ear to the other is equal to the distance from the meeting of the eyebrows to the chin; and in a handsome face, the width of the mouth is equal to the distance from the parting of the lips to the bottom of the chin.

---

### 312.

The indentation or edge of the lower lip of the mouth is halfway between the bottom of the nose and the bottom of the chin.
The face itself forms a square; that is, its width is measured from the outer corner of one eye to the other, and its height is from the very top of the nose to the bottom of the lower lip of the mouth; then, the remaining portions above and below this square are equal to the height of another such square. $a$ $b$ is equal to the space between $c$ $d$; $d$ $n$ is equal to $n$ $c$ in the same way, and likewise $s$ $r$, $q$ $p$, and $h$ $k$ are equal to each other.
The distance between $m$ and $s$ is the same as the distance from the bottom of the nose to the chin. The ear is exactly as long as the nose. The distance from $x$ to $s$ is the same as from the nose to the chin. The parting of the mouth, when seen in profile, is aligned with the angle of the jaw. The ear should be as high as the distance from the bottom of the nose to the top of the eyelid. The space between the eyes is equal to the width of one eye. The ear is positioned over the middle of the neck when seen in profile. The distance from 4 to 5 is equal to the distance from $s$ to $r$.

---

### 313.

$(a$ $b)$ is equal to $(c$ $d)$.

---

**312.** See Plate VIII, No. 1, where the text of lines 3—13 is also provided in facsimile.

**313.** See Plate VII, No. 3. Reference may also be made here to two pen-and-ink drawings of heads in profile with recorded measurements, for which there is no description in the manuscript. These are shown on Plate XVII, No. 2. A head facing left, with part of the torso [W. P. 5a], No. 1 on the same plate, is from MS. A 2b; in the original, it appears on a page with entirely unrelated text concerning matters of natural history. M. RAVAISSON, in his edition of the Paris MS. A, has reproduced this head and discussed it fully [note on page 12]; however, he has somewhat altered the original measurements. The complicated calculations provided by M. RAVAISSON do not seem justified to me. The sketch, as we see it, can hardly have been intended as anything more than an experimental attempt to determine relative proportions. We do not find that Leonardo used circular lines in any other study of the proportions of the human head. At the same time, we see that the proportions of this sketch do not align with the rules he usually observed (see, for example, No. 310).

The width of the shoulders is one-fourth of the man’s height. From the elbow to the tip of the longest finger is the fifth part. From the elbow to the pit of the arm is the eighth part. The whole hand is the tenth part. The beginning of the genitals is the middle of the man. The foot is the seventh part of the man. From the sole of the foot to just below the knee is the fourth part of the man. From just below the knee to the beginning of the genitals is the fourth part of the man. The remaining parts have their own proportional measurements.

is equal to the space between the mouth and the hairline, and is 1/12 of the total height. From the top of the ear to the top of the head is equal to the distance from the bottom of the chin to the tear duct of the eye; it is also equal to the distance from the point of the chin to the angle of the jaw, and this is 1/16 of the total height. The small cartilage that projects over the opening of the ear toward the nose is halfway between the nape of the neck and the eyebrow. The thickness of the neck in profile is equal to the distance from the chin to the eyes, and also to the distance from the chin to the jaw; it fits 15 times into the total height of the man.

### 318.

a b, c d, e f, g h, and i k are equal to each other in size, except for d f, which is variable.

---

### 319.

a n, o, and f are equal to the width of the mouth. a c and a f are equal to the space between one eye and the other. n m, o p, q, and r are equal to half the width of the eyelids—that is, from the inner tear duct of the eye to its outer corner—and likewise the division between the chin and the mouth, and similarly the narrowest part of the nose between the eyes. Each of these individual spaces is the 19th part of the head. n o is equal to the length of the eye or the space between the eyes. m c is 1/3 of n m, measuring from the outer edge of the eyelid to the point c. b s will be equal to the width of the nostrils.

---

### 320.

The distance between the centers of the pupils of the eyes is 1/3 of the face. The space between the outer corners of the eyes toward the ears,

--- Page 228 ---
that is, where the eye ends in the eye socket which contains it—specifically the outer corners—is half the width of the face.

The greatest width of the face at the line of the eyes is equal to the distance from the front hairline to the parting of the lips.

---

### 321.

The nose forms a double square; that is, the width of the nose at the nostrils fits twice into the length from the tip of the nose to the eyebrows. And, in the same way, in profile, the distance from the outer edge of the nostril where it joins the cheek to the tip of the nose is equal to the width of the nose seen from the front, from one nostril to the other. If you divide the entire length of the nose—that is, from its tip to the start of the eyebrows—into four equal parts, you will find that one of these parts extends from the top of the nostrils to the bottom of the tip of the nose; the uppermost part lies between the tear duct of the eye and the start of the eyebrows; and the two middle parts together are equal to the length of the eye from the tear duct to its outer corner.

---

### 322.

The big toe is the sixth part of the foot, measuring in profile on the inside from where the toe begins at the ball of the foot to its tip a b; and it is equal to the distance from the mouth to the bottom of the chin. If you draw the foot in profile from the outside, make the little toe begin at three-quarters of the length of the foot, and you will find the same distance from the start of that toe to the farthest projection of the big toe.

--- Page 229 ---
## 323.

For each man respectively, the distance between $a\ b$ is equal to $c\ d$.

---

## 324.

The foot is as much longer than the hand as the thickness of the arm at the wrist joint where it is thinnest, when viewed from the front.

Furthermore, you will find that the foot is longer than the hand by the same distance that exists from the inner attachment of the little toe to the farthest projection of the big toe, when taking the measurement along the straight length of the foot.

The palm of the hand without the fingers fits twice into the length of the foot without the toes.

If you hold your hand with your fingers straight and pressed together, you will find it to be as wide as the greatest width of the foot—that is, where it joins with the toes.

And if you measure from the tip of the inner ankle bone to the tip of the big toe, you will find this measurement to be as long as the entire hand.

From the top of the ankle joint to the attachment of the toes on the upper side is the same distance as from the wrist joint to the tip of the thumb.

The narrowest width of the hand is equal to the narrowest width of the foot between its attachment to the leg and the beginning of the toes.

The width of the heel at its bottom is equal to the thickness of the arm where it joins the hand on the inside.

--- Page 230 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s detailed anatomical observations on the proportions of the human body, specifically focusing on how the measurements of the foot and toes relate to the hand and face.</meta>

# THE THEORY OF THE ART OF PAINTING. [325–327.]

And similarly <note>original: *E simile*</note>, the leg is at its thinnest when viewed from the front.

The length of the longest toe—from where it first branches off from the big toe to its tip—is one-fourth the length of the foot (measuring from the center of the inner ankle bone to the toe's tip). This length is equal to the width of the mouth. The distance between the mouth and the chin is equal to the distance between the knuckles of the three middle fingers and their first joints when the hand is spread open. This same distance is equal to the length from the joint of the thumb to the beginning of its nail when extended; this measurement represents one-fourth of the hand and one-fourth of the face.

The space between the outer points of the inner and outer ankle bones <note>original: *poli dentro e fori de' piedi*</note>—referred to as the ankles <term>ankles</term> <note>original: *talloni ovvero nocche- o burelle del piè*</note> marked $a$ and $b$—is equal to the distance between the mouth and the tear duct <note>original: *lagrimatojo*</note> of the eye.

## 325.

W. P. 4b]

<margin>Relative proportions of the foot and of the face (325–327).</margin>
The foot, from where it attaches to the leg to the tip of the big toe, is equal to the distance from the top of the chin to the hairline (marked $a$–$b$); this is also equal to five-sixths of the height of the face.

## 326.

W. P. 7a]

The distance $a$–$d$ is equal to the length of one head; $c$–$b$ is also equal to the length of one head. The four smaller toes are all of equal thickness from the top of the nail to the bottom, and their thickness is 1/13th of the total length of the foot.

## 327.

W. P. 3, II]

The entire length of the foot fits exactly into the space between the elbow and the wrist joint, and also between the elbow and the inner crease of the arm where it meets the chest <note>original: *diuerso la poppa*</note>.

<header>THE PROPORTIONS OF THE HUMAN FIGURE.</header>

### 328.

<margin>Proportions of the leg (328—331).</margin>

The greatest thickness of the calf <note>original: *polpa della gāba*</note> of the leg is at one-third of its height *a b*, and it is one-twentieth wider than the greatest width of the foot. 

*a c* is half a head, and is equal to *d b* and to the attachment <note>original: *appiccatura*</note> of the five toes *e f*. *d k* decreases by one-sixth in the leg *g h*. *g h* is 1/3 of a head; *m n* increases by one-sixth from *a e* and is 7/12 of a head; *o p* is 1/10 less than *d k* and is 6/17 of a head. *a* is located at half the distance between *b* and *q*, and is 1/4 of the man’s total height. *r* is halfway between *s* and *b* [11]. The hollow <note>original: *cauo*</note> of the knee on the outside *r* is higher than the hollow on the inside *a*. One half of the thickness of the leg at the foot; *r* is found midway between the prominence <note>original: *gobbo*</note> *s* and the ground *b*; *v* is halfway between *t* and *b*. 

The thickness of the thigh seen from the front is equal to the greatest width of the face—that is, 2/3 of the space from the chin to the top of the head; *z r* is 5/6 of *7 v*. *m n* is equal to *7 v* and is 1/4 of *r b*; *x y* fits 3 times into *r b* and into *r s*.

*a b* fits into *c f* six times, and six times into *c n*, and is equal to *g h | i k*. *l m* fits 4 times into *d f*, and 4 times into *d n*, and is 3/7 of the foot. *p q r s* fits 3 times into *d f* and 3 times into *b n*. *x y* is 1/8 of *x f* and is equal to *n q*. The measurement 3 7 is 1/9 of *n f*; 4 5 is 1/10 of *n f* [27].

---

**328.** The drawing of a leg seen from the front in Plate XIII, No. 1 corresponds to the text in lines 3–21. The measurements in this section should be compared with the text in No. 331, lines 1–13, and the sketch of a leg seen from the front on Plate XV.

I want to know how much a man increases in height by standing on tiptoe, and how much $p \ g$ decreases by stooping; and how much it increases at $n \ q$, as well as in the bending of the foot.

$e \ f$ is four times the distance between the genitals and the sole of the foot; $3 \ 7$ is six times the distance from 3 to 2 and is equal to $g \ h$ and $i \ k$.

### 329.

The length of the foot from the end of the toes to the heel fits twice into the distance from the heel to the knee—that is, where the leg bone [fibula] joins the thigh bone [femur].

---

### 330.

$a \ n \ b$ are equal; $c \ n \ d$ are equal; $n \ c$ makes two feet; $n \ d$ makes 2 feet.

---

### 331.

$m \ n \ o$ are equal. The narrowest width of the leg seen from the front fits 8 times from the sole of the foot to the knee joint, and is the same width as the arm seen from the front at the wrist, as the longest measurement of the ear, and as the three primary divisions into which the face is divided; and this measurement fits 4 times from the wrist joint to the point of the elbow. The foot is as long as the space of the knee between $a$ and $b$; and the kneecap [patella] is as long as the leg between $r$ and $s$.

The narrowest thickness of the leg in profile fits 6 times from the sole of the foot

In kneeling down, a man will lose one-fourth of his height.

### [Fragment 1 - Top Right]

<margin>Anatomical sketch of a human head in profile with lines of vision</margin>

Let the eyeballs <note>original: "palle della vista" — literally "balls of sight"</note> be equal—almost dark and distorted.

<margin>Text above the head, oriented at a 90-degree angle</margin>

The line of any object that passes through the center of that eyeball reaches the center of the eye without passing through the true convergence of the visual rays <note>original: "raggi della vista"</note>.

---

### [Fragment 2 - Center Left]

<margin>Geometric or optical study of perspective</margin>

If the eye is placed in the middle of two parallel lines, it is an infallible truth that they will appear to unite at the same point between them at the distance of a bow-shot <term>bow-shot</term><note>original: "tratto d'arco"; a common Renaissance unit of distance based on the range of an arrow.</note>.

<margin>Vertical text to the right of the center block</margin>

Let the walls <note>original: "pariete"</note> of the staircase be hollow on every side.

<margin>Short note to the right</margin>

The method of proving.

---

<note>Markers: n, o, p, q, r, s, t, x, y, z, +, S</note>

...
let the wall
be made
straight
by its
angles.

<page-num>181</page-num>
<header>339—342.] THE PROPORTIONS OF THE HUMAN FIGURE. 181</header>

<margin>W. P. 7b] <note>Windsor Papers, folio 7b</note></margin>

### 339.

The ear canal <note>original: "buco dell’ orecchio"</note>, the point where the shoulder begins, the hip, and the ankle are all aligned on a perpendicular line; the distance from $a$ to $n$ is equal to the distance from $m$ to $o$.

<note>original: "Il buco dell’ orecchio, la nascita della spalla, quella del fiāco e del piè son perpēdiculare linia; a n · è simile a · m · o ·"</note>

---

<margin>C. A. 350a; 1089a] <note>Codex Atlanticus</note></margin>

### 340.

From the chin to the hairline <note>original: "nascimēto de’ capelli"</note> is 1/10th of the whole figure. From the joint of the wrist to the tip of the middle finger is 1/10th. From the chin to the top of the head is 1/8th; and from the pit of the throat <note>original: "forciella"</note> to the top of the chest is 1/6th, and from the pit of the throat to the top of the head is 1/4th.

From the chin to the nostrils is 1/3rd of the face; the same distance exists from the nostrils to the eyebrow and from the eyebrow to the hairline. The foot is 1/6th of the height, the forearm <note>original: "gomito" — literally "elbow," but here referring to the length of the lower arm</note> is 1/4th, and the width of the shoulders is 1/4th.

---

<margin>W. P. 5a]</margin>

### 341.

The width of the shoulders is 1/4th of the total height. From the shoulder joint to the hand is 1/3rd. From the edge of the lower lip to the shoulder blade <note>original: "omero"</note> is one foot.

The greatest thickness of a man, measured from the chest to the back, fits 8 times into his total height and is equal to the distance from the chin to the top of the head. The greatest width is at the shoulders, which fits 4 times into the total height.

---

<margin>W. P. 7b]</margin>

### 342.

A man’s width under the arms is the same as the width across his hips.

<margin>The torso from the front and back.</margin>

A man’s width across the hips is equal to the distance from the top of the hips to the bottom of the buttocks, when he stands with his weight distributed equally...

---

<summary>This page details Leonardo da Vinci's precise mathematical proportions for the human body, defining measurements for the face, limbs, and torso as fractions of total height. These rules provided Renaissance artists with a scientific framework for achieving realistic anatomical symmetry.</summary>
<keywords>human proportions, anatomical measurements, Leonardo da Vinci, facial proportions, shoulder width, Vitruvian principles, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 252 ---
...equally balanced on both feet; and there is the same distance from the top of the hips to the shoulder joint. The waist, or the area above the hips, will be halfway between the shoulder joints and the bottom of the buttocks.

# 343.

Vitruvius' scheme of proportions.

Vitruvius the architect states in his work on architecture that the measurements of the human body are distributed by Nature in this manner: that is, 4 fingers make 1 palm, and 4 palms make 1 foot; 6 palms make 1 cubit; 4 cubits make a man’s height, and 4 cubits make one pace, and 24 palms make a man; and these measurements are used in his buildings. If you open your legs so much that you lower your head by 1/14 of your height, and spread and raise your arms until your long fingers touch the line of the top of your head, know that the center of the extremities of the outspread limbs will be the navel, and the space found between the legs will be an equilateral triangle.

A man’s wingspan is equal to his height.

From the hairline to the bottom of the chin is one-tenth of a man's height; from the bottom of the chin to the top of the head is one-eighth of a man's height; from the top of the chest to the top of the head will be one-sixth of a man. From the top of the chest to the hairline will be the seventh part of the whole man; from the nipples to the top of the head will be the fourth part of a man. The greatest width of the shoulders contains within itself the fourth part of a man; from the elbow to the tip of the hand will be the fifth part of a man; from that elbow to the end of the shoulder will be the eighth part of that man. The whole hand will be the tenth part of the man.

--- Page 253 ---
Vitruvius the architect states in his work on architecture that the measurements of the human body are distributed by Nature in this manner: that is, 4 fingers make 1 palm, and 4 palms make 1 foot; 6 palms make 1 cubit; 4 cubits make a man’s height, and 4 cubits make one pace, and 24 palms make a man; and these measurements are used in his buildings. If you open your legs so much that you lower your height by 1/14, and spread and raise your arms until your long fingers touch the line of the top of your head, know that the center of the extremities of the outspread limbs will be the navel, and the space found between the legs will be an equilateral triangle.

---

-> [Illustration: The Vitruvian Man] <-

---

A man’s wingspan is equal to his height. From the hairline to the bottom of the chin is one-tenth of a man's height; from the bottom of the chin to the top of the head is one-eighth of his height; from the top of the chest to the top of the head will be one-sixth of his height. From the top of the chest to the hairline will be the seventh part of the whole man. From the nipples to the top of the head will be the fourth part of the man. The greatest width of the shoulders contains within itself the fourth part of the man. From the elbow to the tip of the hand will be the fourth part of the man; from that elbow to the end of the shoulder will be the eighth part of that man. The whole hand will be the tenth part of the man; the penis originates at the center of the man. The foot will be the seventh part of the man. From the bottom of the foot to below the knee will be the fourth part of the man. From below the knee to the origin of the penis will be the fourth part of the man. The parts found between the chin and the nose, and the hairline and the eyebrows—each space being equal to the other—are the same as the ear and are one-third of the face.

The beginning of the genitals marks the middle of the man. The foot is the seventh part of the man. From the sole of the foot to below the knee is the fourth part of the man. From below the knee to the beginning of the genitals is the fourth part of the man. The distance from the bottom of the chin to the nose, and from the hairline to the eyebrows, is in each case the same; like the ear, each is a third of the face.

### 344.

From *b* to *a* is one head, as is the distance from *c* to *a*; this occurs when the elbow forms a right angle.

---

### 345.

From the tip of the longest finger of the hand to the shoulder joint is four hands or, if you prefer, four faces. *a*, *b*, and *c* are equal, and each interval is two heads.

---

### 346.

The length of the hand, from the tip of the longest finger to the wrist joint, fits four times into the distance from the tip of the longest finger to the shoulder joint.

---

### 347.

*a*, *b*, and *c* are equal to each other, to the foot, and to the space between the nipple and the navel. *d-e* is the third part of the whole man. *f-g* is the fourth part of a man, is equal to *g-h*, and measures one cubit.

--- Page 256 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of human proportions, focusing specifically on the measurements and anatomical changes of the arm during movement and bending.</meta>

<page-num>184</page-num>
<header>THE THEORY OF THE ART OF PAINTING. [348.]</header>

<margin>W. P. 5a]</margin>

### 348.

A-B fits 4 times into A-C and 9 times into A-M.
The greatest thickness of the arm between the elbow and the hand fits 6 times into A-M and is equal to R-F.
The greatest thickness of the arm between the shoulder and the elbow fits 4 times into C-M and is equal to H-N-G.
The smallest thickness of the arm above the elbow, X-Y, is not a square root <note>original: *radice quadrata*; likely referring to a specific geometric ratio</note>, but is equal to half the space H-3 which is found between the inner joint of the arm and the wrist joint.

The thickness of the arm at the hand <note>original: *grossezza... sulla mano*; referring to the wrist</note> fits 12 times into the whole arm—that is, from the tip of the fingers to the shoulder joint. This is divided as 3 parts in the hand and 9 in the arm.
The arm, when bent, measures 4 heads.
The arm from the shoulder to the elbow increases in length when it is bent; specifically, the length from the shoulder to the elbow grows. This increase is equal to the thickness of the arm at the wrist when seen in profile. It is also equal to the space between the bottom of the chin and the opening of the mouth, the thickness of the two middle fingers, the width of the mouth, the space from the hairline on the forehead to the top of the head, and the very top of the head. These mentioned parts are equal to each other, but they are not equal to the previously mentioned increase in the arm <note>The author notes that while these facial and hand measurements are equal to one another, they differ from the specific amount the arm lengthens when bent.</note>.
The arm from the elbow to the hand never increases in length by being bent or straightened.
The arm from the shoulder to the inner joint when it is extended:
When the arm is extended, P-N is equal to N-A. When it is bent, N-A decreases by 1/6 of its length, and P-N does the same. The outer elbow increases by 1/7 when bent, and through this bending, it grows to reach a total length of 2 heads.

***

**29.** *These things.* This passage seems to have been written specifically to correct the preceding lines. The error is clarified by the accompanying sketch of the bones of the arm.

--- Page 259 ---
On the side, by bending, it is found that while the arm from where it joins the side to the wrist was 2 and a half heads long, in bending it loses that half head and measures only two: one from the shoulder joint to the end at the elbow, and the other from there to the hand.

The arm, when folded, will measure 2 faces from the top of the shoulder to the elbow, and 2 from that elbow to the base of the four fingers on the palm of the hand. The length from the base of the fingers to the elbow never changes, regardless of any movement of the arm.

If this arm is straightened, it will decrease by 1/3 of the distance between $b$ and $n$; and if, being straight, it is then bent, it will increase by half of $o \ c$. The length from the shoulder to the elbow is the same as the length from the base of the thumb on the inside to that same elbow, $a \ b \ c$.

The smallest thickness of the arm in profile, $z \ c$, fits 6 times between the knuckles of the hand and the dimple of the extended elbow, 14 times into the whole arm, and 42 times into the entire height of the man. The greatest thickness of the arm in profile is equal to the greatest thickness of the arm from the front; however, one is located at a third of the distance from the shoulder joint to the elbow, and the other at a third of the distance from the elbow to the hand.

--- Page 260 ---
### 349.

The distance from the top of the shoulder to the point of the elbow is the same as the distance from that point to the joints where the four fingers meet the palm of the hand, and each section is equal to 2 faces.

$a \ e$ is equal to the palm of the hand; $r \ f$ and $o \ g$ are equal to half a head, and each fits 4 times into $a \ b$ and $b \ c$. From $c$ to $m$ is $1/2$ of a head; $m \ n$ is $1/3$ of a head and fits 6 times into $c \ b$ and into $b \ a$. $a \ b$ loses $1/7$ of its length when the arm is extended; $c \ b$ never changes; $o$ will always be the midpoint between $a$ and $s$.

$y \ l$ is the fleshy part of the arm and measures one head; when the arm is bent, this fleshy part shrinks by $2/5$ of its length. In bending, $o \ a$ loses $1/6$, and $o \ r$ does the same.

$a \ b$ is $1/7$ of $r \ c$. $f \ s$ is $1/8$ of $r \ c$, and each of these two measurements represents the thickest part of the arm. $k \ h$ is the thinnest part between the shoulder and the elbow, and it is $1/8$ of the whole arm $r \ c$. $o \ p$ is $1/5$ of $r \ l$; $c \ z$ fits 13 times into $r \ c$.

### 350.

In the innermost folds of the joints of any limb, the raised areas are converted into hollows; likewise, every hollow of these innermost folds becomes a convexity when the limb is straightened to its fullest extent. Very great mistakes are often made in this regard by those who lack sufficient knowledge and trust too much to their own invention, failing to resort to the imitation of nature. These variations occur more in the middle of the sides than at the front, and more at the back than at the sides.

### 351.

When the arm is bent at an angle at the elbow, it will produce an angle of some kind; the more acute this angle becomes, the more the muscles inside the bend will be shortened.

### 355.

Observe the change in the position of the shoulder in all movements of the arm: going up and down, inwards and outwards, backwards and forwards, as well as in circular movements and any other motions.

Do the same regarding the neck, hands, feet, and the chest above the hips, etc.

### 356.

There are three principal muscles of the shoulder, namely $b$, $c$, and $d$; and there are two lateral muscles that move it forward and backward, namely $a$ and $o$. Muscle $a$ moves it forward, and $o$ pulls it back. Muscles $b$, $c$, and $d$ raise it; $a$, $b$, and $c$ move it upwards and forwards, while $c$, $d$, and $o$ move it upwards and backwards. Its own weight is almost sufficient to move it downwards.

Muscle $d$ acts in conjunction with muscle $c$ when the arm moves forward; and when moving backward, muscle $b$ acts in conjunction with muscle $c$.

### 357.

### CONCERNING THE LOWER BACK, WHEN BENT.

When the lower back or spine is bent, the breasts are always lower than the shoulder blades of that back.

When the chest is arched, the breasts are always higher than the shoulder blades of the back.

When the lower back is straight, the breasts will always be found at the same level as those shoulder blades.

### 358.

$a$ and $b$, the tendon and the ankle bone, move a finger’s breadth closer to each other when the heel is raised; when the heel is lowered, they separate by a finger’s breadth.

### 359.

Just as much as the part of the nude figure from $d$ to $a$ decreases due to the posture, the opposite part increases by the same amount; that is: in the same proportion that the measurement of part $d$ to $a$ diminishes, the opposite upper part increases beyond its normal measurement. The navel never changes its height relative to the male organ; and this lowering occurs because when a figure rests its weight on one foot, that foot becomes the center of the weight placed above it. This being the case, the middle of the shoulders is positioned directly above that foot, moving out of its perpendicular line—the line that passes through the center of the body's surface. This line becomes curved at its upper end so that it stays above the supporting foot, and the horizontal lines of the body are forced into angles where their ends are lower on the side that bears the weight, as shown at $a$, $b$, and $c$.

---

### 360.

## PAINTING.

Observe how the limbs and their joints vary during the movements and poses of figures; for the shoulder blades, during the movements of the arms and shoulders, significantly alter the appearance of the back. You will find all the causes of this explained in my book of anatomy.

---

### 361.

## ON POSE.

The pit of the throat is positioned directly over the foot; however, by extending one arm forward, the pit of the throat moves away from the line of that foot. If the leg is extended backward, the pit of the throat moves forward, and thus it changes with every pose.

--- Page 268 ---
### 362.

PAINTING.

Describe which are the muscles and which are the tendons that, through the various movements of each limb, become visible or hidden, or do neither. And remember that these indications of action are of the utmost importance and necessity for painters and sculptors who claim to be masters, etc.

And you shall do the same for a child, from its birth until the time of its extreme old age, through all the stages of its life, such as infancy, childhood, adolescence, youth, etc.

And in all of these, you will describe the changes in the limbs and joints—which ones grow fat and which ones grow thin.

---

### 363.

O anatomical painter! Beware lest too much knowledge of the bones, tendons, and muscles causes you to become a "wooden" painter by wanting your nude figures to display all their anatomical details. Therefore, in seeking to remedy this, observe how the muscles in old or thin people cover or clothe their bones; and besides this, note the rule of how these same muscles fill the surface spaces that lie between them. Note which muscles never lose their prominence regardless of the degree of fatness, and which muscles lose the visibility of their contact points at the slightest bit of plumpness. Many times, several muscles appear as a single muscle as one grows fat, and many times, as one grows thin or old, a single muscle divides into several muscles. In this discourse, all their specific details will be demonstrated in their proper place, and especially regarding the spaces located between the joints of each limb, etc.

--- Page 269 ---
Again, do not fail to observe the variations in the forms of the aforementioned muscles around the joints of any animal's limbs, caused by the different motions of each limb. On some sides of those joints, the visibility of these muscles is completely lost due to the increase or decrease of the flesh of which these muscles are composed, etc.

---

### 364.

**ON THE DIFFERENT MEASUREMENTS OF BOYS AND MEN.**

There is a great difference in the length between the joints in men and boys. In a man, the distance from the shoulder joint to the elbow, from the elbow to the tip of the thumb, and from one shoulder to the other, is in each instance two heads long. In a boy, however, it is only one head, because Nature first constructs the housing for the intellect before forming the parts that contain the vital elements.

---

### 365.

**ON PAINTING.**

Which muscles subdivide in old age, or in young people who become thin? Which parts of the limbs of the human frame never increase in flesh regardless of fatness, and never decrease in flesh regardless of leanness?

The answer to this question will be found at all the external joints of the bones, such as the shoulder, elbow, wrists, finger joints, hips, knees, ankle bones, toes, and similar parts; all of which will be discussed in their proper places. The greatest thickness acquired by any limb occurs at the part of the muscle that is farthest from its attachments.

Flesh never increases on those parts of the bones that are close to the surface of the limbs.

--- Page 270 ---
At *b r d a c e f*, the increase or decrease of the flesh never makes a significant difference. Nature has placed on the front of man all those parts which, when struck, would cause him to feel pain; this is felt in the shins, the forehead, and the nose. This was done for the preservation of man, because if such pain were not prepared in those members, the many blows received by such parts would certainly be the cause of their destruction.

Describe why the bones of the arms and legs are double near the hands and feet.

Where the flesh increases or decreases in the folds of the limbs.

---

# 366.

## ON PAINTING.

Every part of a whole should be proportioned to its whole. For example, if a man has a thick and short figure, every one of his members should be the same: that is, short and thick arms, wide and thick hands, and short fingers with their joints in the same manner, and so on with the rest. I intend the same to be said of all animals and plants, both in the proportional reduction of their thicknesses and in their enlargement.

---

# 367.

## ON THE AGREEMENT OF THE LIMBS.

And again, I remind you to take great care in giving limbs to your figures so that they appear consistent with the size of the body and also with its age. That is, to young people give limbs with few muscles and veins, a delicate surface, and rounded forms of a pleasing color. To men, let them be sinewy and full of muscles. To old men, let the surface be wrinkled, rough, and veiny, with the sinews very evident.

--- Page 271 ---
## HOW YOUNG BOYS HAVE THEIR JOINTS THE REVERSE OF MEN IN TERMS OF SIZE.

Little children have all their joints slender, while the spaces between them are thick. This happens because only the skin covers the joints without any other flesh; it has the nature of sinew, which binds and ties the bones together. The fatty fleshiness is found between one joint and the next, contained between the skin and the bone. But because the bones are thicker at the joints than between the joints, as a man grows, the flesh loses that excess which was between the skin and the bone; consequently, the skin clings closer to the bone and the limbs become more slender. However, because there is nothing over the joints but the cartilaginous and sinewy skin, it cannot dry up, and by not drying up, it does not diminish. For these reasons, children are slender at the joints and thick between the joints, as seen in the joints of the fingers, arms, and shoulders, which are slender and have dimpled hollows; a man, on the contrary, is thick in all the joints of the fingers, arms, and legs, and where children have hollows, men have prominences.

---

### 368.

The method of representing the 18 actions of man: stability, movement, running, standing upright, leaning, sitting, bending, kneeling, lying down, suspended, carrying, being carried, pushing, pulling, striking, being struck, pressing down, and lifting up.
[Regarding how a figure should stand with a weight in its hand. Remember.]

---

### 369.

A person who is sitting cannot stand up unless the part of the body in front of the axis weighs more than the part behind that axis, without using the strength of the arms.

--- Page 272 ---
Anyone climbing a slope must naturally shift more weight forward onto the higher foot than behind—that is, in front of the center of gravity rather than behind it. Therefore, a person will always naturally shift more weight toward the direction they wish to move than in any other direction.

The faster a person runs, the more they lean toward the destination and throw more weight in front of their center of gravity than behind it. Someone running downhill shifts their center of gravity onto their heels, while someone running uphill shifts it to the balls of the feet; a person running on level ground shifts it first to the heels and then to the balls of the feet.

This person cannot carry their own weight unless, by leaning the body backward, they balance the weight in front, so that the foot on which they stand remains the center of gravity.

---

### 370.

How a person proceeds to stand up when sitting on level ground.

---

### 371.

A person’s head moves faster than their feet while walking.
A person walking across a long, level plain first leans somewhat backward and then just as far forward.

---

### 372.

A person throws less weight on their legs when running than when standing still. In the same way, a running horse feels the weight of its rider less. Consequently, many people find it remarkable that, while running, a horse can support itself on a single spot.

---

**371. 3—6.** He strides forward with the manner of a person going downhill; when tired, on the contrary, he walks like someone going uphill.

this loads onto the opposite leg all the rest of the bulk of the entire man, including the weight of the other leg; he then raises the other leg and sets the foot upon the step to which he wishes to raise himself. The weight that a man carries on his shoulders.

--- Page 279 ---
The person who straightens the angle made by the thigh where it joins the body has less difficulty and has less weight to lift, because they lack the additional weight of the thigh itself. Besides this, they have stronger muscles, specifically those which form the buttocks.

### 379.

A man walking downhill takes small steps because the weight remains upon the rear foot; a man climbing takes large steps because his weight rests on the forward foot.

### 380.

**ON THE HUMAN BODY IN ACTION.**

When you want to represent a man moving some weight, consider that the movements are to be performed along different lines: that is, either from low to high with a simple motion, like a man who stoops to pick up a weight which he intends to lift as he straightens up; or when a man wants to crush something behind him, or push it forward, or pull it downward with a rope passing through pulleys. Here, remember that a man’s weight pulls in proportion to how far his center of gravity is outside the center of his support. To this is added the force exerted by the legs and the bent back as they straighten.

### 381.

A man also has a greater amount of strength in his legs than is required for his own weight. To prove this is true, have a man stand on soft sand and then place another man on his back, and you will see how much deeper he sinks. Then take the man off his back and have him jump straight up as high as he can; you will find that the print of his feet has sunk deeper from the jump than it did with the man on his back. Therefore, by these two methods, it is proved that a man has twice the strength required to support himself.

--- Page 280 ---
# 382.

### ON PAINTING.

If you have to represent a man who is moving, or lifting, or pulling, or carrying a weight equal to his own, in what way must you position his legs beneath his body?

---

# 383.

### ON THE STRENGTH OF MAN.

A man pulling a weight balanced against himself cannot pull more than his own weight. And if he has to lift it, he will be able to lift as much more than his weight as his strength exceeds that of other men. The greatest force a man can apply, with equal speed and momentum, is when he sets his feet on one end of a balance or lever and then presses his shoulders against some stable object. This will lift a weight at the opposite end of the balance equal to his own weight, plus as much weight as he can carry on his shoulders.

---

# 384.

No animal can move a weight by its own mass that is greater than the portion of its weight located outside the center of its support.

---

# 385.

A man who wants to shoot an arrow a very long distance with a bow must stand entirely on one foot, raising the other far enough from the standing foot to provide the necessary counterweight to his body as it is thrown forward over the leading foot. He must not hold his arm fully extended; and so that he may better endure the strain, he should hold a piece of wood against the bow which...

--- Page 281 ---
extends from the grip to the shoulder. And if he wishes to shoot a very long distance, he must stand on the tips of his toes, and as the arrow is released, he must jump forward at the same moment.

### 386.

When two men are at the opposite ends of a plank that is balanced, and they are of equal weight, if one of them wants to make a leap into the air, his leap will be directed downward from his end of the plank. The man will never rise upward but will remain in his place until the man at the other end strikes the board back against his feet.

### 387.

On delivering a blow to the right or left.

---

### 388.

Why can an impetus not be spent immediately, but instead diminishes gradually along any given line? The impetus acquired along the line a b c d is spent along the line d e, but not so completely that some of its power does not remain. When the motion of the line d e and the force of its mover are added to this remaining power, the impetus of the blow must necessarily be multiplied, becoming greater than the simple impetus produced by the motion of d e alone.

A man who has to deliver a powerful blow with his weapon prepares himself with all his strength on the side opposite to the spot he intends to hit; this is because an object that moves with greater velocity exerts more power against the object that impedes its motion.

--- Page 284 ---
## 389.

**On hair falling down in curls.**

Observe the motion of the surface of the water, which resembles that of hair. Hair has two motions: one depends on the weight of the locks, and the other on the direction of the curls. Similarly, water has its eddying swirls, one part of which follows the momentum of the main current, while the other follows the incidental motion and the return flow.

---

## 390.

**On draperies.**

**OF THE NATURE OF THE FOLDS IN DRAPERY.**

That part of a fold which is farthest from the points where it is constrained will return most nearly to its original state. By nature, everything seeks to maintain its own condition. Because cloth is of equal density and thickness on both its front and back sides, it naturally tends to lie flat. Therefore, when a fold or pleat forces it to leave that flatness, it obeys the nature of the force at the point where it is most constrained; however, you will find that the part farthest from these constraints returns most to its original nature—that is, remaining spread out and wide.

**EXAMPLE.**

Let *a b c* be the fold of the cloth mentioned above. Let *a c* be the places where this folded cloth is held fast. I proposed to you that the part of the cloth farthest from the constrained ends would return most to its original nature. Therefore, since *b* is farthest from *a* and *c*, the fold *a b c* will be wider there than in any other place.

--- Page 285 ---
Observe the motion of the surface of the water, which behaves like hair; hair has two motions, one of which depends on the weight of the strands, and the other on the direction of their curls. Likewise, water has its eddying swirls, one part of which is governed by the weight of its current, while the other part is governed by the direction of its backflow.
