--- Page 292 ---
It will be easily understood that the sequence of disconnected notes, as they occurred to Leonardo and were written down from time to time, might be hardly satisfactory as a systematic arrangement of his principles. The reader will find in the Appendix an exact account of the order of the chapters in the original manuscript and, from the data provided there, can restore them at will. As the materials are arranged here, the structure of the tree regarding the growth of the branches comes first (394—411), followed by the attachment of the leaves on the stems (412—419). Then follow the laws of Light and Shade as applied, first, to the leaves (420—434), and, secondly, to the whole tree and to groups of trees (435—457). After the remarks on Light and Shade in landscapes generally (458—464), we find special observations on the lighting of views of towns and buildings (465—469). To the theory of Landscape Painting also belong the passages on the effect of Wind on Trees (470—473) and on the Light and Shade of Clouds (474—477), since we find in these certain comparisons with the effect of Light and Shade on Trees (for example, in No. 476, 4. 5; and No. 477, 9. 12). The chapters given in the Appendix Nos. 478 and 481 have hardly any connection with the subjects previously treated.

--- Page 293 ---
### 393.

**TREES.**

Small, lofty, sparse, thick—that is, in terms of foliage—dark, light, reddish, branched at the top; some directed toward the eye, some downward; with white stems; some transparent against the sky, others not; some growing close together, others scattered.

### 394.

All the branches of a tree at every stage of its height, when added together, are equal in thickness to the trunk below them.
All the branches of a watercourse at every stage of its length, provided they have an equal rate of flow, are equal to the thickness of the main source.

### 395.

Every year, when the branches of a plant or tree have finished maturing their growth, they will have produced, when added together, a thickness equal to that of the main stem; and at every stage of its branching, you will find the thickness of the said main stem; for example: *i k, g h, e f, c d, a b* will always be equal to each other, unless the tree has been pruned or damaged—if so, the rule does not hold true.
All the branches have a direction that points toward the center of the tree *m*.

--- Page 296 ---
### 403.

**ON THE BRANCHING OF PLANTS.**

Plants that spread extensively have the angles of the spaces dividing their branches more obtuse the lower their point of origin—that is, the closer they are to the thickest and oldest part of the tree. Therefore, in the younger parts of the tree, the angles of branching are more acute.

---

### 404.

The tips of the branches of plants and trees, unless they are weighed down by the weight of their fruit, turn toward the sky as much as possible.

The upper side of their leaves is turned toward the sky so that it may receive nourishment from the dew that falls at night.

The sun gives spirit and life to plants, and the earth nourishes them with moisture. Regarding this, I once performed an experiment by leaving only one tiny root on a gourd plant, which I kept nourished with water. This gourd plant successfully brought to maturity all the fruit it could produce, which was about sixty gourds of the long variety. I applied my mind diligently to study this vitality and realized that the night dews were what supplied it abundantly with moisture through the attachment points of its large leaves, providing nourishment to the plant and its offspring—or rather, the seeds that were to produce its offspring.

The rule for leaves produced on the latest shoot of the year is that they will grow in opposite directions on twin branches; that is, the attachment of the leaves rotates around each branch in such a way that the sixth leaf above...

--- Page 297 ---
...is produced over the sixth leaf below. The direction of their rotation is such that if one turns toward its companion to the right, the other turns to the left; the leaf serves as the nourishing breast for the shoot or fruit that grows the following year.

---

### 405.

The lowest branches of those trees which have large leaves and heavy fruits, such as nut trees, fig trees, and the like, always droop toward the ground.

The branches always originate above [in the axil of] the leaves.

---

### 406.

The upper shoots of the lateral branches of plants stay closer to the parent branch than the lower ones do.

---

### 407.

The lowest branches, after they have formed the angle of their separation from the parent stem, always bend downward so as not to crowd against the other branches which follow them on the same stem and...

--- Page 298 ---
...and to be better able to take in the air which nourishes them. As is shown by the angle *b* *a* *c*; the branch *a* *c*, after it has formed the corner of the angle *a* *c*, bends downward to *c* *d*, and the lesser shoot *c* dries up, being too thin.

The main branch always goes below, as is shown by the branch *f* *n* *m*, which does not go to *f* *n* *o*.

---

### 408.

The elm always gives a greater length to the last branches of the year's growth than to the lower ones; and Nature does this because the highest branches are those which must add to the size of the tree, and those at the bottom must dry up because they grow in the shade, and their growth would be an impediment to the entrance of the solar rays and the air among the main branches of such a plant.

The lower main branches bend down more than those above, because they are more oblique than the upper ones and also because they are larger and older.

---

### 409.

In general, almost all the upright portions of trees curve somewhat, turning the convex side toward the South; and their branches are longer, thicker, and more abundant toward the South than toward the North. And this occurs because the sun draws the sap toward that surface of the plant which is nearest to it.

And this may be observed unless the sun is screened off by other plants.

---

### 410.

The cherry tree is of the same character as the fir tree regarding its ramification, which is arranged in tiers around its stem.

--- Page 299 ---
### 411.

...in stages round its main stem; and its branches spring, four or five or six together, opposite each other; and the tips of the topmost shoots form a pyramid from the middle upwards; and the walnut and oak form a hemisphere from the middle upwards.

### 411.

The bough of the walnut which is only hit and beaten when it has brought to perfection...

---

### 412.

### ON THE GROWTH OF BRANCHES IN PLANTS.

The growth of the branching system of plants upon their principal boughs follows the same pattern as the growth of the leaves upon the shoots of the same year. These leaves have four modes of growing, one above another. The first, which is the most general, is that the sixth leaf always originates above the sixth leaf below; the second is that the two third leaves above are over the two third leaves below; and the third way is that the third leaf above is over the third leaf below.

--- Page 300 ---
### 413.

DESCRIPTION OF THE ELM.

This branching of the elm has the largest branch at its front, and the smallest are the first and the next to last when the main stem is straight.

The space from the origin of one leaf to the next is half the maximum length of the leaf or slightly less, because the leaves have an interval that is about a third of the width of such a leaf.

The elm has its leaves closer to the tip of its branch than to its base, and their widths vary little, and they appear to have the same aspect when viewed.

---

### 414.

The leaves of the walnut, distributed throughout the shoot of that year, are further apart from each other and in greater number the younger the branch is where such a shoot originates; and they are closer at their origins and fewer in number the older the branch is where the shoot originates. Its fruits grow at the end of its shoot, and its larger branches are below the branch where they originate. This happens because the weight of its sap is more inclined to descend than to rise, and for this reason the branches that grow above them, which go toward the sky, are small and thin. When the shoot looks toward the sky, its leaves spread out from its end with equal distribution of their tips; and if the shoot looks toward the horizon, the leaves remain flattened, and this arises because leaves universally keep their underside turned toward the earth.

The shoots are smaller the closer they originate to the base of the branch that produces them.

--- Page 301 ---
### 415.

**ON THE INSERTION OF LEAVES ON THE BRANCHES.**

The thickness of a branch never diminishes in the space between one leaf and the next, except by the thickness of the bud that is above the leaf; and this thickness is taken off from the branch above the node as far as the next leaf.

Nature has placed the leaves of the newest shoots of many plants in such a way that the sixth leaf is always above the first, and so on in succession, if the rule is not accidentally obstructed. This occurs for two useful purposes for the plant: First, because the shoot and the fruit of the following year spring from the bud or eye which lies above and in close contact with the insertion of the leaf in the axil, the water that falls upon the shoot can run down to nourish that bud, as the drop is caught in the hollow axil at the insertion of the leaf. The second advantage is that, as these shoots develop in the following year, one will not cover the next below, since the five shoots come forth on five different sides; and the sixth, which is above the first, is at a considerable distance.

---

### 416.

**ON THE RAMIFICATIONS OF TREES AND THEIR FOLIAGE.**

The ramifications of any tree, such as the elm, are wide and slender like an open hand with spread fingers, viewed in foreshortening. These are seen in their distribution as follows: the lower portions are seen from above; those that are higher up are seen from below; and those in the middle are seen partly from below and partly from above. The upper part is the extreme top of this ramification, and the middle portion is more foreshortened than any of the others that are turned with their tips toward you. And of those parts in the middle of the height of the tree...

--- Page 302 ---
the height of the tree, the longest will be towards the top of the tree and will produce a ramification like the foliage of the common willow, which grows on the banks of rivers.

Other ramifications are spherical, such as those of trees that put forth their shoots and leaves in an order where the sixth is placed above the first. Others are thin and transparent, like the willow and similar trees.

### 417.

You will see in the lower branches of the elder—which puts forth leaves two by two, placed crosswise at right angles one above another—that if the stem rises straight up towards the sky, this order never fails. Its largest leaves are on the thickest part of the stem and the smallest on the slenderest part, which is towards the top. But, to return to the lower branches, I say that the leaves on these are placed crosswise like those on the upper branches; and as, by the law of all leaves, they are compelled to turn their upper surface towards the sky to catch the dew at night, it is necessary that those so placed should twist around and no longer form a cross.

### 418.

A leaf always turns its upper side towards the sky so that it may better receive, on its entire surface, the dew which drops gently from the atmosphere. These leaves are distributed on the plant so that one covers the other as little as possible, but instead they lie alternately one above another, as can be seen in the ivy that covers walls. This alternation serves two purposes: first, to leave intervals through which the air and sun may penetrate between them; the second reason is so that the drops which fall from the first leaf may fall onto the fourth or—in other trees—onto the sixth.

--- Page 304 ---
This page appears to be blank, likely a flyleaf or an unprinted back or front side. There are some minor spots of brown staining in the upper right corner.

--- Page 306 ---
# 424.

A leaf with a concave surface, seen from the underside and looking upward, will sometimes appear half in shadow and half transparent. For example, if *o p* is the leaf, *m* is the light, and *n* is the eye, the eye will see *o* in shadow because the light does not strike it at equal angles, neither on the front nor the back; and *p* is illuminated from the front, and this light shines through to its back.

# 425.

Even though leaves with a polished surface are largely the same color on the front as on their back, it happens that the part seen by the atmosphere takes on the color of that atmosphere. It appears to take on more of the atmosphere's color the closer the eye is to it and the more it is seen in perspective. Generally, its shadows appear darker on the front than on the back, because of the contrast created by the highlights that border those shadows.

The back of the leaf, even though its color is inherently the same as the front, displays a more beautiful color—a green tending toward yellow. This occurs when the leaf is positioned between

# 426.

the eye and the light, which illuminates it from the opposite side.

And its shadows are in the same positions as they were on the opposite side. Therefore, you as a painter, when you depict trees up close, remember that if your eye is somewhat beneath the tree, you will happen to see its leaves from both the front and the back. The front parts will be bluer the more they are seen in perspective; and sometimes a single leaf shows part of its front and part of its back, and for this reason, you must paint it with two colors.

--- Page 309 ---
# 433.

**OF DARK LEAVES IN FRONT OF TRANSPARENT ONES.**

When leaves are positioned between the light source and the eye, the leaf nearest to the eye will be the darkest, and the most distant will be the lightest, provided they are not viewed against the open sky. This occurs with leaves located beyond the center of the tree—that is, those on the side facing toward the light.

---

# 434.

**OF THE LIGHTS ON DARK LEAVES.**

The highlights on leaves of a darker color will most closely resemble the color of the atmosphere reflected in them. This is because the brightness of the illuminated area, when combined with the underlying dark hue, creates a blue color. This light originates from the blueness of the sky reflected in the smooth surface of such leaves, enhancing the blue tone that light typically produces when it meets dark objects.

**OF THE LIGHTS ON YELLOWISH-GREEN LEAVES.**

However, when leaves of a yellowish-green hue reflect the sky, they do not produce a reflection that tends toward blue. Since everything seen in a reflection takes on the color of the surface acting as the mirror, the blue of the sky reflected in the yellow of the leaf appears green; blue and yellow mixed together create a beautiful green. Therefore, the highlights on light-colored leaves verging on yellow will be a greenish-yellow.

---

# 435.

The trees in a landscape consist of various kinds of green, inasmuch as some verge...

--- Page 310 ---
towards blackness, such as firs, pines, cypresses, laurels, box trees, and the like. Some tend toward yellow, such as walnuts, pears, vines, and greenery. Some are both yellowish and dark, such as chestnuts and holm oaks. Some turn red toward autumn, such as the service tree, pomegranate, vine, and cherry; and some are whitish, such as the willow, olive, reeds, and the like.
Trees are of various forms.

---

### 436.

**OF A GENERALLY DISTRIBUTED LIGHT AS LIGHTING UP TREES.**

That part of the tree will be seen to lie in the least dark shadow which is farthest from the earth.

To prove it, let *a p* be the tree and *n b c* be the illuminated hemisphere [the sky]. The lower portion of the tree faces the earth *p c*—that is, on the side *o*—and it faces a small part of the hemisphere at *c d*. But the highest part of the convexity *a* faces the greatest part of the hemisphere, which is *b c*. For this reason—and because it does not face the darkness of the earth—it remains in fuller light. But if the tree has dense foliage, such as the laurel, arbutus, box, or holm oak, it will be different; because, although *a* does not face the earth, it faces the darkness of the leaves, which are divided by many shadows. This darkness is reflected upward onto the undersides of the leaves immediately above. Thus, these trees have their darkest shadows nearest to the middle of the tree.

---

### 437.

**OF THE SHADOWS OF VERDURE.**

The shadows of greenery always participate in blue, as does every shadow of every other object; and they take on more of this hue in proportion to how distant they are from the eye, and less in proportion to how near they are.

--- Page 311 ---
The leaves which reflect the blue of the atmosphere always present themselves to the eye edgewise.

### OF THE ILLUMINATED PART OF VEGETATION AND OF MOUNTAINS.

The illuminated portion, at a great distance, will appear most nearly of its natural color where the strongest light falls upon it.

---

### 438.

**OF TREES THAT ARE LIGHTED BY THE SUN AND BY THE ATMOSPHERE.**

In trees that are illuminated by both the sun and the atmosphere and that have leaves of a dark color, one side will be illuminated by the atmosphere only, and as a result of this light will tend toward blueness; while on the other side they will be illuminated by both the atmosphere and the sun. The side which the eye sees illuminated by the sun will be shiny.

---

### 439.

**OF DEPICTING A FOREST SCENE.**

The trees and plants which are most thickly branched with slender branches ought to have less dark shadow than those trees and plants which, having broader leaves, will cast more shadow.

---

### 440.

**ON PAINTING.**

From the position of the eye which sees that portion of a tree illuminated which turns toward the light, one tree will never be seen to be illuminated equally with another. To prove this, let the eye be *c* which sees the two trees...

--- Page 312 ---

This is because the tree which is nearest to the sun will show a much stronger shadow than the more distant one, in proportion as one tree is closer to the convergence of the sun's rays that reach the eye than the other, etc.

## 441.

<margin>
E. 19a]
</margin>

<margin>
The distribution of light and shade with reference to the position of the spectator (441-443).
</margin>

The shadows of trees placed in a landscape do not appear in the same position on the trees to the right as they do on those to the left, especially when the sun is to the right or left. This is proved by the fourth rule, which states: opaque bodies placed between the light and the eye appear entirely in shadow; and by the fifth: the eye, when placed between the opaque body and the light, sees the opaque body entirely illuminated; and by the sixth: when the eye and the opaque body are placed between darkness and light, the body will be seen half in shadow and half in light.

### OF TREES WHICH ARE BETWEEN THE EYE AND THE LIGHT.

Of the trees which are between the eye and the light, the part in front will be light; but this light will be broken by the branches of transparent leaves—being seen from the underside—and lustrous leaves—being seen from the upper side. The background below and behind will be dark green, being in shadow from the front portion of the said tree. This occurs in trees placed above the eye level.

---

<margin>G. 19b]</margin>

### 443.

### FROM WHERE TO DEPICT A LANDSCAPE.

Landscapes should be represented so that the trees may be half in light and half in shadow; but it is better to depict them when the sun is covered by clouds, for then the trees are lighted by the general light of the sky and the general darkness of the earth. In that case, they are darkest in certain parts in proportion as those parts are nearest to the center of the tree and to the ground.

---

<margin>G. 20b]</margin>

### 444.

### OF TREES TO THE SOUTH.

When the sun is in the east, the trees to the south and to the north have almost as much light as shadow. However, they have a greater total amount of light in proportion as they are situated toward the west, and a greater total amount of shadow in proportion as they are situated toward the east.

---

### OF MEADOWS.

<margin>The effects of morning light (444-448).</margin> When the sun is in the east, the greenery of the meadows and other small plants is of a most beautiful green because they are transparent to the sun. This does not occur in the meadows to the west; and in those to the south and north, the grass is of a moderately brilliant green.

--- Page 317 ---
...perceptible gradations, because the atmosphere that lies between the eye and those trees is very dense (see the 7th section of this work), and they are deprived of shade. For although a shadow exists in every detail of the branches, it happens that the images of light and shade reaching the eye are confused and mingled together, and cannot be perceived because of their small size. The principal lights are in the middle of the trees, and the shadows are toward the edges; their separations are marked by the shadows in the intervals between these trees. When the forests are thick with trees, the thin edges are hardly visible.

## 448.

### OF TREES TO THE EAST.

When the sun is in the East, the trees are dark toward the middle and their edges are bright.

---

## 449.

### OBJECTS PLACED IN BRIGHT LIGHT ARE DIFFICULT TO DISTINGUISH, BUT BETWEEN LIGHT AND SHADOW THEY STAND OUT WELL.

To paint landscapes, ensure the sun is at noon; turn toward the West or the East and then paint. If you turn toward the North, all objects placed along that line will be without shadows, especially those closest to the direction of the shadow of your head. If you turn toward the South, everything along that line will be entirely in shadow. All the trees facing the sun that have the sky as their background will be dark, and the other trees set against that darkness will be black in the middle and light toward the edges.

--- Page 319 ---
to the eye, it displays the true form of its branches; but this shows less at *b* and is altogether lost at *c*, where not only are the branches of the tree invisible, but the entire tree is distinguished with difficulty. Every shadowed object, whatever its form may be, appears to be spherical at a great distance. And this occurs because, if it is a square body, at a very short distance it loses its angles, and a little farther off it loses even more of its remaining smaller sides. And so, before the whole is lost to sight, the parts are lost, being smaller than the whole; just as a man in such a distant position loses his legs, arms, and head before the mass of his body; then the outlines of length are lost before those of breadth, and where they have become equal it would be a square if the angles remained; but as they are lost, it is round.

---

### 452.

The image of the shadow of any object of uniform breadth can never be exactly the same as that of the body which casts it.

---

### 453.

All trees seen against the sun are dark toward the middle, and this shadow will take the shape of the tree when it is separated from others.
The shadows cast by trees on which the sun shines are as dark as those in the middle of the tree.
The shadow cast by a tree is never smaller than the mass of the tree itself, but becomes longer in proportion as the ground on which it falls slopes toward the center of the world.
The shadow will be densest in the middle of the tree when the tree has the fewest branches.

--- Page 320 ---
Every branch participates in the central shadow of every other branch and, consequently, in that of the whole tree.

The form of any shadow from a branch or tree is surrounded by the light which falls from the side where the light originates; this illumination defines the shape of the shadows, and this effect may occur at a distance of a mile from the side where the sun is located.

Light and shade on groups of trees (453—457).

If it happens that a cloud anywhere overshadows some part of a hill, the trees there will change less than those on the plains; for these trees on the hills have thicker branches, because they grow less tall each year than those in the plains. Therefore, as these branches are dark by nature and full of shade, the shadow of the clouds cannot darken them any further; but the open spaces between the trees, which have no strong shadow, change very much in tone—particularly those that are not green, such as ploughed lands, mountain landslides, barren ground, or rocks. Where the trees are seen against the atmosphere, they appear to be all the same color—unless they happen to be very close together or very thickly covered with leaves, like the fir and similar trees. When you see trees from the side from which the sun lights them, you will see them almost all of the same brightness, and the shadows within them will be hidden by the illuminated leaves that come between your eye and those shadows.

### TREES AT A SHORT DISTANCE.

When trees are situated between the sun and the eye, beyond the shadow that spreads from their center, the green of their leaves will appear transparent; but this transparency will be interrupted in many places by the leaves and boughs in shadow which come between you and them, or, in their upper portions, they will be accompanied by many highlights reflected from the leaves.

--- Page 321 ---
### 454.

The trees in a landscape stand out very little from one another because their illuminated sections are positioned against the illuminated sections of the trees behind them; consequently, there is little difference between the highlights and the shadows.

---

### 455.

Regarding trees seen from below and against the light, positioned one behind the other and close together: the highest part of the first tree will be largely transparent and bright, and it will stand out against the dark portion of the second tree. This will continue in succession for all trees placed under these same conditions.

Let $s$ be the light, $r$ be the eye, $c$ $d$ $n$ be the first tree, and $a$ $b$ $c$ be the second. I maintain that the eye at $r$ will see the portion $c$ $f$ as largely transparent and bright because of the light $s$, which hits it from the opposite side. The eye will see it against the dark background $b$ $c$, because that darkness is the shadow of the tree $a$ $b$ $c$.

However, if the eye is positioned at $t$, it will see $o$ $p$ as dark against the light background $n$ $g$.

Of the transparent and shadowed parts of trees, the part nearest to you is the darkest.

---

### 456.

That part of a tree which stands out against a background of shadow is all of one tone; wherever the trees or branches are thickest, the color is darkest because there is less air visible through them. But where the branches stand out against a background of other branches, the illuminated parts appear brighter and the leaves appear lustrous from the sunlight hitting them.

--- Page 323 ---
The trees in the landscape that are between you and the sun are far more beautiful than those you see when you are between the sun and them. This is because those facing the sun show their leaves as transparent toward the ends of their branches, while those parts that are not transparent—that is, at the tips—reflect the light; and the shadows are dark because they are not obscured by anything.

When you place yourself between the trees and the sun, they will only show you their light and natural color, which is not very intense in itself. Furthermore, there will be some highlights which, being against a background that does not differ much from them in tone, are not very noticeable. If you are positioned lower than the trees, they may also show those portions where the sunlight does not fall, and these will be dark.

### In the Wind.

But if you are on the side from which the wind blows, you will see the trees look much lighter than you would from other directions. This happens because the wind reveals the undersides of the leaves, which in all trees are much whiter than their upper sides. They will be especially bright if the wind blows from the direction of the sun while you have your back turned to it.

---

### 459.

When the sun is hidden by clouds, objects are less distinct because there is little difference between the lights and shadows of the trees and buildings. This is because they are illuminated by the brightness of the atmosphere, which surrounds the objects in such a way that shadows are few, and those few fade away so that their outlines vanish into haze.

---

### 460.

**OF TREES AND THEIR LIGHT.**

The best practical method for representing the countryside, or what I call landscapes, with its...

--- Page 324 ---
...trees, is to choose a time when the sun is covered by clouds. This ensures that the landscape receives a universal light rather than the direct light of the sun, which creates sharp shadows that contrast too strongly with the highlights.

---

### 461.

**OF PAINTING.**

In landscapes representing winter, the mountains should not be shown as blue as we see them in summer. This is proved by the fourth rule of this treatise, which says: Among mountains seen from a great distance, those that are naturally darker will appear to be of a deeper blue color. Therefore, since trees are stripped of their leaves, they will appear darker; and because they have lost their leaves, they will appear gray, whereas with their leaves they are green. In proportion as green is darker than gray, the green will appear to have a bluer tinge than the gray. Furthermore, according to the second rule of this treatise: The shadows of trees covered with leaves are darker than the shadows of those trees that have lost their leaves, in proportion as trees with leaves are denser than those without leaves—and thus my point is proved.
The definition of the blue color of the atmosphere explains why landscapes are bluer in summer than in winter.

---

### 462.

**OF PAINTING IN A LANDSCAPE.**

If the slope of a hill intervenes between the eye and the horizon, sloping toward the eye, and the eye is positioned opposite the middle of the height of this slope, then that hill will...

--- Page 327 ---
467.

### ON TOWNS OR OTHER BUILDINGS SEEN IN THE EVENING OR THE MORNING THROUGH THE MIST.

Regarding buildings seen at a great distance in the evening or the morning, such as in mist or a dense atmosphere, only those portions are visible in brightness which are lit up by the sun when it is near the horizon; and those portions which are not lit up by the sun remain almost the same color and medium tone as the mist.

### WHY OBJECTS WHICH ARE HIGH UP AND AT A DISTANCE ARE DARKER THAN THE LOWER ONES, EVEN IF THE MIST IS UNIFORMLY DENSE.

Of objects standing in a mist or other dense atmosphere—whether from vapor, smoke, or distance—those will be most visible which are the highest. And among objects of equal height, the one that will appear darkest (most distinct) is the one that has the deepest mist as its background. This is shown by the eye *h* looking at *a*, *b*, and *c*, which are towers of equal height to one another. The eye sees *c*, the top of the first tower, at *r*, through two degrees of depth in the mist; and it sees the height of the middle tower *b* through only one single degree of mist. Therefore, the top of tower *c* appears darker than the top of tower *b*, and so on.

468.

### ON THE SMOKE OF A TOWN.

Smoke is seen better and more distinctly on the eastern side than on the western side when the sun is in the east. This happens for two reasons: the first is that the sun, with its rays, shines through the particles of the smoke, lighting them up and making them visible. The second is that the roofs of the houses seen in the east at this time are in shadow, because their slant does not allow them to be lit...

--- Page 328 ---
...being illuminated by the sun. The same thing occurs with dust; both are brighter in proportion to their density, and they are densest toward the middle.

### 469. ON SMOKE AND DUST.

If the sun is in the East, the smoke of cities will not be visible in the West, because from that direction it is not seen penetrated by the solar rays, nor is it seen against a dark background. Since the roofs of the houses show the eye the same side that they show to the sun, the smoke is not very visible against this light background.

But dust, under the same conditions, will look darker than smoke because it consists of denser material than smoke, which is a moist substance.

### 470. ON REPRESENTING WIND.

In representing wind, besides the bending of the boughs and the reversing of their leaves toward the direction from which the wind comes, you should also depict them amid clouds of fine dust mingled with the turbulent air.

### 471. THE WIND.

Describe landscapes with the wind, the water, and the setting and rising of the sun.

All the leaves that were hanging toward the ground due to the bending of the shoots and branches are turned upside down by the gusts of wind, and here their perspective is reversed. For if the tree is between you and the source of the wind, the parts of the leaves that are toward you remain in their natural position, while those on the opposite side—which should have pointed away from you—have their tips turned toward you because they have been blown over.

--- Page 329 ---
### 472.

Trees struck by the force of the wind bend toward the direction in which the wind is blowing; and once the wind has passed, they bend in the opposite direction, following a rebounding motion.

### 473.

The part of a tree that is farthest from the force striking it is the most damaged by the impact because it acts as a longer lever; therefore, nature has provided for this by thickening trees in the areas where they are most vulnerable to injury. This is especially true in trees that grow to great heights, such as pines and similar species.

### 474.

Describe how clouds are formed and how they dissolve, and what cause makes vapor rise.

### 475.

The shadows in clouds are lighter in proportion to how close they are to the horizon.

### 476.

When clouds come between the sun and the eye, all the outer edges of their rounded forms are light, and they are dark toward the middle.

**473.** Compare the sketch drawn with a pen and washed with Indian ink on Plate XL, No. 2. In the Vatican copy, under a section titled *'On Smoke'*, we find the following remark:
> *Below this chapter was a drawing of a mountain cleft, through which flames of fire flickered; it was drawn with a pen and shaded with watercolor, appearing as a wonderful and lifelike thing.*

This seems to refer to the left-hand portion of the drawing from the Windsor collection provided here. From this, it must be inferred that the leaf as it currently exists in the library of the Queen of England was already separated from the original manuscript at the time the Vatican copy was made.

**475.** The drawing belonging to this was in black chalk and is completely erased.

**476.** A drawing in red chalk from the Windsor collection (see Plate XXIX), representing a landscape with storm clouds, may serve to illustrate this section as well as the following one.

--- Page 330 ---
And this happens because, toward the upper edges mentioned, they are seen by the sun from above them and by you from below them; and this same thing happens with the positions of the branches of trees. Furthermore, both the clouds and the trees appear bright in part because they are somewhat transparent, and at the edges they show more thinness.

But when the eye is located between the cloud and the sun, the cloud produces the opposite effect of what it did before, because the edges of its rounded masses are dark and they are bright toward the middle. This happens because you see that part which the sun also sees from the front, and because those edges have some transparency and reveal to the eye those parts hidden behind them; since these are not seen by the sun like the parts turned toward it, they must necessarily be somewhat darker. Furthermore, it may be that you see the details of those rounded masses from the underside while the sun sees them from above; and because they are not situated in a way that allows them to reflect the brightness of the sun as they did before, they are therefore dark.

The black clouds, which are often seen above the bright ones illuminated by the sun, are shaded by other clouds that are interposed between them and the sun.

Additionally, the rounded forms of the clouds that face the sun show their edges as dark because they stand out against a bright background. To see that this is true, you should look at the summit of the entire cloud, which is bright because it stands out against the blue of the air, which is darker than the cloud.

---

### OF CLOUDS, SMOKE, AND DUST, AND THE FLAMES OF A FURNACE OR OF A BURNING KILN.

The cloud does not show its rounded forms except in those parts that are seen by the sun, and the other rounded masses are imperceptible because they are in the shadows.

--- Page 332 ---
[This page is blank]

--- Page 333 ---
If the sun is in the East and the clouds are in the West, the eye placed between the sun and the clouds sees the edges of the rounded forms composing these clouds as dark, and the portions which are surrounded by this darkness are light. And this occurs because the edges of the rounded forms of these clouds are turned toward the upper or lateral sky, which is reflected in them. Both the cloud and the tree display no roundness at all on their shaded side.

### 478.

Painters often deceive themselves by representing water in which they make the water reflect the objects as they are seen by the person. But the water reflects the object from one side and the person sees it from the other; and it often happens that the painter sees an object from below, and thus one and the same object is seen from front to back and upside down, because the water shows the image of the object in one way, and the eye sees it in another.

### 479.

The colors in the middle of the rainbow mingle together. The bow in itself is not in the rain nor in the eye that sees it, though it is generated by the rain, the sun, and the eye. The rainbow is always seen by the eye that is between the rain and the body of the sun; hence, if the sun is in the East and the rain is in the West, the bow is generated in the rain in the West.

### 480.

VOL. I.

## IX.

# The Practice of Painting.

It is hardly necessary to offer any excuses for the division made in the arrangement of the text into practical suggestions and theoretical inquiries. It was evidently intended by Leonardo himself, as we conclude from incidental remarks in the manuscripts (for instance, No. 110). The fact that this arrangement was never carried out, either in the old manuscript copies or in any edition since, is easily explained by the general disorder resulting from the temporary distribution of the various chapters in the old copies. We have every reason to believe that the earliest copyists, when organizing the materials they collected, did not in the least consider the order in which the original manuscript lay before them.

It is evident that almost all the chapters referring to the profession and life of the painter—which are brought together here in the first section (Nos. 482–508)—can be assigned to two distinct periods in Leonardo’s life; most of them can be dated to the year 1492 or to 1515. Around this later time, Leonardo may have formed the plan of completing his Libro della Pittura after an interval of some years, during which, it would seem, his interest in the subject had somewhat receded into the background.

In the second section, which first deals with the artist’s studio, the construction of a suitable window is the subject of careful investigation; the special importance Leonardo attached to this is quite obvious. His theory of the incidence of light, which was fully discussed in a former part of this work, was by no means of merely abstract value to him; rather, being derived, as he says, from experience (or experiment), it was required to prove its utility in practice. Connected with this, we find suggestions for the choice of light, along with practical hints for sketching a picture and several other instructions of a practical nature that must be considered during the process of completing a painting. In all of this, I have followed the same principle of arrangement in the text as was used in the Theory of Painting; thus, the suggestions for the Perspective of a picture (Nos. 536–569) are followed by the theory of light and shade for the practical method of optics (Nos. 548–566), and this is followed by the practical instructions for the treatment of aerial perspective (567–570).

--- Page 336 ---
242
THE PRACTICE OF PAINTING.

In the passage on Portrait and Figure Painting, the principles of painting as applied to a bust and head are separated and placed first, since the advice to figure painters must have some connection with the principles of the treatment of composition by which they are followed.

But this arrangement of the text made it seem advisable not to pick out the practical precepts regarding the representation of trees and landscape from the close connection in which they were originally placed—unlike the rest of the practical precepts—with the theory of this branch of the subject. They must therefore be sought under the section entitled Botany for Painters.

As a supplement to the Libro di Pittura, I have here added those texts which treat of the Painter’s materials—such as chalk, drawing paper, colors and their preparation, and the management of oils and varnishes; in the appendix are some notes on chemical substances. Possibly some of these, if not all, may have been connected with the preparation of colors. It is in the very nature of things that Leonardo’s incidental indications regarding colors and the like should be extremely obscure nowadays and could only be explained by professional experts—and even by them in only a few instances. It might therefore have seemed advisable to reproduce the original text exactly without offering any translation. The rendering given here is merely an attempt to suggest what Leonardo’s meaning may have been.

LOMAZZO tells us in his Trattato dell’arte della Pittura, Scultura ed Architettura (Milan 1584, Book II, Chapter XIV): “Leonardo Vinci goes on discussing and arguing in a book of his read by me (?) in years past, which he wrote with a tired hand at the request of LUDOVICO SFORZA, Duke of Milan, to determine this question: whether painting or sculpture is more noble; saying that the more an art brings with it bodily labor and sweat, the more it is vile and less esteemed.” But the existence of any book specifically written for Lodovico il Moro on the superiority of Painting over Sculpture is perhaps mythical. The various passages in praise of Painting as compared not merely with Sculpture but with Poetry are scattered among manuscripts of very different dates.

Besides, the way in which the subject is discussed does not appear to support the supposition that these texts were prepared at a special request of the Duke.

--- Page 337 ---
# I.

## MORAL PRECEPTS FOR THE STUDENT OF PAINTING.

---

->482.<-

**ADVICE FOR YOUTHS INCLINED TOWARD PAINTING.**

How to determine an aptitude for an artistic career. Many people have a desire and love for drawing but no talent; this will be recognizable in boys who lack diligence and never finish their work with shading.

---

->483.<-

The course of instruction for an artist (483—485). The youth should first learn perspective, then the proportions of all things. Next, he should copy drawings by the hand of a good master to accustom himself to beautiful anatomy. After that, he should draw from nature to confirm the principles of what he has learned. Then, he should study the works of various masters for a time. Finally, he should develop the habit of practicing and executing his art.

---

**483.** The Vatican copy and numerous abridgments all place this chapter at the beginning of the *Trattato*, and consequently DUFRESNE and all subsequent editors have done the same. In the Vatican copy, however, all general considerations on the relationship of painting to the other arts are placed first as an introduction.

--- Page 338 ---
## 484.

**THE ORDER OF LEARNING TO DRAW.**

First, draw from drawings by good masters produced according to artistic principles and from nature, rather than from memory. Next, draw from three-dimensional objects in conjunction with the drawings made from those same objects. Finally, draw from good natural models, and you must make this your regular practice.

---

## 485.

**PRECEPTS FOR DRAWING.**

The artist should first train his hand by copying drawings by a good master. Having acquired that skill under the guidance of his teacher, he should next practice drawing high-quality objects in relief, following the rules that will be provided below.

---

## 486.

**ON DRAWING.**

Which is better: to draw from nature or from the antique? And which is more difficult: to produce outlines or light and shade?

---

## 487.

It is better to imitate the antique than modern work.

---

**486. 487.** These are the only two passages in which Leonardo refers to the importance of antique art in the training of an artist. The question asked in No. 486 remains unanswered by him, and it seems very doubtful to me whether the opinion stated in No. 487 should be regarded as a reply to it. This opinion appears in the manuscript in a context—as will be explained later—which seems to require us to limit its application to a single special case. At any rate, we may suspect that when Leonardo posed the question, he felt some hesitation regarding the answer. Among his very numerous drawings, I have not been able to find a single study from the antique, though a black chalk drawing at Windsor of a man on horseback (Plate LXXIII) may perhaps be a memory of the statue of Marcus Aurelius in Rome. It seems to me that the drapery in a pen and ink drawing of a bust, also at Windsor, has been borrowed from an antique model (Plate XXX). G. G. Rossi has, I believe, correctly interpreted Leonardo's feeling toward the antique in the following note on this passage in Manzi's edition, p. 501:

> "We know from history that the talented Tuscan artists of the golden age of art studied the ancient marbles collected by Lorenzo the Magnificent. It appears that Da Vinci did not approach such monuments. This man always recognized nature as his teacher, and this principle bound him solely to the imitation of it."

--- Page 339 ---
"We know that Leonardo was an ardent observer of nature and a most diligent imitator of it; but we also know that he did not despise the beautiful works of the ancients, as he saw in them a second nature, or rather, nature itself in its most perfect form. He did not want the artist to become a mere copyist of the ancients, but rather to learn from them how to choose the most beautiful parts of nature and how to combine them with judgment and grace. In this sense, we must understand his preference for the antique over modern works, which in his time often lacked the noble simplicity and the profound knowledge of anatomy and proportion that characterized Greek and Roman art."

--- Page 341 ---
# 488.

## ON PAINTING.

It is indispensable for a painter, in order to be a skilled master of the limbs in all the poses and gestures of which they are capable in the nude, to know the anatomy of the sinews, bones, muscles, and tendons. This is so that, in various movements and exertions, he may know which nerve or muscle is the cause of each movement, and show only those as prominent and swollen, and not the others throughout the limb. Many do this who, wanting to seem like great draughtsmen, make their nudes look woody and devoid of grace; so that when you look at them, you would think you were seeing a sack of walnuts rather than the human form, or a bundle of radishes rather than muscular nudes.

---

# 489.

## WHY IT IS NECESSARY FOR A PAINTER TO KNOW THE INTRINSIC STRUCTURE OF MAN.

The painter who is familiar with the nature of the sinews, muscles, and tendons will know very well, when moving a limb, how many and which sinews cause it; and which muscle, by swelling, causes the contraction of that sinew; and which cords, turned into the thinnest cartilage, surround and bind that muscle. Thus, he will be a diverse and universal demonstrator of various muscles through the various actions of his figures. He will not act like many who, in different poses, always show the exact same details in the arms, back, chest, and legs. Such things should not be counted among minor errors.

---

# 490.

## ON STUDY AND ITS ORDER.

I say that first you must learn the limbs and their mechanics, and having finished this—

--- Page 342 ---
knowledge, their actions should follow next, according to the circumstances that occur to a person. And thirdly, you must compose subjects, the studies for which should be taken from natural actions observed by chance as circumstances allow. Pay attention to them in the streets, squares, and fields, and note them down with a brief sketch of their outlines; for instance, use an "o" for a head and a straight or bent line for an arm, and do the same for the legs and the torso. Then, when you return home, work out these reminders into a finished form. The opponent says that to become proficient and produce a great deal of work, it is better that the first period of study be spent in copying various compositions made on paper or on walls by different masters, and that in this way one gains rapid practice and good habits. To this it is replied that such a habit would be good if it were based on works of good composition and by diligent masters. But since such masters are so rare that few of them are found, it is safer to go to natural objects than to those which are imitated from nature with great deterioration, thereby forming bad habits; for he who can go to the fountain should not go to the water-jar.

### WHAT RULES SHOULD BE GIVEN TO BOYS LEARNING TO PAINT.

We know clearly that sight is one of the fastest operations there is, and in an instant it sees infinite forms; nevertheless, it only comprehends one thing at a time. Let us suppose the case that you, the reader, glance at this entire written page at once; you will immediately judge it to be full of various letters, but you will not recognize in that moment what the letters are, nor what they mean. Therefore, you must go word by word and line by line if you wish to gain knowledge of these letters. Furthermore, if you wish to climb to the top of a building, you must go up step by step; otherwise it will be impossible to reach the height. And so I say to you...

--- Page 343 ---
to reach the top. Thus I say to you, whom nature prompts to pursue this art: if you wish to have a sound knowledge of the forms of objects, begin with their details, and do not go on to the second step until you have the first well fixed in your memory and in practice. If you do otherwise, you will waste your time, or certainly greatly prolong your studies. And remember to acquire diligence rather than rapidity.

### 492.

**HOW DILIGENCE SHOULD BE LEARNED BEFORE RAPID EXECUTION.**

If you, as a draftsman, desire to study well and to good purpose, always work slowly; and distinguish among the lights which have the highest degree of brightness, and to what extent. Likewise, among the shadows, determine which are darker than the others and in what way they intermingle, then consider their masses and the relative proportions of one to the other. Note in their outlines which way they tend, and which part of the lines curves to one side or the other, and where they are more or less conspicuous and consequently broad or fine. Finally, ensure that your light and shade blend without strokes or borders, looking like smoke. When you have schooled your hand and your judgment by such diligence, you will acquire rapidity before you are aware of it.

### 493.

**THE LIFE OF THE PAINTER IN THE COUNTRY.**

A painter needs such mathematics as belong to painting, and the absence of all companions who are uninterested in his studies. His brain must be easily impressed by the variety of objects which successively come before him.

--- Page 345 ---
If you desire companionship, find it in your studio. This may help you to gain the insights that arise from various observations. Any other company could be highly harmful to you.

---

### 495.

#### ON WHETHER IT IS BETTER TO DRAW IN COMPANY OR NOT.

I say and maintain that drawing in company is much better than drawing alone, for many reasons. The first is that you would be ashamed to be seen as inadequate among the number of draftsmen, and this shame will be the cause of diligent study. Secondly, healthy envy will stimulate you to be among those more praised than yourself, as the praise of others will spur you on. Another reason is that you will adopt the techniques of those who do better than you; and if you are better than the others, you will profit by avoiding their shortcomings, while the praise of others will increase your own excellence.

---

### 496.

#### ON STUDYING WHEN YOU WAKE, OR BEFORE YOU FALL ASLEEP IN BED IN THE DARK.

I have experienced it to be of no small benefit, when you are in the dark in bed, to go over in your imagination the surface outlines of the forms previously studied, or other notable things understood through subtle reflection. This is truly a commendable act and useful for fixing things in the memory.

---

### 497.

#### ON THE TIME AT WHICH ONE SHOULD STUDY THE SELECTION OF SUBJECTS.

Winter evenings should be used by young people for the study of things prepared during the summer; that is, all the nudes that—

--- Page 347 ---
...who would not have attained some perfection in it? But, since we know that painting embraces and includes in itself every object produced by nature or resulting from the accidental actions of men—in short, all that can be understood by the eye—it seems to me that he is a poor master who can only do one figure well. For do you not perceive how many and what kind of actions are performed by men alone? Do you not see how many different animals there are, as well as trees, plants, and flowers; the variety of mountainous and level regions, springs, rivers, cities, public and private buildings; tools fit for human use, various costumes, ornaments, and arts? All these things ought to be practiced with equal effort and quality by the man you wish to call a good painter.

---

# 501.

## OF THE WRETCHED EXCUSES MADE BY THOSE WHO FALSELY AND UNWORTHILY CALL THEMSELVES PAINTERS.

There is a certain generation of painters who, because of their lack of study, must exist under the beauty of gold and azure. With supreme foolishness, they claim they do not produce good work because of poor payment, and that they would know how to do as well as any other if they were well paid. Now look, you foolish people: do such men not know how to keep some good work on hand, saying, "this is for a high price, this is for a middle price, and this is for a common price," and thus show that they have work for every price?

---

# 502.

## HOW, IN IMPORTANT WORKS, A MAN SHOULD NOT TRUST HIS MEMORY SO MUCH THAT HE DOES NOT DEIGN TO DRAW FROM NATURE.

That master who would lead himself to believe that he could retain within himself all the forms and effects of nature would certainly seem to me to be marked by great ignorance, since the said effects are infinite and our memory does not have enough capacity to be sufficient. Therefore, you painter...

--- Page 348 ---
Take care that the greed for gain does not overcome in you the honor of art, for the acquisition of honor is much greater than the honor of riches. Therefore, for these and other reasons that could be mentioned, you should first strive through drawing to give the eye a visible form of the intention and invention originally created in your imagination. Then, continue removing and adding until you are satisfied. Afterward, arrange men, either clothed or nude, in the manner you have planned for the work. Ensure that by measure and size, and subject to perspective, nothing enters the work that is not well-advised by reason and natural effects. This shall be the way to bring honor to your art.

---

### 503.
### OF VARIETY IN THE FIGURES.

The painter must strive to be universal, because he lacks much dignity if he does one thing well and another poorly, like many who only study the measured and proportioned nude and do not seek its variety. For a man may be well-proportioned and yet be fat and short, or tall and thin, or medium. Anyone who takes no account of this variety always makes his figures according to a pattern so that they all appear to be brothers, which is a thing deserving of great reproach.

---

### 504.
### HOW ONE CAN LEARN BY ALL MEANS.

Bountiful nature has provided that you may find something to imitate throughout the entire world.

---

### 505.
### OF THE METHOD FOR BECOMING UNIVERSAL.

It is an easy thing for a man to become universal, because all terrestrial animals have a similarity of limbs—that is, muscles, nerves, and bones—and they do not vary except in length or thickness, as will be demonstrated in the...

--- Page 350 ---
Let one of you draw a straight line at random on a wall, and let each of you hold a thin blade of grass or straw in your hand. Each person should cut it to the length that the line on the wall appears to be while standing at a distance of ten braccia. Then, each person may go up to the example to measure their estimated length against it. Whoever comes closest with their measure to the length of the pattern shall be the winner and superior, and shall receive the prize that was previously agreed upon by all of you. Furthermore, you should take foreshortened measurements; that is, take a spear or another rod and look at a certain distance in front of you, and let each person estimate how many times that measure would fit into that distance. Again, see who can best draw a line one braccio long, which shall then be tested with a pulled thread. Such games are the means of developing a good judgment of the eye, which is the primary function of painting.

---

## A WAY OF DEVELOPING AND STIMULATING THE MIND TO VARIOUS INVENTIONS.

508.

I will not, however, omit from these precepts a new method of speculation which, although it may seem minor and almost laughable, is nevertheless of great utility in stimulating the mind to various inventions. This is: if you look at certain walls fouled with various stains or stones of mixed colors, and you have to invent some scene, you will be able to see there the resemblance of various landscapes adorned with mountains, rivers, rocks, trees, plains, great valleys, and hills in diverse arrangements. Moreover, you will be able to see various battles and figures in lively action, strange expressions on faces, costumes, and infinite things which you can then reduce into complete and good forms. It happens with such walls and mixtures of stone as it does with the sound of bells: in their ringing, you will find every name and word that you can imagine.

--- Page 351 ---

The window at *a* is the least useful and the coldest. The most useful, the brightest, and warmest, and the one that sees the most sky, is the window at *b*. The window at *c* is of medium utility.

### **THE PAINTER’S WINDOW AND ITS CONVENIENCE.**

The painter who works from nature should have a window which he can raise and lower. The reason for this is that sometimes you will want to finish a detail for which you require more light. Let *a, b, c, d* be the frame of this window, which can be raised and lowered so that the work—that is, the painting—is positioned in such a way that the top of it is never struck by light from above. Close the upper part of the window so that the light is blocked from below, serving to create a steady light.

Let *a b c d* be the chest on which the work may be raised or lowered, so that the work moves up and down and not the painter. And every evening you can lower the work and shut it up from above, so that in the evening it may function as a chest which, when closed, may serve the purpose of a bench.

### 513.

Which light is best for drawing from nature: whether high or low, or large or small, or strong and broad, or strong and small, or broad and weak, or small and weak?

---

### 514.

### ON THE QUALITY OF THE LIGHT.

A broad light placed high up and not too strong will make the details of objects appear very pleasing.

---

### 515.

### THAT THE LIGHT FOR DRAWING FROM NATURE SHOULD BE HIGH UP.

The light for drawing from nature should come from the North so that it does not change. And if you have it from the South, keep the window screened with cloth, so that even with the sun shining all day, the light does not change. The height of the light should be arranged so that every object casts a shadow on the ground of the same length as its own height.

--- Page 354 ---
### 516.

# ON THE QUALITY OF AIR IN RELATION TO SHADOWS AND LIGHTS.
# THE KIND OF LIGHT REQUIRED FOR PAINTING LIGHT AND SHADE.

An object will show the greatest difference between its shadows and highlights when it is seen in the strongest light, such as sunlight or, at night, the light of a fire. However, this should be used sparingly in painting, because the resulting works remain harsh and lack grace.

An object situated in moderate light will show little difference between its highlights and shadows; this occurs toward evening or when it is cloudy. Works painted under these conditions are soft and bring grace to every type of face. Thus, in all things, extremes are flawed: too much light creates harshness, and too much darkness prevents us from seeing; the middle ground is best.

# ON SMALL LIGHTS.

Furthermore, light coming from small windows creates a great difference between highlights and shadows, especially if the room illuminated by them is large; this is not a good technique to use.

---

### 517.

## PAINTING.

The luminous air that penetrates and passes through openings in walls into dark dwellings will make the place less gloomy in proportion to how much that opening penetrates the walls that surround and cover its floor.

--- Page 356 ---
...men and women, and when the weather is dull, notice what grace and softness can be seen in them. Therefore, Oh Painter, you should have a courtyard arranged with the walls painted black, with a small roof projecting over the wall. It should be 10 braccia wide, 20 braccia long, and 10 braccia high. When the sun is out, it should be covered with a linen awning; otherwise, paint a work toward evening, or when it is cloudy or misty, as this provides perfect light.

---

### 521.

On various helps in preparing a picture (521-530).

To draw a nude figure from nature, or any other object, get into the habit of holding a plumb-line in your hand so that you can observe how the parts of the objects align with one another.

---

### 522.

#### OF DRAWING AN OBJECT.

When you draw, ensure that you establish a primary line which you observe throughout the entire body you are drawing; everything you draw should align with the direction of this established line.

---

### 523.

#### OF A METHOD FOR DRAWING A SITE ACCURATELY.

Have a piece of glass as large as a half-sheet of royal folio paper and fix it firmly in front of your eyes—that is, between your eye and the object you wish to draw. Then position your eye at a distance of 2/3 of a braccia from the glass, and secure your head with a device so that you cannot move it at all. Next, shut or cover one eye, and with a brush or a crayon of ground pigment, trace onto the glass what appears on the other side. Then trace the image from the glass onto paper, transfer it onto good paper by pouncing, and paint it if you wish, making sure to apply aerial perspective correctly.

#### HOW TO LEARN TO POSITION FIGURES WELL.

If you want to become proficient in the correct and good posing of figures, make a square frame or net, and divide it into squares with threads...

--- Page 358 ---
### 524.

**A METHOD OF DRAWING AN OBJECT IN RELIEF AT NIGHT.**
Place a sheet of paper that is not too transparent between the relief and the light, and you will be able to draw very well.

### 525.

If you want to represent a figure on a wall where the wall is foreshortened, yet the figure is intended to appear in its proper shape and as if standing free from the wall, you must proceed in this way: obtain a thin plate of iron and make a small hole in the center; this hole must be round. Set a light close to it in such a position that it shines through the central hole, then place any object or figure you like so close to the wall that it touches it, and trace the outline of its shadow on the wall. Afterward, fill in the shadows and add the highlights. Position the person who is to view the work so that they look through that same hole where the light was originally placed; and you will never be able to convince yourself that the image is not detached from the wall.

***

524. Bodies illuminated in this manner will show on the surface of the paper how the artist must distribute light and shade.

525. *a small hole in the center*. M. RAVAISSON, in his edition of MS. A (Paris), p. 52, reads *in the wall*—evidently a mistake for *in the center*, which is quite plainly written; and he translates it as "*make a small opening in the wall*," adding in a note: "*the words 'in the wall' appear to be redundant. Leonardo must have written them out of distraction.*" However, '*in the center*' is clearly legible even on the photographic facsimile provided by Ravaisson himself, and the objection he raises disappears at once. It is not always wise or safe to attempt to prove our author's absence of mind or carelessness based on apparent difficulties in the meaning or flow of the text.
