525. *a small hole in the center*. M. RAVAISSON, in his edition of MS. A (Paris), p. 52, reads *in the wall*—evidently a mistake for *in the center*, which is quite clearly written; and he translates it as "*make a small opening in the wall*," adding in a note: "*the words 'in the wall' appear to be redundant. Leonardo must have written them out of distraction.*" However, '*in the center*' is clearly legible even on the photographic facsimile provided by Ravaisson himself, and the objection he raises disappears at once. It is not always wise or safe to attempt to prove our author's absence of mind or carelessness based on apparent difficulties in the meaning or flow of the text.

--- Page 362 ---
## 527.

If you wish to draw a cube in the corner of a wall, first draw the object in its own proper shape and project it onto a vertical plane until it matches the angle in which the said object is to be represented.

---

## 528.

Why are paintings seen more accurately in a mirror than outside of it?

---

## 529.

### HOW THE MIRROR IS THE MASTER [AND GUIDE] OF PAINTERS.

When you want to see if your picture corresponds entirely with the objects you have drawn from nature, take a mirror and look at the reflection of the real things within it. Compare the reflected image with your picture, and consider whether the subject in both images corresponds properly, paying particular attention to the mirror. You should take the mirror as your guide—specifically a flat mirror—because on its surface, objects appear in many respects just as they do in a painting. Thus you see that in a painting done on a flat surface, objects appear to be in relief, and in the mirror—which is also a flat surface—they look the same. The picture has a single flat surface, and the mirror is the same. The picture is intangible, in that what appears rounded and prominent cannot be grasped with the hands; the mirror is the same.

--- Page 363 ---
...and the mirror is the same. And since you recognize that the mirror, by means of outlines, shadows, and lights, makes objects appear to stand out in relief, and since you have among your colors shadows and lights more powerful than those in the mirror, it is certain that if you know how to compose them well together, your painting will also appear like a natural scene viewed in a large mirror.

### 530.

### ON JUDGING YOUR OWN PAINTING.

We know clearly that errors are recognized more easily in the works of others than in our own, and often, while criticizing the small errors of others, you will ignore your own great ones. To avoid such ignorance, first become a good master of perspective; then, acquire a complete knowledge of the measurements of man and other animals; and also be a good architect—that is, in what pertains to the form of buildings and the other things that are upon the earth, which are infinite forms. The more knowledge you have of these, the more praiseworthy your work will be; and in those things where you lack practice, do not refuse to draw them from nature. 

But to return to the promise made above, I say that while you are painting, you should keep a flat mirror and frequently look at your work within it. There, it will be seen in reverse and will appear to you as if by the hand of another master; thus, you will judge its errors better than you would otherwise. It is also good to get up often and take a little of some other relaxation, because when you return to the work, you have better judgment; staying fixed upon the work causes you to be greatly deceived. It is also good to move further away, because the work appears smaller and more of it is understood in a single glance, and the discordant or disproportionate limbs and colors of the objects are better recognized.

--- Page 365 ---
### 533.

#### HOW ERRORS ARE LESS EVIDENT IN SMALL OBJECTS THAN IN LARGE ONES.

In objects of tiny size, the extent of an error cannot be perceived as clearly as it can in large ones. The reason for this is that if a small object is made in the likeness of a man or another animal, its parts—due to the immense reduction in scale—cannot be executed by the artist with the required level of finish. Consequently, the work remains unfinished; and because it is unfinished, you cannot identify its flaws. For example: look at a man from a distance of 300 braccia and judge carefully whether he is handsome or ugly, or if he is monstrous or of ordinary appearance. You will find that even with your greatest effort, you cannot bring yourself to make a judgment. The reason is that at such a distance, the man is diminished so much that the qualities of his specific features cannot be perceived. If you wish to clearly see this reduction of the aforementioned man, place one of your fingers a span’s length away from your eye, and raise or lower that finger until its top edge aligns with the feet of the figure you are looking at; you will see an incredible reduction. For this reason, we often feel uncertain about the identity of a friend seen from a distance.

---

### 534.

#### WHY A PAINTING CAN NEVER APPEAR AS DETACHED FROM THE BACKGROUND AS NATURAL OBJECTS DO.

Painters often fall into despair regarding their imitation of nature when they see that their paintings lack the depth and vividness of objects seen in a mirror. They claim they possess colors that, in terms of brightness or darkness, far exceed the quality of light and shadow in the objects seen in the mirror. In doing so, they reveal their own ignorance rather than the true cause, because they do not understand it. It is impossible for a painted object to appear with such depth that it resembles objects in a mirror—even though both are on a flat surface—unless they are seen...

--- Page 369 ---
**ON JUDGING A PICTURE.**

...points, which is the ultimate foolishness of such masters; we know that the point of sight is placed at the eye of the spectator of the scene. And if you want me to tell you how to represent the life of a saint divided into several scenes on one and the same wall, I answer that you must set out the foreground with its point of sight on a level with the eye of the spectator of the scene. Upon this plane, represent the more important part of the story on a large scale; then, by gradually diminishing the figures and the buildings on various hills and plains, you can represent all the events of the history. On the remainder of the wall up to the top, paint trees that are large compared to the figures, or angels if they are appropriate to the story, or birds, clouds, or similar objects; otherwise, do not trouble yourself with it, for your entire work will be wrong.

---

### 543.

**A PICTURE OF OBJECTS IN PERSPECTIVE WILL LOOK MORE LIFELIKE WHEN SEEN FROM THE POINT FROM WHICH THE OBJECTS WERE DRAWN.**

If you want to represent an object near to you which is to have the effect of nature, it is impossible for your perspective not to look wrong—with every false depiction and discordant proportion imaginable in a wretched work—unless the spectator, when looking at it, has his eye at the exact distance, height, and direction where the eye or the point of sight was placed when creating that perspective. Therefore, it would be necessary to make a window, or rather a hole the size of your face, through which you could look at the work. If you do this, your work will undoubtedly have the effect of nature, provided it is well-balanced in light and shade; indeed, you will hardly be able to convince yourself that those objects are painted. Otherwise, do not trouble yourself with it, unless you make your view at least...

--- Page 371 ---
545. ON JUDGING A PICTURE.

The columns are gradually and entirely lost to view. Therefore, if your method of perspective is correct, it will produce the same effect; this effect occurs when standing near the line where the columns are placed. This method will lack grace unless the depicted object is viewed through a small hole, in the center of which your point of sight is located. If you do this, your work will be perfect and will deceive the viewers, and the depicted columns will appear in the form illustrated below.

In these, the eye is in the middle at point *a* and is near the columns.

## 545.

If you cannot ensure that the people viewing your work stand at a single point, then when you design your work, withdraw your eye to a distance at least 20 times the maximum height or width of your work. This will create so little variation when the spectator’s eye moves that it will hardly be noticed, and it will be very commendable.

If the point of sight is at *t*, you should make the figures placed on the circle *d b e* of the same size.

--- Page 372 ---
...of one size, since each of them bears the same relation to point $t$. But consider the diagram given below and you will see that this is not the case, and why I shall make $b$ smaller than $d$ $e$.

It is easy to understand that if two equal objects are placed side by side, the one situated at a distance of three braccia will appear smaller than the one placed at two braccia. This matter, however, is more theoretical than practical, because you are standing close by.

All objects in the foreground, whether they are large or small, should be drawn in their proper size; if you see them from a distance, they will appear as they ought, and if you see them from nearby, they will diminish on their own.

Ensure that your vertical plane, upon which you diminish the objects seen, is of the same shape as the wall where the work itself is to be executed.

---

### 546.

#### ON PAINTING.

The size of a painted figure ought to show you the distance from which it is being viewed; if you see a figure that is life-sized, you know that it appears to be close to the eye.

---

### 547.

#### WHERE A SPECTATOR SHOULD STAND TO LOOK AT A PICTURE.

Suppose that $a$ $b$ is the picture being viewed and that $d$ is the light. I say that if you place yourself between $c$ and $e$, you will not be able to understand the painting well...

--- Page 373 ---
...understand the picture well, and especially if it is done in oil or truly varnished, because it will have a luster and be almost like a mirror. For this reason, the closer you approach point $c$, the less you will see, because the rays of light sent from the window to the painting reflect there. But if you place yourself between $e$ and $d$, your vision will be well-served, and even more so as you approach point $d$, because that location is less affected by the impact of those reflected rays.

--- Page 375 ---
And every continuous quantity is infinitely divisible; therefore, the varieties of the lengths of the lines extending from the light source to the illuminated object are infinite. The proportion of the lights will be the same as the proportion of the lengths of the lines between them, extending from the center of the luminous body to the parts of that illuminated object.

### 549.

**HOW THE PAINTER MUST POSITION HIMSELF RELATIVE TO THE LIGHT TO CREATE THE EFFECT OF RELIEF.**

Let $a$ $b$ be the window and $m$ be the point of light. I say that in whatever position the painter stands, he will be well-placed as long as his eye is between the shaded part and the illuminated part of the body being portrayed; you will find this location by placing yourself between point $m$ and the division that the shadow makes from the light upon the body being portrayed.

### 550.

**THAT SHADOWS CREATED BY SPECIFIC LIGHT SOURCES SHOULD BE AVOIDED BECAUSE THEIR ENDS ARE SIMILAR TO THEIR BEGINNINGS.**

Shadows created by the sun or other specific light sources lack grace on the body they accompany, because they leave the parts confusedly divided by a distinct boundary between shadow and light; and the shadows are of equal intensity at the end as they are at the beginning.

--- Page 376 ---
### 551.

**HOW LIGHT SHOULD BE THROWN UPON FIGURES.**

The light must be arranged in accordance with the natural conditions under which you wish to represent your figures: that is, if you represent them in the sunshine, make the shadows dark with large spaces of light, and mark their shadows and those of all the surrounding objects strongly on the ground. And if you represent them as in dull weather, give little difference between light and shade, without any shadows at their feet. If you represent them as indoors, make a strong difference between the lights and shadows, with shadows on the ground. If the window is screened and the walls are white, there will be little difference in light. If it is lighted by firelight, make the high lights ruddy and strong, and the shadows dark; and those cast on the walls and on the floor will be clearly defined, and the farther they are from the body, the broader and longer they will be. If the light comes partly from the fire and partly from the daylight outside, the daylight will be the stronger and that of the fire will be almost as red as fire itself. Above all, see that the figures you paint are broadly lighted and from above—that is to say, all living persons that you paint; for you will see that all the people you meet out in the street are lighted from above, and you must know that if you saw your most intimate friend with a light on his face from below, you would find it difficult to recognize him.

---

### 552.

**OF HELPING THE APPARENT RELIEF OF A PICTURE BY GIVING IT ARTIFICIAL LIGHT AND SHADE.**

To increase the relief of a picture, you may place, between your figure and the solid object on which its shadow falls, a line of bright light, dividing the figure from the object in shadow. And on the same object, you shall represent two light parts which will surround the shadow cast upon the wall.

--- Page 378 ---
The second point is that the distribution or arrangement of the figures should be organized according to the specific requirements of the subject you are depicting. The third point is that the figures should be depicted with an active focus on their particular actions.

---

### ON THE TREATMENT OF THE LIGHTS.

First, apply a general shadow across the entire area that is not exposed to the light. Then, add the medium shadows and the primary shadows, comparing them against one another. In the same manner, apply the general light in a medium tone, subsequently adding the half-lights and the highlights, likewise comparing them with each other.

---

### ON SHADOWS ON BODIES.

When you depict dark shadows on shaded bodies, always show the cause of that darkness; do the same with reflections, because dark shadows are produced by dark objects, and reflections are produced by objects of low brightness—that is, by diminished light. The proportion between the illuminated part of a body and the part brightened by a reflection is the same as the proportion between the source of the body's light and the source of that reflection.

---

### ON LIGHTS AND SHADOWS.

I remind you to ensure that every part of the body, and every tiny detail that has even the slightest amount of relief, is given its proper primary shadows and lights.

--- Page 379 ---
### 558.

HOW TO MAKE THE SHADOW ON FIGURES CORRESPOND TO THE LIGHT AND TO THE COLOR OF THE BODY.

When you draw a figure and you wish to see whether the shadow corresponds correctly to the light, and that it is neither redder nor yellower than the natural color you intend to represent in shade, proceed as follows: cast a shadow with your finger upon the illuminated part, and if the accidental shadow you have created resembles the natural shadow cast by your finger upon your work, it is correct; and by moving your finger closer or farther away, you can create darker or lighter shadows, which you should always compare with your own painted shadow.

### 559.

ON SURROUNDING BODIES WITH VARIOUS FORMS OF SHADOW.

Ensure that the shadows cast upon the surfaces of bodies by various objects always undulate with different curves, according to the variety of the limbs that cast the shadows and the shape of the object that receives that shadow.

### 560.

ON PAINTING.

The juxtaposition of light and shade.

The comparison of the various qualities of shadows and lights often makes the subject appear ambiguous and confusing to the painter who wishes to imitate and copy the things he sees. The reason is this: if you see a white cloth next to a black one, that part of the white cloth which borders the black will certainly appear much whiter than the part which borders a brighter white; and the reason for this is proven in my book on perspective.

--- Page 381 ---
### 564.

A most important part of painting consists in the backgrounds of the objects represented; against these backgrounds, the outlines of those natural bodies which have a convex curvature are always visible. The forms of such bodies are recognizable against these backgrounds even if the colors of the bodies are the same as the color of the background. This occurs because the convex edges of the bodies are not illuminated in the same way that the background is illuminated by the same light; consequently, such an edge will often be lighter or darker than the background itself. However, if the edge is of the same color as the background, that part of the painting will undoubtedly prevent the recognition of the figure's outline. Such a choice in painting is to be avoided by the judgment of good painters, since the intention of the painter is to make his bodies appear to stand out in front of the backgrounds; whereas in the case mentioned above, the opposite happens, not only in painting but in objects in relief.

---

### 565.

How you ought, when representing objects that are above the eye and to one side—if you want them to appear detached from the wall—to place a middle light between the original shadow and the derivative shadow, and the object will appear separated from the wall.

---

### 566.

**HOW WHITE BODIES SHOULD BE REPRESENTED.**

If you represent a white body, let it be surrounded by plenty of air, because white has no color of its own, but is tinted and partially transformed into the color of whatever is placed before it.

--- Page 383 ---
<page-num>285</page-num>
<header>ON AERIAL PERSPECTIVE.</header>
<meta>568. 569.]</meta>

If a man is at a distance from you of an arrow’s flight, and you hold the eye of a small needle close to your own eye, you can see through it several men whose images are transmitted to the eye and will all be contained within the size of the needle’s eye. Hence, if the man who is at the distance of an arrow’s flight can send his whole image to your eye, occupying only a small space in the needle’s eye, how can you expect in so small a figure to distinguish or see the nose or mouth or any detail of his person? And, not seeing these, you cannot recognize the man, since these features, which he does not show, are what give men different appearances.

<margin>Ash. I. 4a]</margin>

## 568.

**THE REASON WHY SMALL FIGURES SHOULD NOT BE FINISHED.**

I say that the reason objects appear diminished in size is because they are remote from the eye; this being the case, it is evident that there must be a great extent of atmosphere between the eye and the objects, and this air interferes with the distinctness of the forms of the objects. Hence, the minute details of these objects will be indistinguishable and unrecognizable. Therefore, O Painter, make your smaller figures merely indicated and not highly finished; otherwise, you will act contrary to the effects of nature, your supreme guide. The object is small because of the great distance between it and the eye; this great distance is filled with air, and that mass of air forms a dense body which intervenes and prevents the eye from seeing the minute details of objects.

<margin>G. 53 b]</margin>

## 569.

Whenever a figure is placed at a considerable distance, you lose first the distinctness of the smallest parts, while the larger parts are preserved the longest, eventually losing all distinctness of detail and outline; what remains is an oval or spherical shape with confused edges.

--- Page 388 ---
point, the side illuminated by it will have its brightest light on the portion where the line of radiance falls at equal angles, as is shown above in lines *a g*, *a h*, and *l a*. That portion of the illuminated side will be less luminous where the line of incidence strikes it between two more unequal angles, as is seen at *b c d*. By this method, you may also identify which parts are deprived of light, as seen at *m k*.

Where the angles made by the lines of incidence are most equal, there will be the brightest light; and where they are most unequal, it will be darkest.

I will make further mention of the principles of reflections.

### 576.

Where the shadow should be placed on the face.

### 577.

General suggestions for historical pictures (577-581).

When you compose a historical picture, establish two points: one for the eye (the viewpoint) and the other for the source of light. Place the light source as far away as possible.

---

### 578.

Historical pictures should not be crowded or confused with too many figures.

---

### 579.

### PRECEPTS IN PAINTING.

Let your sketches of historical pictures be done quickly, and do not finish the details of the limbs too thoroughly. Limit yourself to the positions of these limbs, which you can later finish at your leisure as you please.

Elderly men see better from a distance than from nearby, because the elderly eye sees distant things better than those close at hand. This is because the same rays that depart from the image of the object to the convergence of the eye settle upon its pupil more effectively from a distance than from nearby. For this reason, the part of the eye that receives the images is placed at r . s . t . And if the rays converge at a point at m . f ., the elderly eye sees poorly from nearby because the rays do not arrive from a distance in a straight line, but with a crooked line and with lines differing from one another, for which reason the eye sees the object as shadowy and confused.

---

### 586.

**Of the delusions which arise in judging the limbs.**

A painter who has clumsy hands will paint similar hands in his works, and the same will happen with any limb, unless long study prevents it. Therefore, you painter, look carefully at which part of your own person is most ill-favored, and apply yourself with great effort to correct it. For if you are coarse, your figures will appear the same and devoid of skill; and similarly, every quality, whether good or bad, that you have within yourself will be revealed, in part, in your figures.

---

### 587.

**Of the selection of beautiful faces.**

It seems to me to be no small grace in a painter when he gives a pleasing air to his figures.

--- Page 396 ---
...and this grace, if he does not possess it by nature, he may acquire through incidental study in this way: Look about you and take the best parts of many beautiful faces, whose beauty is confirmed more by public reputation than by your own judgment; for you might be mistaken and choose faces that resemble your own. It seems that such resemblances often please us; and if you were ugly, you would select faces that were not beautiful and would then create ugly faces, as many painters do. For often a master's work resembles himself. Therefore, select beauties as I tell you, and fix them in your mind.

---

### 588.

Of the limbs, which ought to be carefully selected, and of all the other parts with regard to painting.

---

### 589.

When selecting figures, you should choose slender ones rather than lean and wooden ones.

---

### 590.

**OF THE MUSCLES OF ANIMALS.**

The hollow spaces placed between the muscles must not be of such a character that the skin appears to cover two sticks laid side by side like *c*, nor should they look like two sticks somewhat distant from such contact so that the skin hangs in an empty, loose curve as at *f*; but it should be like *i*, resting over the spongy fat that lies in the angles such as the angle *n m o*. This angle is formed by the contact of the ends of the muscles, and since the skin cannot sink down into such an angle, nature has filled these angles with a small quantity of spongy and, as I might say, vesicular fat, containing minute bladders full of air, which is condensed or thinned within them according to the increase or the reduction of the substance of the muscles; in which latter case the concavity *i* always has a wider curve than the muscle.

--- Page 397 ---
### 591.

**ON THE UNDULATING MOVEMENT AND BALANCE OF FIGURES AND OTHER ANIMALS.**

When representing a human figure or any graceful animal, remember to avoid a wooden stiffness; that is to say, make them move with counterpoise and balance so that they do not look like a piece of wood. However, those you wish to represent as strong, you must not depict in this manner, except for the turn of the head.

---

### 592.

**ON THE GRACE OF THE LIMBS.**

The limbs of the body should be arranged with grace in accordance with the effect you want the figure to produce. If you want to create a figure that displays elegance, you must make the limbs graceful and extended, without showing too many muscles; and those few that your purpose requires you to show, make them soft—that is, with little prominence and with shadows that are not harsh. The limbs, and especially the arms, should be easy; that is, no limb should be in a straight line with the part to which it is joined. And if the hips, which are the axis of a man, are positioned so that the right is higher than the left because of the pose, you should make the joint of the higher shoulder fall in a perpendicular line above the most prominent part of the hip, and let that right shoulder be lower than the left. The pit of the throat should always be positioned above the center of the joint of the foot that is bearing the weight. The leg that is not bearing weight should have its knee lower than the other and close to the other leg. The positions of the head and arms are infinite, so I will not expand upon any specific rules for them; however, let them be easy and pleasing, with various turns and twists and with the joints flexibly bent, so that they do not look like pieces of wood.

--- Page 398 ---
### 593.

**Of appropriate gestures (593—600).**

A painting, or the figures depicted within it, must be made in such a way that the spectators can easily recognize the purpose of their minds through their attitudes. If you have to represent a man of good character, ensure that his actions match his virtuous words. Similarly, if you have to represent a brutal man, depict him with fierce movements, flinging his arms toward the listener, with his head and chest thrust forward beyond his feet to accompany the speaker's hands. This is similar to a deaf-mute who, seeing two people talking—even though he is deprived of hearing—nevertheless understands the theme of their dispute through their expressions and actions. I once saw in Florence a man who had become deaf; if you spoke loudly to him, he did not understand you, but if you spoke softly without any sound of voice, he understood you solely by the movement of the lips. Now, you might tell me that a person who speaks loudly does not move their lips the same way as one who speaks softly, and that if they moved them the same way, one would be understood as well as the other. On this point, I leave the judgment to experience; have someone speak softly and observe the movement of their lips.

---

### 594.

**ON REPRESENTING A PERSON SPEAKING AMONG SEVERAL PEOPLE.**

When you wish to represent someone speaking among many people, you should consider the subject matter he has to discuss and adapt his gestures to the topic.

--- Page 399 ---
Give him the gestures appropriate to the subject matter; that is, if the subject is persuasive, let the gestures be suitable to that purpose. If the subject is an explanation based on various reasons, let the speaker take one finger of the left hand with the fingers of the right hand, having the two smaller fingers closed. Let his face be alert and turned toward the people, with the mouth slightly open so that he appears to be speaking. If he is sitting, let him appear to be rising slightly and leaning forward with his head; and if you depict him standing, make him lean slightly with his chest and head toward the people.

--- Page 400 ---
You should represent them as silent and attentive, all looking at the orator's face with expressions of admiration. Depict the mouth of some old man, astonished at the profound ideas he hears, with the corners of his mouth pulled down and drawn back, creating many folds in his cheeks, and with his eyebrows raised where they meet, creating many wrinkles across his forehead. Show some who are sitting with the fingers of their hands clasped together, holding their weary knees; others with one knee crossed over the other, upon which he rests a hand that supports the elbow of his other arm, while that hand supports the bearded chin of the stooping old man.

---

### ON THE DISPOSITION OF THE LIMBS.

Regarding the disposition of these limbs, you must consider that when you wish to represent someone who, for some reason, has to turn backward or to the side, you should not make him move his feet and all his limbs in the same direction that he turns his head. Instead, you should create the movement by distributing the twist among the joints—namely those of the foot, the knee, the hip, and the neck. If he rests his weight on the right leg, make the left knee bend inward, with the foot slightly raised on the outside; the left shoulder should be somewhat lower than the right, and the nape of the neck should align with the same spot where the outer ankle of the left foot is positioned. The left shoulder should be in a perpendicular line above the toes of the right foot. Always pose your figures so that the chest does not face the same direction as the head; for nature, for our convenience, has given us a neck that can easily serve different directions when the eye wishes to look at various places. To this same end, the other joints are also partially compliant. If you depict a man sitting, and his arms must occasionally be used for something off to the side, make the chest turn upon the hip joint.

--- Page 401 ---
### 596.

When you draw nudes, always sketch the figures in their entirety first, and then finish whichever limb seems best to you. Apply the style of that limb to the others; otherwise, you will develop a habit of never joining the limbs properly to the body.

Never make the head turn in the same direction as the chest, nor let the arm and leg move together on the same side. If the head is turned toward the right shoulder, make its features lower on the left side than on the right; and if you depict the chest facing outward, and the head turns to the left, make the features on the right side higher than those on the left.

---

### 597.

#### OF PAINTING.

The nature of movements in man: Do not repeat the same actions in a person's limbs unless the necessity of their task compels you to do so, as is shown in *a b*.

---

### 598.

The movements of men must be such as reflect their dignity or their baseness.

---

### 599.

#### OF PAINTING.

Ensure that the work corresponds to the purpose and the intention; that is, when you create your figure, consider carefully who it is and what you want it to be doing.

--- Page 402 ---

--- Page 403 ---
### V.

## SUGGESTIONS FOR COMPOSITIONS.

**HOW TO REPRESENT A BATTLE.**

First, you must represent the smoke of the artillery mingling in the air with the dust stirred up by the movement of the horses and the combatants. You should depict this mixture in the following way: because dust is an earthy substance, it has weight; and although its fineness allows it to be easily tossed up and mixed into the air, it nevertheless tends to fall back down. Its highest point is reached by its finest parts; therefore, that portion will be the least visible and will appear almost the same color as the air. The higher the smoke rises toward a certain height while mixed with the dust-laden air, the more it will look like a dark cloud; and at the top, the smoke will be seen more clearly than the dust. The smoke will lean toward a somewhat bluish color, while the dust will retain its own natural color. On the side from which the light comes, this mixture of air, smoke, and dust will appear much brighter than on the opposite side. The more the combatants are positioned within this turmoil, the less visible they will be, and there will be less contrast between their highlights and their shadows. You must make their faces, their figures, their general appearance, and the musketeers as well as those near them, a glowing red. This glow will diminish in proportion to its distance from its source.

--- Page 404 ---
You shall make the faces, the figures, their general appearance, and the musketeers together with those near them, glow with a reddish light. This redness should fade the further it moves from its source. The figures that are between you and the light, if they are at a distance, will appear dark against a light background; and the lower parts of their legs will be less visible the closer they are to the ground, because there the dust is coarsest and densest. And if you depict horses galloping outside the crowd, make the small clouds of dust separated from one another according to the interval of the strides made by the horse; and the cloud that is furthest from the horse should be the least visible—indeed, it should be high, scattered, and thin—while the one nearest should be more evident, smaller, and denser. The air should be full of arrows of various kinds: some ascending, some descending, and some flying on a level line. The balls from the muskets should be accompanied by a trail of smoke following their course. You shall make the figures in the foreground dusty, specifically the hair, eyebrows, and other flat areas suited to holding dust. You shall depict the victors rushing forward with their hair and other light garments streaming in the wind, with their brows lowered, and thrusting the opposite limbs forward; that is, if a man puts forward his right foot, the left arm should also come forward. And if you depict anyone who has fallen, you must show the mark of his sliding through the dust, which has been turned into a bloody mire; and in the partially liquid earth, you shall show the imprinted footprints of the men and horses that have passed there. Depict a horse dragging its dead master, and behind it, leave the mark of the dragged body in the dust and mud. You shall make the conquered and the beaten pale, with their eyebrows raised where they meet, and the skin above them should be full of painful wrinkles. The sides of the nose should have several wrinkles forming an arc from the nostrils to the eyes, and make the nostrils flared—which is the cause of the lines I mentioned—and the lips arched upward, revealing the upper teeth, with the teeth apart as if crying out.

--- Page 405 ---
...extending from the nostrils and ending at the corner of the eye. The nostrils should be flared, causing these folds; the arched lips should reveal the upper teeth, and the teeth should be parted as if shouting in lamentation. Have one of the figures shield his terrified eyes with one hand, turning the palm toward the enemy, while the other hand rests on the ground to support his raised torso. You shall depict others shouting with their mouths wide open and fleeing. You must scatter many kinds of weapons among the feet of the combatants, such as broken shields, lances, broken swords, and other similar things. You shall depict dead men, some half-covered by dust and others entirely covered. The dust that mixes with the flowing blood turns into red mud, and you should see the blood, in its natural color, running in a winding course from the body into the dust. Others, as they die, should be grinding their teeth, rolling their eyes, clenching their fists against their bodies, and have their legs contorted. One might see someone disarmed and beaten down by the enemy, turning upon his foe with bites and scratches to take a cruel and bitter revenge. You might see a light horse running with its mane streaming in the wind among the enemies, doing great damage with its hooves. You would see someone who has been maimed falling to the ground and shielding himself with his buckler, while the enemy, leaning down low, struggles to deal him a deathblow. One might see many men fallen in a heap over a dead horse. You will see some of the victors stop fighting and leave the crowd, wiping their eyes and cheeks with both hands, as they are covered in the mud created by the watering of their eyes caused by the dust. You would see the reserve squads standing full of hope and apprehension, with their brows knit, shading their eyes with their hands and peering through the thick and confused haze, being attentive to the commander's orders. Similarly, the captain, with his staff raised, should be running toward the reserves to show them the area where they are most needed. And there may be a river with horses running through it, filling the surrounding water with turbulent waves, foam, and splashing water leaping into the air and between the legs and bodies of the horses. And do not make any spot of ground level, except for the footprints filled with blood.

--- Page 406 ---
### 603.

### ON THE LIGHTING OF THE LOWER PARTS OF BODIES CROWDED TOGETHER, AS OF MEN IN BATTLE.

Regarding men and horses engaged in battle, their various parts will be darker in proportion to how close they are to the ground that supports them. This is proven by the sides of wells, which grow darker the deeper they go; this occurs because the deepest part of the well sees and is seen by a smaller portion of the luminous sky than any other part.

And the pavement, if it is the same color as the legs of the aforementioned men and horses, will always be more illuminated at equal angles than the aforementioned legs, etc.

---

### 604.

### ON THE METHOD OF DEPICTING A NIGHT SCENE.

That which is entirely deprived of light is total darkness. Since night is in such a condition, if you wish to represent a narrative scene in it, arrange it so there is a great fire. Then, whatever is closest to that fire will be most saturated with its color, because the closer an object is to a colored source, the more it partakes of its nature. Since you are making the fire tend toward a red color, you must make everything illuminated by it also appear reddish; and those things that are farther from the fire will be more tinted by the black color of the night. The figures seen against the fire appear dark against its brightness, because the side of the object you see is tinted by the darkness of the night and not by the brightness of the fire. Those who are at the sides should be half dark and half reddish, and those who can be seen beyond the edges of the flames will be entirely illuminated by a reddish light against a black background. As for their actions, make those who are near the fire shield themselves with their hands and cloaks as a protection against the excessive heat, turning away their faces.

PL. XXXVIII

This page is a heliogravure reproduction of several manuscript fragments by Leonardo da Vinci. It features Leonardo's characteristic "mirror writing" alongside a section of normal script. The fragments contain scientific observations on fluid dynamics (water and air) and anatomical or figure sketches. The labels 1, 2, and 3 indicate separate fragments arranged for reproduction.

Much of the text is written in mirror script, which is reversed and read from right to left. The transcription below provides the normal script and indicates the presence of mirror script where applicable.

---

### Fragment 1

When it rises above the air, it will not move with equal speed in all parts of its thickness; rather, that part which is furthest from the center line of its thickness will descend more slowly. This happens because the part furthest from the center is more mixed with the air than the part moving in the middle; for this reason, it becomes lighter, and the lighter it is, the slower it becomes. The same should be understood regarding smoke in its motion, which is the opposite of that of water.

...that water also, when it is struck...
...by other waters...
...becomes lighter...

[Large block of mirror script on the right, likely discussing the mechanics of motion and gravity in fluids]
...every motion...
...from where such motion arises...

---

...every motion of gravity is...
...in the water...

### 618.

To make a fine green, take green and mix it with bitumen, and you will make the shadows darker. Then, for lighter shades, green with yellow ochre; and for still lighter, green with yellow; and for the high lights, pure yellow. Then mix green and turmeric together and glaze everything with it. To make a fine red, take cinnabar or red chalk or burnt ochre for the dark shadows, and for the lighter ones, red chalk and vermilion, and for the lights, pure vermilion; then glaze with fine lake. To make good oil for painting: one part of oil, one of the first refining, and one of the second.

---

### 619.

Use black in the shadow, and in the lights: white, yellow, green, vermilion, and lake. Medium shadows: take the shadow as described above and mix it with the flesh tints just alluded to, adding to it a little yellow and a little green and occasionally some lake; for the shadows, take green and lake for the middle shades.

---

### 620.

You can make a fine ochre by the same method as you use to make white.

--- Page 436 ---
### 621.

**A FINE YELLOW.**

Dissolve realgar with one part of orpiment, using aqua fortis.

**WHITE.**

Put the white lead into an earthen pot, and spread it no thicker than a string; let it stand undisturbed in the sun and the open air for two days. Ensure that in the morning, when the sun has dried off the night dew...

---

### 622.

To make reddish black for flesh tints: take red rock crystals from Rocca Nova or garnets and mix them briefly; armenian bole is also partially effective.

---

### 623.

The shadow shall be burnt terra-verte.

---

### 624.

**THE PROPORTIONS OF COLOURS.**

If one ounce of black mixed with one ounce of white produces one degree of darkness, how many degrees of darkness will be produced by two ounces of black mixed with one ounce of white?

---

### 625.

Refine black and greenish yellow, and finally blue.

---

### 626.

Verdigris with aloes, gall, or turmeric makes a fine green; so does saffron or burnt orpiment, but I suspect that in a short time they may turn black. Ultramarine blue and yellow of...

--- Page 440 ---
### 635.
VARNISH [OR POWDER].

Take cypress oil and distill it. Take a large pitcher and put the extract in it with enough water to make it look like amber. Seal it tightly so that none of it evaporates. When it has dissolved, you may add more of the same solution into the pitcher until it reaches the consistency you prefer. You should know that amber is the resin of the cypress tree.

VARNISH [OR POWDER].

Since varnish is the resin of juniper, if you distill juniper, you can dissolve the varnish in that essence using the method described above.

### 636.
VARNISH [OR POWDER].

Cut a notch into a juniper tree and water its roots. Mix the liquid that seeps out with walnut oil, and you will have a perfect varnish, similar to amber varnish, of excellent and fine quality. Make this in May or April.

### 637.
VARNISH [OR POWDER].

Mercury with Jupiter and Venus—once a paste is made from these, it must be worked with the mold continuously until the Mercury separates itself entirely from Jupiter and Venus.

### 638.
Note how spirits of wine absorb all the colors and scents of flowers. If you want to make blue, put irises into it; for red, use nightshade berries.

--- Page 442 ---
### 643.

Pitch four ounces; virgin wax four ounces, incense two ounces, oil of roses one ounce.

---

### 644.

Four ounces of virgin wax, four ounces of Greek pitch, two ounces of incense, one ounce of oil of roses. First melt the wax and oil, then the Greek pitch, then the other ingredients in powder form.

---

### 645.

Very thin glass may be cut with scissors; when placed over inlaid work of bone that is gilded or stained with other colors, you can saw it through together with the bone and then assemble it. It will retain a luster that will not be scratched or worn away by rubbing with the hand.

---

### 646.

TO DILUTE WHITE WINE AND MAKE IT DARK.

Pulverize gall nuts and let them stand for eight days in white wine; in the same way, dissolve vitriol in water. Let the water and the wine each settle until they are very clear, and strain them well. When you dilute the white wine with this water, it will turn red.

---

### 647.

Put marcasite into aqua fortis, and if it turns green, know that it contains copper. Extract it with saltpeter and soft soap.

---

### 648.

A white horse may have its spots removed with Spanish hematite or aqua fortis, or otherwise with depilatory. Remove the black hair from the white horse with a cauterizing iron. Force it to the ground.

# VII.

## PHILOSOPHY AND HISTORY OF THE ART OF PAINTING.

---

The relation of art and nature (651. 652).

### 651.

What is beautiful in mortals passes away, but not so in art.

---

### 652.

**HE WHO DESPISES PAINTING LOVES NEITHER PHILOSOPHY NOR NATURE.**

If you despise painting, which is the sole imitator of all the visible works of nature, you will certainly despise a subtle invention which, with philosophical and subtle speculation, considers all the qualities of forms: seas and landscapes, trees, animals, plants, and flowers, all of which are surrounded by shadow and light. And truly this is science, and the legitimate daughter of nature, because painting is born of nature itself; but to speak more accurately, we will call it the grandchild of nature, because all visible things have been produced by nature, and from these things painting is born. Therefore, we may rightly call it the grandchild of nature and related to God.

--- Page 447 ---
...produced by nature, and these her children have given birth to painting. Hence we may justly call it the grandchild of nature and related to God.

---

### 653.

**THAT PAINTING SURPASSES ALL HUMAN WORKS BY THE SUBTLE CONSIDERATIONS BELONGING TO IT.**

The eye, which is called the window of the soul, is the principal path by which the common sense can most abundantly and magnificently consider the infinite works of nature; and the ear is the second, which gains nobility through the things recounted that the eye has seen. If you historians, or poets, or other mathematicians had not seen things with your eyes, you could hardly report them in your writings. And if you, O poet, depict a story with the painting of your pen, the painter with his brush will make it more easily satisfying and less tedious to be understood. If you call painting "dumb poetry," the painter may likewise call poetry "blind painting." Now consider which is the more damaging affliction: to be blind or to be mute. Although the poet is as free as the painter in his inventions, his fictions do not provide as much satisfaction to men as paintings do; for while poetry extends itself through words to describe forms, actions, and places, the painter moves with the actual likenesses of those forms to represent them. Now consider which is nearer to the man: the name of the man or the likeness of that man. The name of the man varies in different countries, but his form is never changed except by death.

---

### 654.

And if the poet serves the sense by way of the ear, and the painter by the eye—the worthier sense—I desire nothing more from such people than that a good painter depict the fury of a battle and that a poet write of another, and that they be placed before the public together. You will see where the spectators will stop more often, where they will reflect more, where more praise will be given, and which will satisfy them better. Certainly painting, being by a long way more useful and...

--- Page 448 ---
...beautiful, will please more. Write the name of God in one place and set His image opposite it, and you will see which is more revered. If painting embraces within itself all the forms of nature, you have nothing but names, which are not universal like forms; if you have the effects of demonstrations, we have the demonstrations of effects. Take a poet who describes the beauty of a lady to her lover, and take a painter who represents her, and you will see where nature will more strongly turn the enamored judge. Certainly, the trial of these things should allow experience to deliver the verdict. You have ranked painting among the mechanical arts; certainly, if painters were as capable of praising their own works in writing as you are, I doubt it would rest under such a vile title. If you call it mechanical because it is primarily manual—in that the hands represent what they find in the imagination—you writers also manually draw with the pen what is found in your intellect. And if you say it is mechanical because it is done for a price, who falls into this error—if it can be called an error—more than you? If you lecture in the schools, do you not go to whoever pays you most? Do you perform any work without some reward? I do not say this to blame such opinions, for every labor expects a reward; and a poet may say, "I will create a fiction that signifies great things," and the painter will do the same, as Apelles did with Calumny. If you say that poetry is more eternal, for this reason I will say that the works of a coppersmith are more eternal, since time preserves them longer than your works or ours; nevertheless, they show little imagination. And painting can, by being painted on copper with glass colors, be made much more eternal. Through art, we may be called the grandsons of God. If poetry extends into moral philosophy, painting extends into natural philosophy; if the former describes the operations of the mind, the latter considers what the mind effects through movements. If the former terrifies people with hellish fictions, the latter does the same with those same things in action. Let the poet set himself to represent a beauty, a ferocity, a base and ugly thing, or a monstrosity alongside the painter; let him make transformations in his own way as he wishes...

--- Page 449 ---
the movements of the limbs. If poetry terrifies people with descriptions of hellish battles, painting does the same by showing those same battles in action. If poetry describes figures, or elements, or landscapes, painting does the same with greater truth. And if a poet claims to incite people to love, which is the central focus of all living things, the painter has the power to do so to an even greater degree. This is because the painter can place before the lover the true image of the beloved, which he often kisses and speaks to—something he would never do with the descriptions of a writer. The painter even more strongly compels the mind of a man to love and fall in enamored with a painting that does not represent any living woman. It once happened to me that I made a painting representing a divine subject, which was bought by someone who loved it so much that he wanted me to remove the symbols of divinity so that he could kiss it without guilt. But eventually, his conscience overcame his desire and sighing, he was forced to remove it from his house. Now, let the poet go and describe a beauty to a man without showing him a representation of the thing described, and see if he can provoke the man to such desires. If you say, "But I can describe a battle that represents a great slaughter," the painter can do the same, and even more so, because he places before you the very scene of the conflict, where the smoke of the artillery mingles with the air, and the dust raised by the horses' hooves obscures the atmosphere. Here, you see the terrified soldiers fleeing through the dust, and there, the brave men performing deeds of great valor. If you say that poetry is more enduring because the words last longer, I say that the work of a sculptor is even more enduring, and that of a painter, if done on a thick plate of copper, is more enduring still. If you say that poetry can reach many people because it is easily copied and distributed, I say that painting can also be multiplied by printing from engravings. And if you say that poetry can express the inner thoughts of the mind, I say that painting can express the same through the gestures and movements of the body. See then, if the poet can show the beauty of the heavens and the earth as the painter can, who brings before your eyes the flow of rivers, the variety of plants, and the vastness of the sea.

--- Page 450 ---
...lied through the art of printing. And if you say that music is the sister of painting, I say that it is inferior, because it is a fleeting thing that dies as soon as it is born, whereas painting preserves the beauty of a face for many years. Painting represents the works of nature to the senses with more truth and certainty than words or letters can, but letters represent words with more truth than painting does. We say that the science which represents the works of nature is more worthy than that which represents the works of the human creator, such as poetry and similar things, which pass through the human tongue. If you call painting "mute poetry," the painter can call poetry "blind painting." Now, consider which is the more debilitating defect: to be blind or to be mute? Although the poet is as free as the painter in his inventions, his fictions do not give the same satisfaction to men as paintings do, because while poetry attempts to represent forms, honors, and landscapes with words, the painter represents them with the actual likeness of those forms. Consider, then, which is closer to man: the name of a man, or the image of that man? The name of a man changes in different countries, but his form is never changed except by death. And if the poet serves the understanding by way of the ear, the painter does so by way of the eye, which is the worthier sense. I will say no more, except that if a good painter represents the fury of a battle, and a poet describes another, and both are shown to the public together, you will see where the spectators will stop, where they will gaze most, where they will give more praise, and which will satisfy them better. Truly, painting, being much more useful and beautiful, will please more. Write the name of God in one place and place His image opposite, and you will see which is more revered. While painting includes in itself all the forms of nature, you have nothing but names, which are not universal like forms. If you have the effects of demonstrations, we have the demonstrations of the effects.

--- Page 451 ---
...e forms, so that the painter no longer satisfies; have we not seen paintings have such conformity with the living thing that they have deceived men and animals?

---

# 655.

## WHY SCULPTURE IS LESS INTELLECTUAL THAN PAINTING AND LACKS MANY NATURAL CHARACTERISTICS.

Since I have practiced sculpture no less than painting, and perform both to the same degree, it seems to me that I can, with little risk of being biased, give a judgment as to which of the two is of greater intellect, difficulty, and perfection. First, sculpture is dependent on certain lights, namely from above, while a painting carries its own light and shadow with it everywhere. Thus, light and shadow are essential to sculpture; in this case, the sculptor is aided by the nature of the relief which it generates on its own, whereas the painter, through accidental art, creates it in the places where nature would reasonably produce it. The sculptor cannot diversify his work with the various natural colors of things; painting is not lacking in any part. The perspectives of sculptors do not seem at all true; those of the painter seem to extend for hundreds of miles beyond the work. Aerial perspective is absent from their work; they cannot represent transparent bodies, they cannot represent luminous bodies, nor reflected lines, nor shiny bodies like mirrors and similar lustrous things, nor mists, nor dark weather, nor infinite things that are not mentioned so as not to be tedious. What it does have is that it is more resistant to time, although painting has a similar resistance when done on thick copper covered with white enamel, painted upon with enamel colors, and then put back into the fire and baked; this, for eternity, surpasses sculpture. It might be said that where they make an error it is not easy to repair it; this is a poor argument to try to prove that an irremediable oversight makes the work more worthy, but I will certainly say that...

--- Page 452 ---
the mind of the master who makes such mistakes will be more difficult to correct than it is to repair the work he has spoiled.

## 656.

We know very well that a truly experienced and skilled artist will not make such mistakes; on the contrary, by following sound rules, he will remove so little at a time that he will bring his work to a successful conclusion. Furthermore, if the sculptor works in clay or wax, he can add or take away material, and when the model is finished, it can easily be cast in bronze; this is the final operation and the most permanent form that sculpture can take. Inasmuch as a work made only of marble is subject to ruin, whereas bronze is not. Therefore, a painting done on copper—which, as I said of painting, can be added to or altered—is equal to bronze; for when you first created that work in wax, it could also be altered or added to. If sculpture in bronze is eternal, then this work on copper and glass is even more everlasting. While bronze remains dark and dull, this medium is full of various and lovely colors in infinite variety, as mentioned above. And if you wish me to speak only of painting on wood panels, I am content to give my judgment between it and sculpture, saying this: just as painting is more beautiful, more imaginative, and more resourceful, sculpture is more durable, but it possesses nothing else. Sculpture displays with little effort what appears to be a miracle in painting: making intangible things appear tangible, making flat objects appear in relief, and making distant objects seem close. In effect, painting is adorned with infinite intellectual possibilities that sculpture does not employ.

### X.

# Studies and Sketches for Pictures and Decorations.

An artist’s manuscript notes can hardly be expected to contain anything more than incidental references to those masterpieces of his work whose fame, celebrated in the writings of his contemporaries, has left a glorious echo for posterity. We need not, therefore, be surprised to find that the texts reproduced here do not provide us with such comprehensive information as we might wish. On the other hand, the sketches and studies prepared by Leonardo for the two grandest compositions he ever executed—the fresco of the *Last Supper* in the refectory of *Santa Maria delle Grazie* in Milan, and the cartoon of the *Battle of Anghiari* for the *Palazzo della Signoria* in Florence—have been preserved. Although they are far from complete, they are so much more numerous than the manuscript notes that we are justified in asserting that, in value and interest, they amply compensate for the scarcity of the written suggestions.

The notes for the composition of the *Last Supper*, which are provided under numbers 665 and 666, appear in a manuscript at South Kensington, II², written in the years 1494–1495. This manuscript sketch was noted down no more than three or four years before the painting was executed, which justifies the inference that at the time it was written, the painter had not definitely decided even on the general scheme of the work. From this, we may also conclude that the red chalk drawings of the apostles’ heads at Windsor must be attributed to a later date. These include studies for the head of St. Matthew, the fourth figure on Christ’s left—see *Plate XLVII*; the black chalk sketch for the head of St. Philip, the third figure on the left—see *Plate XLVIII*; St. Peter’s right arm—see *Plate XLIX*; and the expressive head of Judas, which has unfortunately suffered somewhat from later restoration of its outlines—see *Plate L*. According to a tradition as unfounded as it is improbable, Leonardo used the head of Padre Bandelli, the prior of the convent, as the model for his Judas; however, this has already been contradicted by Amoretti in "*Memorie storiche*," chapter XIV. The study of the head of a criminal on *Plate LI* has, it seems to me, a better claim to be regarded as one of the preparatory sketches for the head of Judas. The

--- Page 460 ---
head of the Prior of the monastery as a model for the traitor. The original drawings for the heads of St. James the Greater and St. Andrew are in the possession of the Earl of Warwick, and that of St. Bartholomew is in the collection of the Duke of Devonshire at Chatsworth.

The text under number 667, which refers to the movement of the hands, was also written in 1494 or 1495. It was not until 1497 that the painting was completed, as we learn from a letter written by the Duke of Milan, Lodovico il Moro, to his secretary Marchesino Stanga, dated June 29th of that year. In this letter, he expresses his desire that Leonardo should finish the work he had begun in the Refectory of *Santa Maria delle Grazie*, so that he might then turn his attention to the other wall of the same room, where Montorfano had painted the Crucifixion.

The original painting, as is well known, has suffered so much from the dampness of the wall and from the repeated, clumsy attempts at restoration, that it is now only a shadow of its former self. However, the numerous contemporary copies, as well as the many engravings and photographs, allow us to form a fairly accurate idea of the composition. The most famous of these copies is the one by Marco d’Oggiono, now in the Royal Academy in London.

The following notes, which are found in the *Codex Atlanticus*, refer to the cost of the materials used for the painting. They are of interest as showing the high price of the colors employed by the artist.

--- Page 462 ---
<lang>None</lang>
<meta>Blank page</meta>

--- Page 463 ---
The Windsor collection contains two old copies of the head of St. Simon—the figure at the far left of Christ—both of roughly equal quality (they are marked as Nos. 21 and 36); the second was reproduced on Plate VIII of the Grosvenor Gallery Publication in 1878. There is also at Windsor a black chalk drawing of folded hands (marked with the old No. 212; No. LXI of the Grosvenor Gallery Publication) which I believe to be a copy of the hands of St. John, made by an unknown pupil. Reproducing the excellent drawings of the Apostles' heads owned by Her Royal Highness the Grand Duchess of Weimar would have been outside the scope of this work; regarding them, I must limit myself to pointing out that the difference in style prevents us from placing the Weimar drawings in the same category as those reproduced here. The way the figures are grouped in the Weimar drawings is enough to indicate that they were not made before the picture was painted, but rather afterward; it is, on the face of it, unbelievable that such a great master would have copied from his own work in this manner.

The drawing of Christ’s head in the Brera palace at Milan was perhaps originally the work of Leonardo’s own hand; unfortunately, it has been entirely retouched and redrawn, so that no firm opinion can be formed regarding its authenticity.

The red chalk drawing reproduced on Plate XLVI is in the Accademia at Venice; it was probably made before the text in Nos. 664 and 665 was written.

The two pen and ink sketches on Plate XLV seem to belong to an even earlier date; the more finished drawing of the two, on the right side, represents Christ with only St. John and Judas and a third disciple whose movement is exactly that described in No. 666, line 4. It is hardly necessary to note that the other sketches on this page and the lines of text below the circle (which contains the solution to a geometry problem) have no connection to the painting of the Last Supper. This figure of Christ may be compared to a similar pen and ink drawing reproduced on page 297 below on the left; the original is in the Louvre. On this page as well, the rest of the sketches have no direct connection to the composition of the Last Supper—not even, it seems to me, the group of four men at the bottom right who are listening to a fifth man addressing them in their midst. Furthermore, the writing on this page (an explanation of a disk-shaped instrument) is certainly not in the style that Leonardo used consistently after the year 1489.

It may be noted in passing that no sketches are known for the portrait of the “Mona Lisa,” nor do the manuscript notes ever mention it, even though according to Vasari the master worked on it for a full four years.

Leonardo’s cartoon for the painting of the battle of Anghiari has shared the fate of its rival work, Michael Angelo’s “Bathers summoned to Battle”: both have been lost in a completely inexplicable way. I cannot go into the remarkable history of this work here; I can only provide an account of what has been preserved of Leonardo’s plan and preparations for its execution. The amount of material in studies and drawings was entirely unknown until now. Their publication here may provide a proper sense of the grandeur of this famous work. The text provided as No. 669 contains a description of the details of the battle, but for the reasons given in the note to that text, I must give up the idea of using this passage as the basis for my attempt to reconstruct the picture as the artist conceived and executed it.

--- Page 464 ---
I may here remind the reader that Leonardo prepared the cartoon in the Sala del Papa of Santa Maria Novella at Florence and worked there from the end of October 1503 until February 1504, and then was occupied with the painting in the Sala del Consiglio in the Palazzo della Signoria until the work was interrupted at the end of May 1506. (See Milanesi's note to Vasari, pp. 43–45, Vol. IV, 1880 edition.) Vasari, as is well known, describes only one scene or episode of the cartoon—the Battle for the Standard in the foreground of the composition, as it would seem; and this alone was ever finished as a mural decoration in the Sala del Consiglio. This portion of the composition is familiar to everyone from the distorted copy engraved by Edelinck. Mariette had already very perceptively observed that Edelinck must surely have worked from a Flemish copy of the picture. There is in the Louvre a drawing by Rubens (No. 565) which also represents four horsemen fighting around a standard and which matches Edelinck's engraving, though the engraving reverses the drawing. An earlier Flemish drawing, such as may have served as the model for both Rubens and Edelinck, is in the Uffizi collection (see Philpots's Photograph, No. 732). It seems to be a work from the second half of the 16th century, a time when both the picture and the cartoon had already been destroyed. It is apparently the production of a not very skilled hand. Raphael Trichet du Fresne, in 1651, mentions that a small picture by Leonardo himself of the Battle of the Standard was then existing in the Tuileries; by this he probably means the painting on panel which is now in the possession of Madame Timbal in Paris, and which has lately been engraved by Haussoullier as a work by Leonardo. The picture, which is very carefully painted, seems to me, however, to be the work of some unknown Florentine painter, and was probably executed within the first ten years of the 16th century. At the same time, it would seem to be a copy, not from Leonardo's cartoon, but from his painting in the Palazzo della Signoria; at any rate, this little picture and the small Flemish drawing in Florence are the oldest finished copies of this episode in the great composition of the Battle of Anghiari.

In his Life of Raphael, Vasari tells us that Raphael copied certain works of Leonardo's during his stay in Florence. Raphael's first visit to Florence lasted from the middle of October 1504 until July 1505, and he revisited it in the summer of 1506. The hasty sketch, now in the possession of the University of Oxford and reproduced on page 337, also represents the Battle of the Standard and seems to have been made during his first stay, and therefore not from the fresco but from the cartoon; for, on the same sheet, we also find, besides an old man's head drawn in Leonardo's style, some studies for the figure of St. John the Martyr which Raphael used in 1505 in his great fresco in the Church of San Severo at Perugia.

Of Leonardo's studies for the Battle of Anghiari, I must first point to five, on three of which—Plate LII 2, Plate LIII, Plate LVI—we find studies for the episode of the Standard. The standard-bearer, who in the above-mentioned copies is seen stooping and holding onto the staff across his shoulder, is immediately recognizable as the left-hand figure in Raphael's sketch, and we find him in a similar posture in Leonardo's pen and ink drawing in the British Museum—Plate LII, 2—the lower figure to the right. It is not difficult to identify the same figure in two more complicated groups in the pen and ink drawings now in the Accademia in Venice—Plate LIII and Plate LIV—where we also find some studies of foot soldiers fighting. On the sheet in the British Museum—Plate LII, 2—we find, among others, one group of three horses galloping forward: one

A horseman is thrown and protects himself with his shield against the lance thrusts of two others on horseback, who try to pierce him as they ride past. The same action is repeated, with some variation, in two pen-and-ink sketches on a third sheet in the Accademia at Venice, Plate LV; a coincidence which suggests the probability of such an incident having actually been represented on the cartoon. We are not, it is true, in a position to declare with any certainty which of these three dissimilar sketches may have been the closest to the group finally adopted in executing the cartoon.

With regard, however, to one of the groups of horsemen, it is possible to determine with perfect certainty not only which arrangement was preferred, but the position it occupied in the composition. The group of horsemen on Plate LVII is a drawing in black chalk at Windsor, which is there attributed to Leonardo, but which appears to me to be the work of Cesare da Sesto, and the Commendatore Giov. Morelli supports me in this view. It can hardly be doubted that da Sesto, as a pupil of Leonardo's, made this drawing from his master's cartoon, if we compare it with the copy made by Raphael—reproduced here.

--- Page 478 ---
For just above the fighting horseman in Raphael's copy, it is possible to detect a horse seen from behind, moving at a slower pace with its tail flying out to the right; the same horse may be seen in the exact same position, carrying a dimly sketched rider, in the foreground of Cesare da Sesto's drawing.

If a very worn black chalk drawing at Windsor—Plate LVI—is, as it appears to be, the reversed impression of an original drawing, it is not difficult to use it to complete the sections drawn by Cesare da Sesto. Indeed, it may prove possible to reconstruct the entirety of the lost cartoon from the mass of materials we now have available, which we may regard as the core of the composition. A large pen and ink...

663. I. I began . . Virgin.

663. Photographs of this page have been published by **BRAUN**, No. 439, and **PHILPOT**, No. 718.

1. *Incominciai*. We have no other information regarding the two pictures of the Madonna mentioned here. Since Leonardo tells us here that he had begun two Madonnas at the same time, the word ‘*incominciai*’ may be understood to mean that he had started preparatory studies for two pictures to be painted later. If this is the case, the non-existence of the pictures may be explained by assuming that they were only planned and never completed. I may mention here a few studies for Madonna pictures which likely belong to this early period; specifically a silver-point drawing on bluish tinted paper at Windsor—see Pl. XL, No. 3—a drawing in which the details have almost vanished in the original but have been made quite clear in the reproduction; secondly, a slight pen and ink sketch in the **Codex VALLARDI**, in the Louvre, fol. 64, No. 2316; and again a silver-point drawing of a Virgin and Child, reworked with a pen, in the **His de la Salle** collection also in the Louvre, No. 101. (See Vicomte **BOTH DE TAUZIA**, *Notes on the drawings of the His de la Salle collection, exhibited at the Louvre.* Paris 1881, pp. 80, 81.) This drawing is, admittedly, traditionally attributed to Raphael, but the author of the catalogue very correctly points out its great resemblance to the sketches for Madonnas in the British Museum which are indisputably Leonardo's. Some of these have been published by Mr. **HENRY WALLIS** in the Art Journal, New Ser. No. 14, Feb. 1882. If the non-existence of the two pictures mentioned here justifies my hypothesis that the text refers only to studies for such pictures, it may also be assumed that the drawings were made for a colleague in **VERROCCHIO'S** workshop. (See **VASARI**, Sansoni's ed. Florence 1880. Vol. IV, p. 564):

> “And because Lorenzo liked Leonardo's style beyond measure, he knew how to imitate it so well that no one imitated him more than he did in the cleanliness and diligent finishing of works.”

Leonardo's notes provide me with no opportunity to discuss the pictures he painted in Florence before he moved to Milan. Therefore, the studies for the unfinished picture of the **Adoration of the Magi**—in the Uffizi, Florence—cannot be described here, nor would any discussion about the picture in the Louvre, “**La Vierge aux Rochers**,” be appropriate given the lack of any reference to it in the manuscripts. Consequently, when I shortly add a few remarks on this painting to explain the Master's drawings for it, it will not merely be to assist critical research regarding the picture now in the National Gallery, London—which some critics have declared to be a replica of the Louvre picture—but also because I am taking this opportunity to publish several of the Master's finished studies. Even if these were not made in Florence but later in Milan, they must have preceded the painting of the Last Supper. The original picture in Paris is currently so obscured by dust and varnish that current photographic reproductions actually show more evidence of the damage the picture has suffered than of the original work itself. The woodcut provided on p. 344 is only intended to give a general idea of the composition. It must be understood that the outline

--- Page 486 ---
and the expression of the heads, which is obscured but not destroyed in the picture, is completely missed here. The facsimiles that follow are from drawings which appear to me to be studies for "*La Vierge aux Rochers.*"

1. A silver point drawing on brown-toned paper of a woman's head looking to the left. In the Royal Library at Turin, apparently a study from nature for the Angel's head (Pl. XLII).

2. A study of drapery for the left leg of the same figure, done with a brush using Indian ink on greenish paper, with the highlights heightened with white.
