The original is at Windsor, No. 223. The reproduction in Plate XLIII is flawed in the shadow on the upper part of the thigh, which is not as deep as in the original; it should also be noted that the folds of the drapery near the hips are somewhat changed in the finished work in the Louvre, while the London copy shows a closer resemblance to this study in that specific detail.

3. A study in red chalk for the bust of the Infant Christ—No. 3 in the Windsor collection (Plate XLIV). The well-known silver-point drawing on pale green paper in the Louvre of a boy's head (No. 363 in Reiset, *Notice des dessins, Ecoles d' Italie*) seems to me to be a slightly modified copy, either from the original painting or from this red chalk study.

4. A silver-point study on greenish paper for the head of John the Baptist, reproduced on page 342. This was formerly in the Codex Vallardi and is now displayed among the drawings in the Louvre. In the original, the highlights are enhanced with white; the outlines, particularly around the head and ear, are visibly restored.

There is a study of an outstretched hand—No. 288 in the Windsor collection—which was published in the *Grosvenor Gallery Publication*, 1878, simply under the title: “No. 72 Study of a hand, pointing.” I, on the other hand, regard this as a copy by a pupil. The action appears in the kneeling angel of the Paris painting and not in the London copy.

These four genuine studies form, I believe, a valuable substitute in the absence of any manuscript notes referring to the celebrated Paris painting.

**664.** These eleven lines of text are beside the pen and ink drawing of a hanged man—Plate LXII, No. 1. This drawing was exhibited in 1879 at the *Ecole des Beaux-Arts* in Paris, and the compilers of the catalogue amused themselves by giving the victim's name as follows: “*A hanged man, dressed in a long robe, hands tied behind his back . . . Bernardo di Bandino Barontigni, a trousers merchant*.”

(See *Catalogue descriptif des Dessins de Maîtres anciens exposés à l'Ecole des Beaux Arts, Paris 1879*; No. 83, pages 9—10). Now, the criminal represented here is none other than Bernardo di Bandino Baroncelli, the murderer of Giuliano de' Medici, whose name as a collaborator in the Pazzi conspiracy gained a tragic notoriety through the events of April 26, 1478. Bernardo was descended from an ancient family and was the son of the man who, under King Ferrante, was President of the High Court of Justice in Naples. It appears his ruined finances induced him to join the Pazzi; he and Francesco Pazzi were entrusted with the task of murdering Giuliano de' Medici on the appointed day. When their victim did not appear in the cathedral at the hour they expected him, the two conspirators ran to the Medici palace and persuaded him to accompany them. Giuliano then took his place in the chancel of the Cathedral, and as the officiating priest raised the Host—the agreed-upon signal—Bernardo stabbed the unsuspecting Giuliano in the chest with a short sword; Giuliano stepped backward and fell dead. However, the attempt on Lorenzo's life by the other conspirators at the same moment failed. As soon as Bernardo saw Lorenzo trying to escape toward the sacristy, he rushed at him and struck down Francesco Nori, who attempted to protect Lorenzo. This is not the place to tell how Lorenzo then took refuge behind the bronze doors of the sacristy, nor how, as soon as Giuliano's death became known, the conspirators' further plans were defeated while a terrible vengeance overtook all the perpetrators and accomplices. Bernardo Bandini alone seemed favored by fortune; he hid first in the tower of the Cathedral and then escaped from Florence undetected. Poliziano, who was with Lorenzo in the Cathedral, says in his ‘*Commentary on the Pazzi Conspiracy*’: “*Bandini, fleeing, encountered a man from Tiferno, by whom he was helped into the ranks and reached Siena.*” And Gino Capponi, in summarizing the reports of the—

**667.** Since this note is in the same small manuscript as the preceding passage, I may be justified in assuming that Leonardo intended to use the features of the person named here as a suitable model for the figure of Christ. The famous drawing of the head of Christ, now hanging in the Brera Gallery in Milan, has obviously been so heavily restored that it is now impossible to say whether it was ever authentic. We only have to compare it with the undoubtedly authentic drawings of the disciples' heads in Plates XLVII, XLVIII, and L to admit that not a single line of the Milan drawing in its current state can be by the same hand.

| Begin with the speech of Niccolò Piccinino to the soldiers and the exiled Florentines, among whom are Messer Rinaldo degli Albizzi and other Florentines. Then let it be shown how he first mounted his horse in armor; and the entire army followed him—40 squadrons of cavalry and 2000 infantry went with him. Very early in the morning, the Patriarch went up a hill to scout the countryside, namely the hills, fields, and the valley watered by a river; and from there he saw Niccolò Piccinino coming from Borgo San Sepolcro with his men and a great cloud of dust; and having spotted them, he returned to the camp of his own people and spoke to them. Having spoken, he prayed to God with joined hands, and a cloud appeared from which Saint Peter emerged and spoke to the Patriarch. 500 cavalry were sent by the Patriarch to hinder or restrain the enemy's momentum. In the first rank, Francesco, the son of Niccolò Piccinino, was the first to attack the bridge, which was guarded by the Patriarch and the Florentines. Beyond the bridge, on the left hand, he sent infantry to block our men, who were resisting; their leader was Micheletto, who on that day by lot had the guard of the army. Here, at this bridge, a battle is fought. | Begin with the address of Niccolò Piccinino to the soldiers and the banished Florentines among whom are Messer Rinaldo degli Albizzi and other Florentines. Then let it be shown how he first mounted on horseback in armor; and the whole army came after him—40 squadrons of cavalry, and 2000 foot soldiers went with him. Very early in the morning the Patriarch went up a hill to reconnoitre the country, that is the hills, fields, and the valley watered by a river; and from there he beheld Niccolò Piccinino coming from Borgo San Sepolcro with his people, and with a great dust; and perceiving them he returned to the camp of his own people and addressed them. Having spoken he prayed to God with clasped hands, when there appeared a cloud in which Saint Peter appeared and spoke to the Patriarch. 500 cavalry were sent forward by the Patriarch to hinder or check the rush of the enemy. In the foremost troop Francesco the son of Niccolò Piccinino was the first to attack the bridge which was held by the Patriarch and the Florentines. Beyond the bridge to his left he sent forward some infantry to engage ours, who drove them back, among |

---

**669.** This passage does not seem to me to be in Leonardo's hand, though it has until now been generally accepted as genuine. Not only is the writing unlike his, but the spelling is also quite different. I would suggest that this passage is a description of the events of the battle drawn up for the Painter by order of the Signoria, perhaps by some historian commissioned by them, to serve as a scheme or program of the work. The entire tone of the style seems to me to argue in favor of this theory; and besides, it would be in no way surprising that such a document should have been preserved among Leonardo's autographs.

*Niccolò da Pisa*, compare line 44.
*Conte Francesco*, see line 24.
*Micheletto Sforza Attendolo*, see line 29. He and *Pier Giampaolo Orsino* were Captains together of the Lombard troops.

**2-7.** Neri di Gino Capponi and Bernardetto de' Medici were the Commissaries of the Florentine Republic.

**13.** *The Patriarch*, Cardinal Scarampi, Patriarch of Aquileia, Legate of the Papal see.

**16.** *Borgo San Sepolcro*. On a map drawn by Leonardo and reproduced on Plate CXIII, *Borgo a San Sepolcro* is to be read near the upper margin to the left between a view of a town and a sketch of the windings of the Tiber. Below, somewhat further to the left, is the word 'Anghiari,' the name of the village where the battle was fought.

**20.** *Saint Peter emerged*. The battle took place on St. Peter and St. Paul's day, June 29th, 1440.

A great battle; our men won and the enemy was driven away. Here Guido and Astorre his brother, the Lord of Faenza, re-formed with many troops and renewed the war; they struck the Florentine forces so hard that they recovered the bridge and reached as far as the pavilions. Simonetto came against them with 600 horsemen to strike the enemy, and he drove them from the place once more, and they recaptured the bridge. Behind him came other troops with 2000 horsemen, and so they fought for a long time with varying results. Afterward, the Patriarch, to throw the enemy into disorder, sent Niccolò da Pisa and Napoleone Orsino—a beardless youth—forward, and behind them a great multitude of people; and here another great feat of arms was performed. During this time, Niccolò Piccinino pushed forward the rest of his troops, who once again made our men give way; and if the Patriarch had not placed himself at the front and restrained those captains with his words and actions, our men would have fled. The Patriarch had certain artillery pieces positioned on the hill, with which he scattered the enemy infantry; and this disorder was so great that Niccolò began to recall his son and all his troops, and they fled toward the town. A great slaughter of men took place here, and none were saved except the first who fled or those who hid themselves. The feat of arms lasted until sunset. The Patriarch then attended to withdrawing the troops and burying the dead, and afterward he created a trophy from the spoils.

But then the Patriarch, in order to throw the enemy into confusion, sent forward Niccolò da Pisa and Napoleone Orsino, a beardless lad, followed by a great multitude of men, and then another great feat of arms was performed. At the same time Niccolò Piccinino urged forward the remnant of his men, who once more made ours give way; and if it had not been that the Patriarch set himself at their head and, by his words and deeds, controlled the captains, our soldiers would have taken to flight. The Patriarch had some artillery placed on the hill and with these he dispersed the enemy’s infantry; and the disorder was so complete that Niccolò began to call back his son and all his men, and they took to flight towards the town. And then began a great slaughter of men; none escaped but the first of those who had fled or who hid themselves. The battle continued until sunset, when the Patriarch turned his attention to recalling his men and burying the dead, and afterwards a trophy was erected.

### 670.

The ermine with blood; Galeazzo between calm weather and a representation of fortune.

Ermine with blood; Galeazzo, between calm weather and a representation of a tempest.

---

670. Only the beginning of this text is legible; the writing is much faded and the meaning is consequently obscure. It seems to refer, like the following passage, to an allegorical picture.

2. *Galeazzo* probably here means Gian Galeazzo Maria Sforza, whose tragic death took place in the year when this manuscript was written. His father Galeazzo Maria—who succeeded Francesco, the founder of the Sforza family—had been murdered in 1476, and his mother, Bona of Savoy, held the regency during the youth's minority; he was imprisoned in 1494 by his uncle Lodovico il Moro and probably poisoned by his orders.

ALLEGORICAL REPRESENTATIONS.

677.

...the same body, because they have the same foundation, inasmuch as the foundation of pleasure is labor and pain, and the foundation of pain is various and lustful pleasures. And therefore it is represented here with a reed in the right hand, which is hollow and without strength, and the wounds made with it are poisonous. In Tuscany, reeds are used as supports for beds, to signify that it is there that vain dreams occur and there a great part of life is consumed; there much useful time is thrown away—namely, that of the morning, when the mind is sober and rested, and the body is thus fit to take on new labors. Furthermore, many vain pleasures are taken there, both by the mind imagining things impossible to itself, and by the body taking those pleasures that are often the cause of the failing of life; so for this reason, the reed is held as such a foundation.
Evil-thinking is either envy or ingratitude.

---

### 677.

This Envy is represented with a contemptuous gesture toward heaven because, if she could, she would use her strength against God. She is made with a mask on her face of fair appearance. She is shown wounded in her sight by palm and olive branches; her ear is wounded by laurel and myrtle, to signify that victory and truth offend her. Many lightning bolts are made to issue from her, to signify her evil speech. She is made lean and haggard because she is always in continual torment. Her heart is shown gnawed by a swelling serpent. She is given a quiver, and the arrows are tongues, because she often offends with them. She is given a leopard's skin, because that animal kills the lion through envy and by deceit. She is given a vase in her hand full of flowers, and let it be full of scorpions, toads, and other poisons. She is made to ride upon Death, because Envy, never dying, never ceases to rule. She is made with a bridle and loaded with various kinds of weapons, because all her thoughts are directed toward the destruction of others.

680. Lines 1 and 2 are written from left to right and upside down. 1. a head .. young. 4. head. 5. Saint Jeromes. 6. measurements .. figure. 8. duke. 11. Girolamo da Fegline. 12. of Christ done. 13. Sebastians. 14. compositions. 15. chalcedony. 16. head .. with .. hair. 17. pitchers in perspective. 19. of water. 20. head .. of Atalanta. 21. head of Geronimo da Fegline. 22. head of Gian Francesco. 23. throats. 24. many heads. 25. complete. 26. positions. 27. our lady. 28. almost in profile. 29. head of our lady .. gospel. 30. head .. with the long chin. 31. head of a gypsy woman. 32. head.

---

9. Many designs for knots. VASARI in his life of Leonardo (IV, 21, ed. MILANESI 1880) says:
> "Furthermore, he wasted time even drawing groups of cords arranged in order, so that from one end the rest followed all the way to the other, until they filled a circle; one very difficult and beautiful example can be seen in a print, and in the middle are these words: *Leonardus Vinci Accademia*."

*Gruppi* must here be understood as a technical term for those twisted ornaments which are well known through woodcuts. AMORETTI mentions six different ones in the Ambrosian Library. I am indebted to M. DELABORDE for kindly informing me that the original blocks of these are preserved in his department in the National Library in Paris. On the cover of these volumes is a copy of one of them. The size of the original is 23 1/2 centimeters by 26 1/4. The center portion of another is given on p. 361. G. GOVI remarks on these ornaments (*Saggio* p. 22):
> "These knots were probably intended to serve as models for bookbinders' tools to decorate the portfolios of students (?). Indeed, we find borders very similar to these stamped in gold on the cardboard covers of various contemporary volumes, and we also see them appearing in the initial letters of some editions of the time."

Dürer, who copied them while omitting the inscription, added his own monogram to the second impressions. In his diary, he designates them simply as "The six knots" (see THAUSING, *Life of A. Dürer* I, 362, 363). In Leonardo's manuscripts, we find here and there little sketches or suggestions for similar ornaments. Compare also G. MONGERI, *L'Arte in Milano*, p. 315, where an ornament of the same character is shown from the old decorations of the vaulted ceiling of the Sacristy of Santa Maria delle Grazie.

680. 17. The meaning in which the word *coppi*, literally pitchers, is used here I am unable to determine; but a change to *copie* (copies) seems to me too doubtful to be risked.

Why the eye does not see clearly when the eye observes a motion which is faster than its power of perception.

The motion of the water.

Why the movements [of] sight are imperfect.

Note how.

--- Page 524 ---
Observe the movement of the surface of the water, and how it resembles that of hair, which has two motions: one depends on the weight of the strands, and the other on the direction of its curls. Thus, the water has its swirling eddies, one part of which follows the force of its primary current, while the other follows the movement of its incidence and reflection.

--- Page 525 ---
### TRUTH.

Fire destroys all sophistry, that is, deceit; and maintains truth alone, that is, gold. Truth at last cannot be hidden. Dissimulation is of no avail. Dissimulation is to no purpose before so great a judge. Falsehood puts on a mask. Nothing is hidden under the sun. Fire is used to represent truth because it destroys all sophistry and lies; and the mask represents lying and falsehood, which conceal the truth.

---

### 685.

Movement will cease before we are weary of being useful. Movement will fail sooner than usefulness. Death sooner than weariness. I am never weary of being useful; this is a motto for carnival. In serving others I cannot do enough. Without fatigue. No labor is sufficient to tire me. Hands into which ducats and precious stones fall like snow; they never become tired by serving, but this service is only for its own utility and not for our own benefit. I am never weary of being useful. Naturally, nature has so disposed me.

---

This shall be placed in the hand of Ingratitude. Wood nourishes the fire that consumes it.

703—705.] ORNAMENTS AND DECORATIONS.

### 703.

Ornaments and Decorations for feasts (703-705).

A bird for a play.

### 704.

**A CARNIVAL COSTUME.**

To make a beautiful costume, cut thin cloth and apply a fragrant varnish made of turpentine oil and seed-lac varnish. Use a perforated stencil, which must be wetted so that it does not stick to the cloth. This stencil should be designed with knotwork patterns, which can then be filled in with black millet, while the background is filled with white millet [7].

### 705.

Snow taken from the high mountain peaks could be transported to hot locations and allowed to fall in public squares during summer festivals.

---

**703.** Biographies mention a great deal, yet the author’s own notes say very little, about the invention attributed to Leonardo for making artificial birds fly through the air; therefore, the text provided here is of exceptional interest as it is accompanied by a sketch. It is a very simple drawing of a bird with spread wings, which appears to be sliding down a taut string. Leonardo’s flying machines and his studies on the flight of birds will be discussed later.

**704.** Ser Giuliano, the painter Leonardo’s brother, had been commissioned along with others to order and produce the costumes for the allegorical figures for the 1515–16 Carnival in Florence. Vasari, however, is mistaken when he says of the 1513 Florentine Carnival: “and those who designed and arranged the costumes of the figures were Ser Piero da Vinci, Leonardo’s father, and Bernardino di Giordano, men of great talent.” (See Milanesi’s edition, Vol. VI, p. 251.)

**7.** The grains of black and white millet would stick to the varnish and resemble embroidery.
